Extensive experiments on Ancient-Bench covering general Vision-Language Models (VLMs) and OCR-specialist models reveal that ancient Chinese artifact text recognition remains fundamentally unsolved, with persistent challenges in variant characters, specialized symbols, and hallucination. The dataset is available at https://github.com/SCUT-DLVCLab/Ancient_Bench.

</details>


### [74] [Temporal Sensitivity Analysis of Tessera Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27175)
*Julia Guerrero-Viu,Alex López-Cifuentes,Ignacio Pérez-Villar,Fabio Pacifici*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a controlled study on how the temporal length of satellite observations affects the performance of Tessera, a leading Earth Observation foundation model, for land-use/land-cover mapping. The study finds that embedding performance is task-dependent: phenology-based classes benefit significantly from longer temporal windows, while temporally stable classes require less temporal data. The work demonstrates that temporal coverage is a tunable cost rather than a fixed prerequisite, enabling potential applications like near-real-time mapping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many Earth Observation applications require land-use/land-cover maps that are both precise and frequently updated. However, existing foundation models like Tessera build embeddings from a full year of observations, which limits their utility for timely mapping. This study investigates whether shorter observation windows can still provide useful embeddings, potentially enabling faster update cycles.

Method: The researchers kept the Tessera encoder frozen and computed embeddings over varying observation windows (from one year down to single day). These embeddings were used as inputs to a linear probe and a UNet segmentation head. Performance was benchmarked against from-scratch networks on three datasets: LUCAS, DynamicEarthNet, and PASTIS-R.

Result: The study reveals that embedding value is task-dependent. For classes separated by phenology (e.g., crop types in PASTIS-R), embeddings achieved 58.3% mean Intersection-over-Union, approximately 46% better than the best from-scratch model. For temporally stable classes (e.g., forests), embedding-based and from-scratch models matched only under full supervision. Embeddings remained more label-efficient across datasets. Degradation under shorter windows was gradual and class-dependent: reducing from one year to one month cost 39% accuracy on PASTIS-R but only 5% on DynamicEarthNet. Single-day embeddings still classified land cover in LUCAS at 3.4 times the chance level.

Conclusion: Temporal coverage in Earth Observation foundation models is a tunable cost rather than a fixed prerequisite. This finding opens possibilities for near-real-time mapping applications and faster land-use/land-cover refresh cycles. The study demonstrates that practitioners can balance temporal coverage against accuracy needs depending on their specific use case.

Abstract: Many Earth Observation applications need land-use/land-cover maps that are both precise and frequently updated, yet the strongest Earth Observation foundation models build their embeddings from a full year of observations. We present a controlled study of the temporal sensitivity of Tessera, one of these leading foundation models, for land-use/land-cover mapping. Keeping the encoder frozen, we recompute its embeddings over varying observation windows, from a full year down to a single day. We use them as inputs to a linear probe and a UNet segmentation head, benchmarking both of them against from-scratch networks on LUCAS, DynamicEarthNet, and PASTIS-R datasets. We show that the value of the embeddings is task-dependent. Where classes are separated by phenology, as for the crop types of PASTIS-R, they reach a mean Intersection-over-Union of $58.3$, about $46\%$ above the best from-scratch model. Where classes are temporally stable (e.g., forests in DynamicEarthNet and LUCAS), embedding-based and from-scratch models match only under full supervision. On both datasets, Tessera embeddings remain markedly more label-efficient. Degradation under shorter temporal windows is gradual and class-dependent. Contracting the window from one year to one month costs $39\%$ of the segmentation accuracy on PASTIS-R but only $5\%$ on DynamicEarthNet. Single-day embeddings still classify land cover in LUCAS at $3.4$ times the chance level. Our study shows that temporal coverage is therefore a tunable cost rather than a fixed prerequisite, opening regimes such as near-real-time mapping and faster land-use/land-cover refresh cycles.

</details>


### [75] [Parameter-Efficient pretrained-CT-to-MRI Transfer for Rectal Cancer Segmentation: Performance-Calibration Trade-offs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27178)
*Aneesh Rangnekar,Jorge Tapias Gomez,Joseph O Deasy,Harini Veeraraghavan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SWIFT is a Swin V2-based model for rectal cancer segmentation from T2-weighted MRI, featuring parameter-efficient and tumor-aware fine-tuning. The study evaluates four configurations on 247 test cases, demonstrating SWIFTe reduces parameters by 70.1% while improving tumor detection (93.9% vs 89.9%), with the ensemble (SWIFTe-LDE4) achieving best calibration after temperature scaling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate rectal cancer segmentation from MRI is essential for adaptive radiotherapy and tumor response assessment, but clinical deployment requires computational efficiency and informative, calibrated uncertainty estimates.

Method: A Swin V2 encoder pretrained on 10,444 public 3D CT volumes using DINOv2-style objective was adapted to T2-weighted MRI through four cumulative configurations: (1) SWIFT: full fine-tuning, (2) SWIFTe: decoder compression, (3) SWIFTe-LoRA: low-rank adaptation, (4) SWIFTe-LDE4: four-member LoRA-decoder ensemble. Evaluated on 247-case test set (1.5/3 Tesla GE scanners) for geometric accuracy, tumor detection, radiomic agreement, and probability calibration.

Result: SWIFTe reduced parameters by 70.1% (72.8M to 21.8M) and increased tumor detection from 89.9% to 93.9%, while achieving slightly lower median surface DSC (0.61 vs 0.62) and improved radiomic agreement. Tumor-aware augmentation ablation showed detection-boundary-agreement trade-off (93.9%→89.9% detection, 0.61→0.64 surface DSC). SWIFTe-LoRA used 14.6% of trainable parameters with similar performance. SWIFTe-LDE4 achieved lowest calibration errors (ECE: 0.217, Brier: 0.222) after temperature scaling.

Conclusion: Parameter-efficient configurations (SWIFTe, SWIFTe-LoRA) maintain strong segmentation performance with reduced computational cost. The ensemble (SWIFTe-LDE4) provides best uncertainty calibration, though residual miscalibration remains. Results are robust across different pretrained initializations (SWIFT vs VoCo).

Abstract: Accurate rectal cancer segmentation from magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) is essential for adaptive radiotherapy and tumor response assessment, but deployment also requires computational efficiency and informative, calibrated uncertainty estimates. We therefore introduce SWIFT, a SWin pretrained model wIth parameter-eFficient and Tumor-aware fine-tuning for rectal cancer segmentation. A Swin V2 encoder pretrained on 10,444 public 3D CT volumes using a DINOv2-style objective was adapted to T2-weighted MRI through four cumulative configurations: full fine-tuning (SWIFT), decoder compression (SWIFTe), low-rank adaptation (SWIFTe-LoRA), and a four-member LoRA-decoder ensemble (SWIFTe-LDE4). Geometric accuracy, tumor detection, radiomic agreement, and probability calibration were evaluated on a held-out 247-case test set from a single-institution cohort acquired using 1.5 or 3 Tesla GE scanners. Compared with SWIFT, SWIFTe reduced total parameters by 70.1% (from 72.8M to 21.8M) and increased tumor detection rate from 89.9% to 93.9%, while achieving a slightly lower median surface DSC (0.61 versus 0.62) and improved radiomic agreement. In a separate SWIFTe ablation, removing tumor-aware augmentation reduced detection from 93.9% to 89.9% but increased surface DSC from 0.61 to 0.64, demonstrating a detection-boundary-agreement trade-off. SWIFTe-LoRA used 14.6% of SWIFTe's trainable parameters while retaining similar segmentation performance. SWIFTe-LDE4 achieved the lowest calibration errors among the four configurations after temperature scaling (expected calibration error, 0.217; Brier score, 0.222), although the absolute expected calibration error indicates residual miscalibration. Similar efficiency-calibration patterns were observed using the public VoCo checkpoint, supporting robustness across pretrained initializations rather than external clinical generalizability.

</details>


### [76] [SSMB: Self-Supervised Local Feature Detection under Motion Blur](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27181)
*Zhenjun Zhao,Fabio Bellavia,Wenting Wang,Fan Zhu,Jiajun Wu,Suryansh Kumar,Mingqiang Wei,Haoang Li,Javier Civera*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SSMB is a novel self-supervised keypoint detector for motion-blurred images that does not require deblurring or external labels. It uses a Local Discriminability Enhancement (LDE) module to restore fine-grained local discriminability after global feature mixing, trained in two stages: geometric pretraining on synthetic shapes and blur-aware training on real sharp-blur image pairs. It achieves state-of-the-art results across keypoint detection, image matching, relative pose estimation, and visual localization tasks under motion blur.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motion blur distorts local image structure and degrades repeatability of feature localization. Existing approaches either rely on computationally expensive deblur-then-detect pipelines that may introduce restoration artifacts, or learn to regress positions of handcrafted keypoints extracted on sharp images, which reflects the assumptions of handcrafted detectors rather than what is truly repeatable under blur.

Method: SSMB introduces the Local Discriminability Enhancement (LDE) module that restores fine-grained local discriminability after global feature mixing. Training is performed in two stages: (1) Geometric pretraining on synthetic shapes bootstraps spatially discriminative keypoint detection without external detectors, using rendered geometry; (2) Blur-aware training on real sharp-blur image pairs learns blur-invariant detection through a multi-component self-supervised objective enforcing cross-domain consistency, geometric alignment, and spatial coverage.

Result: SSMB establishes a new state-of-the-art among sparse keypoint detectors, consistently outperforming both supervised and self-supervised baselines across all tasks including keypoint detection, image matching, relative pose estimation, and visual localization under motion blur.

Conclusion: This work presents a deblur-free, self-supervised approach that successfully learns blur-invariant keypoint detection without relying on handcrafted detectors or external pseudo-labels, demonstrating superior performance across multiple vision tasks under motion blur conditions.

Abstract: Keypoint detection under motion blur remains a significant challenge, as blur distorts local image structure and degrades the repeatability of feature localization. Existing approaches either rely on computationally expensive deblur-then-detect pipelines that may introduce restoration artifacts, or learn to regress the image positions of handcrafted keypoints extracted on sharp images, which reflects the assumptions of the handcrafted detector rather than what is truly repeatable under blur. We present SSMB, a deblur-free, self-supervised keypoint detector for motion-blurred images that requires neither handcrafted detectors nor external pseudo-labels. SSMB introduces the Local Discriminability Enhancement (LDE) module, which restores fine-grained local discriminability after global feature mixing. Training is performed in two stages. First, geometric pretraining on synthetic shapes bootstraps spatially discriminative keypoint detection without any external detector, just from the rendered geometry. Second, blur-aware training on real sharp-blur image pairs learns blur-invariant detection through a multi-component self-supervised objective that enforces cross-domain consistency, geometric alignment, and spatial coverage. Extensive evaluations on keypoint detection, image matching, relative pose estimation, and visual localization under motion blur demonstrate that SSMB establishes a new state-of-the-art among sparse keypoint detectors, consistently outperforming both supervised and self-supervised baselines across all tasks. Code, models, and datasets will be publicly available upon paper acceptance.

</details>


### [77] [Unsupervised Adaptation of 3D CT Foundation Models for 3D CBCT Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27190)
*Gauthier Miralles,Loic Le Folgoc,Vincent Jugnon,Pietro Gori*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A novel unsupervised domain adaptation (UDA) framework for 3D CBCT liver segmentation that uses redundancy-reducing feature alignment to bridge the domain gap between diagnostic CT and interventional CBCT, without requiring target-domain annotations or inference-time adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate 3D CBCT segmentation is critical for interventional and radiation therapy, but is limited by two key challenges: (1) scarcity of annotated CBCT data, and (2) large domain shift from diagnostic CT due to fundamental modality differences in acquisition, physics effects, and contrast-specific vascular content.

Method: Proposes a novel UDA framework based on redundancy-reducing feature alignment that enables architecture-agnostic adaptation for both CNN-based and ViT-based foundation models. The method requires no target-domain annotations or inference-time adaptation.

Result: Evaluated on two challenging CT-CBCT liver segmentation benchmarks (interventional vascular procedures and radiation therapy). The approach consistently outperforms existing pretrained foundation model and UDA strategies, demonstrating that even large-scale pretrained segmentation networks require explicit feature-space bridging to generalize across acquisition modalities.

Conclusion: The proposed UDA framework successfully addresses the cross-modality domain gap in CBCT segmentation. The method is architecture-agnostic and works with both CNN and ViT-based models. The authors release liver segmentations for a public CBCT dataset, along with code, trained models, and weights to support reproducibility and benchmarking.

Abstract: Accurate 3D segmentation of cone-beam CT (CBCT) is critical for interventional and radiation therapy applications, yet it remains limited by two compounding challenges: the scarcity of annotated CBCT data and the large domain shift from diagnostic CT. Interventional CBCT exhibits fundamental modality differences from conventional CT, driven by acquisition and physics effects as well as contrast-specific vascular content, thereby limiting effective cross-modality model transfer. We propose a novel unsupervised domain adaptation (UDA) framework based on redundancy-reducing feature alignment, enabling 3D CBCT segmentation with no target-domain annotations or inference-time adaptation. Our framework is architecture-agnostic, seamlessly adapting both CNN-based and ViT-based foundation models. We evaluate our method on two challenging CT-CBCT liver segmentation benchmarks: one for interventional vascular procedures and one for radiation therapy, demonstrating that even large-scale pretrained segmentation networks require explicit feature-space bridging to generalize across acquisition modalities, and that our approach consistently outperforms existing pretrained foundation model and UDA strategies. To support reproducibility and benchmarking, we release the liver segmentations for a public CBCT dataset, along with the code, trained models, and weights.

</details>


### [78] [Vision-centric generative AI models: A software-hardware perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27199)
*Eleni Tselepi,Cristian Sestito,Shady Agwa,Themis Prodromakis*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A perspective paper arguing that vision generative AI development has been quality-driven rather than hardware-aware, and proposing a software-hardware co-design approach to make generative AI sustainable and accessible across edge devices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current vision generative AI models are designed primarily for large datacenters with abundant resources, creating a gap for edge applications (autonomous vehicles, agricultural sensors, mobile devices) that operate under strict hardware constraints. The authors believe progress has been driven by output quality while hardware evolves reactively.

Method: The authors quantify parameter cost and energy efficiency of vision generative models across various accelerator platforms, and map four generative model families against seven real-world application domains.

Result: The paper provides a framework mapping 4 generative model families against 7 application domains, along with quantified analysis of parameter costs and energy efficiency across different accelerator platforms.

Conclusion: The authors advocate for a software-hardware co-design approach where deployment constraints are considered from the start of design, ensuring the 'right model' runs on the 'right hardware' to serve the 'right application', making generative AI deployment sustainable and accessible across a broader range of platforms.

Abstract: Vision generative artificial intelligence (AI) has emerged as one of the most rapidly advancing areas of deep learning. The explosion of multimodal models has made them widely associated with text-to-image applications running on large datacentres. However, vision generative models are equally needed in applications that operate under strict hardware constraints at the edge, including autonomous vehicles, agricultural sensors, and mobile devices. In this Perspective, we argue that progress in vision generative AI has been driven by output quality, with hardware evolving reactively to accommodate growing model demands. We quantify the parameter cost and energy efficiency of these models across a range of accelerator platforms, and map four generative model families against seven real-world application domains. Finally, we advocate a software-hardware co-design approach, where deployment constraints are considered from the start of the design process, ensuring that the "right model" runs on the "right hardware" to serve the "right application", making generative AI deployment sustainable and accessible across a much broader range of platforms.

</details>


### [79] [PACE: A Unified Condense-and-Extract Paradigm for Fast VLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27206)
*Junjie Liu,Shengyuan Ye,Xu Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) demonstrate exceptional visual reasoning capabilities, yet their inference costs escalate rapidly with the proliferation of visual tokens. Existing visual token pruning methods exhibit two fundamental limitations. First, most approaches operate exclusively post-vision encoder, leaving the substantial latency of the visual encoding phase unoptimized. Second, under strict token budgets, these methods often fail to jointly preserve holistic visual contexts and fine-grained details, leading to performance degradation. To address these bottlenecks, we propose PACE (Pixel-Adaptive Condense and Extract), a training-free inference framework that accelerates both the vision encoder and the Large Language Model (LLM) via a unified Condense-and-Extract paradigm. During the Condense stage, an Adaptive Pixel Compressor (APC) evaluates visual information density prior to encoding, adaptively downsampling redundant inputs, curtailing encoder computation while preserving global context and essential visual cues. In the Extract stage, a Dynamic Dual-Attention Extractor (DDAE) selectively retains visual tokens via a fusion of internal visual signals from the encoder and semantic signals from the LLM, safeguarding task-critical details. By integrating PACE into Qwen2.5-VL-7B, the model retains 93.8% of its original performance while utilizing only 10% of the visual tokens, yielding a 3.1x speedup in time to first token (TTFT). Our code is available at https://github.com/jjL357/PACE.

</details>


### [80] [CODE: Cross-Modal Calibration and Dynamic Suppression for Open World Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27214)
*Hao Xu,Zhaoning Shi,Hehe Jin,Bo Ma*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CODE is an inference-time framework for Open World Object Detection that addresses semantic ambiguity in multimodal models through three components: Cross-Modal Joint Confidence Calibration, Uncertainty-Guided Universal Objectness Enhancement, and Dynamic Outlier Suppression via Confidence Margin. It achieves 21.7 U-mAP and 40.8 K-mAP on Real-World Detection benchmark with OWL-ViT L/14, improving SOTA by 2.6 and 2.3 points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open World Object Detection (OWOD) built on multimodal foundation models suffers from two key issues: (1) semantic ambiguity caused by unidirectional text-to-vision matching, and (2) rigid outlier penalties that over-suppress unknown objects near known-class decision boundaries. These limitations hinder the detection of unknown objects in real-world scenarios.

Method: CODE proposes three complementary components: (1) Cross-Modal Joint Confidence Calibration injects global visual prototypes to calibrate text-driven known-class predictions; (2) Uncertainty-Guided Universal Objectness Enhancement measures classification hesitation from local visual responses to strengthen potential unknown objects; (3) Dynamic Outlier Suppression via Confidence Margin replaces rigid suppression with a margin-aware adjustment that preserves ambiguous out-of-distribution instances.

Result: Using OWL-ViT L/14 backbone, CODE achieves 21.7 U-mAP (unknown class detection) and 40.8 K-mAP (known class detection) in Task 1 of the Real-World Detection benchmark, surpassing previous state-of-the-art by 2.6 and 2.3 points respectively.

Conclusion: CODE successfully addresses semantic ambiguity and over-suppression issues in OWOD through cross-modal calibration and dynamic suppression mechanisms, achieving new state-of-the-art performance on the Real-World Detection benchmark while better preserving ambiguous unknown objects.

Abstract: Open World Object Detection (OWOD) built on multimodal foundation models often suffers from semantic ambiguity caused by unidirectional text-to-vision matching, while rigid outlier penalties may over-suppress unknown objects near known-class decision boundaries. We propose CODE (Cross-Modal Calibration and Dynamic Suppression), a unified inference-time framework with three complementary components. Cross-Modal Joint Confidence Calibration injects global visual prototypes to calibrate text-driven known-class predictions. Uncertainty-Guided Universal Objectness Enhancement measures classification hesitation from local visual responses to strengthen potential unknown objects. Dynamic Outlier Suppression via Confidence Margin replaces rigid suppression with a margin-aware adjustment that preserves ambiguous out-of-distribution instances. Experiments on the Real-World Detection benchmark demonstrate that, with the OWL-ViT L/14 backbone, CODE achieves 21.7 U-mAP and 40.8 K-mAP in Task 1, surpassing the previous state of the art by 2.6 and 2.3 points, respectively.

</details>


### [81] [DINOcular: Self-Supervised Visuospatial Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27226)
*Farkhat Almukhamedov,Sami Azirar,Hermann Blum*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A self-supervised framework for learning joint visuospatial representations from RGB-D observations that integrates depth-derived geometric priors with a visual backbone through inter-patch and intra-patch fusion, improving 3D geometry understanding while maintaining semantic capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision foundation models are trained almost exclusively on RGB images, but embodied systems have access to explicit depth sensing which provides geometric information that monocular inputs cannot recover. There is a need to leverage this depth information alongside visual features.

Method: Self-supervised framework integrating depth-derived geometric priors with a visual backbone through inter-patch and intra-patch fusion mechanisms, enabling the model to encode both appearance and spatial structure efficiently.

Result: The method outperforms prior methods of comparable scale on multiple 3D geometry benchmarks while remaining competitive on RGB-D semantic segmentation tasks.

Conclusion: The proposed representation successfully improves 3D awareness while preserving semantic transfer, demonstrating that depth information can be effectively integrated into visual representations for embodied AI applications.

Abstract: We introduce a self-supervised framework for learning joint visuospatial representations from RGB-D observations. While modern vision foundation models are trained almost exclusively on RGB images, many embodied systems have access to explicit depth sensing, which provides geometric information that monocular inputs cannot recover. Our method integrates depth-derived geometric priors with a visual backbone through inter-patch and intra-patch fusion, enabling the model to encode both appearance and spatial structure efficiently. The resulting representation shows promising improvements on 3D awareness while preserving semantic transfer: it outperforms prior methods of comparable scale on multiple 3D geometry benchmarks, and remains competitive when probed for standard RGB-D semantic segmentation tasks.

</details>


### [82] [UniFLM: United Segmentation and Measurement on Fetal Limb Ultrasonic Image](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27240)
*Zeen Zhou,Qiuhua Chen,Xiaojun Cao,Changmao Chen,Chao Sun,Bo Du*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents UniFLM, a unified framework for automatic cross-plane segmentation and measurement of fetal long bones in ultrasound images, addressing the lack of high-quality annotated data and unified frameworks for detecting fetal lethal skeletal dysplasias.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prenatal ultrasound examination is crucial for assessing fetal limb development and detecting congenital anomalies. However, existing AI models overlook fetal lethal skeletal dysplasias due to lack of high-quality annotated data and unified frameworks for multiple long bones. Generic segmentation models also struggle with noise and semantic gaps in ultrasound images.

Method: The authors construct the Fetal Limb Bones (FLB) dataset with high-quality annotations for humerus, femur, tibia-fibula, and radius-ulna. They propose UniFLM incorporating: (1) Semantic-Aware Skip Connection module to bridge semantic gap between encoder and decoder features; (2) Positive Sampling strategy to adaptively filter noise and extract essential semantic information; (3) Point Regression Mapping module to learn clinician annotation patterns for precise bone length measurement.

Result: Extensive experiments on the FLB dataset demonstrate that UniFLM achieves superior accuracy and enhanced generalization capabilities in fetal long bone assessment compared to current state-of-the-art models.

Conclusion: The proposed UniFLM framework successfully addresses the challenges of fetal long bone segmentation and measurement in ultrasound images, providing a unified solution that outperforms existing methods in accuracy and generalization.

Abstract: Prenatal ultrasound examination is crucial for assessing fetal limb development and detecting congenital anomalies. However, existing artificial intelligence models often overlook fetal lethal skeletal dysplasias due to the lack of high-quality annotated data and a unified framework for multiple long bones. Moreover, generic segmentation models struggle with the inherent noise and semantic gaps in ultrasound images. To address these challenges, we construct the Fetal Limb Bones (FLB) dataset, comprising high-quality annotations for the humerus, femur, tibia-fibula, and radius-ulna. Furthermore, we propose UniFLM, a unified framework for automatic cross-plane segmentation and measurement. UniFLM incorporates a Semantic-Aware Skip Connection module to bridge the semantic gap between encoder and decoder features, and a Positive Sampling strategy to adaptively filter noise and extract essential semantic information. Finally, a Point Regression Mapping module is introduced to learn clinician annotation patterns for precise bone length measurement. Extensive experiments conducted on the FLB dataset demonstrate that the proposed UniFLM achieves superior accuracy and enhanced generalization capabilities in fetal long bone assessment compared to current state-of-the-art models.

</details>


### [83] [Sidecar: Training-Free Semantic Reuse for Character-Consistent Free-form Visual Storytelling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27280)
*Sibo Dong,Sarah Adel Bargal*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Visual storytelling requires generating images that follow a narrative while preserving consistent character identities across frames. In free-form story generation, a character is fully described only when first introduced and is later referred to by a type-level mention or pronoun. Although this setting better reflects natural storytelling, later prompts may omit important identity-related semantics, making character consistency more difficult to maintain. We propose \textbf{Sidecar}, a plug-and-play semantic augmentation module that preserves entity-level information from the initial description and injects the missing semantics into later prompt embeddings. Sidecar requires no additional training and does not modify the architecture of the base diffusion model. Experiments on FreeStoryBench show that Sidecar consistently improves prompt-image alignment and character consistency across multiple SDXL- and FLUX-based baselines, with negligible computational overhead.

</details>


### [84] [TADP: Task-Aware Deformable Prediction for Single-Stage 3D Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27282)
*Su Wang,Yaochen Li,Min Yang,Jiaohao Nie,Chang Liu,Yuehu Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A novel task-aware deformable prediction (TADP) method for single-stage 3D object detection that addresses the limitation of using shared features for multiple tasks by introducing adaptive feature extraction and task-specific deformation heads.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-stage 3D object detectors use the same extracted features for different tasks, but it's impossible to project features into a common space that is adaptive for all tasks.

Method: 1) Triple Feature Refinement Aggregation module for three-level adaptive feature extraction; 2) Multi-scale Feature Aggregation block for scale-aware feature fusion; 3) Plug-and-play Task-aware Deformation Head that perceives task emphasis and interaction, with three different deformation modules designed.

Result: On KITTI dataset, car mAP reaches 80.91%, surpassing many state-of-the-art methods. The deformation head also shows good generalization when applied to other detection methods.

Conclusion: TADP effectively addresses the feature adaptation problem in single-stage 3D detectors through task-aware deformation, achieving state-of-the-art performance on KITTI with 80.91% car mAP.

Abstract: Most single-stage 3D object detectors complete different tasks with the same extracted features. Nevertheless, it is impossible to project features into a common space that is adaptive for all the tasks. We present a novel task-aware deformable prediction (TADP) method for single-stage 3D object detection to solve this problem. Firstly, a triple feature refinement aggregation module is designed to extract three-level features adaptively. Additionally, we design the multi-scale feature aggregation block to fuse multi-scale features in a scale-aware manner. Finally, the prediction of each task is deformed with the designed plug-and-play task-aware deformation head. It can percept the emphasis and interaction of each task. We also designed three different deformation modules. The experimental results demonstrate that the proposed deformation head shows good results on other detection methods. The experimental results on the KITTI dataset demonstrate that the car mAP is 80.91%, surpassing many state-of-the-art methods on the KITTI benchmark.

</details>


### [85] [Detection of Christmas tree plantations from high-resolution aerial imagery. A case study in the French Morvan](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27290)
*Francesca Razzano,Emanuele Dalsasso,Adrien Baysse-Lainé,Silvia Liberata Ullo,Gilda Schirinzi,Jocelyn Chanussot*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses the challenging task of mapping Christmas tree plantations using deep learning and remote sensing, treating it as a rare-target semantic segmentation problem. The authors introduce a Hard Negative Mining strategy to handle visual confusion with surrounding vegetation and severe class imbalance. The best model (DeepLabV3+ResNet-34) achieves IoU of 0.733 and F1 of 0.846, with HNM significantly improving precision-recall behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Christmas tree plantations represent an economically relevant but largely unexplored application in remote sensing. The task is particularly challenging due to high planting density, short rotation cycles, visual similarity with surrounding vegetation, limited training data for only one reference year, and severe class imbalance at the landscape scale. Existing deep learning methods are designed for forests, generic plantations, or orchards and fail to address the unique structural characteristics and hard-negative confusion of Christmas tree plantations.

Method: The study makes three key contributions: (1) Framing Christmas tree plantation mapping as a distinct rare-target semantic segmentation problem; (2) Introducing a Hard Negative Mining (HNM) strategy to improve discrimination against confusing background patterns; (3) Evaluating the framework across complementary levels including supervised testing, temporal transfer, and large-scale validation. The best model uses DeepLabV3 architecture with a ResNet-34 encoder.

Result: On the 2020 held-out test set, DeepLabV3 with ResNet-34 achieves an IoU of 0.733 and F1-score of 0.846. HNM substantially improves precision-recall behavior, increasing the area under the precision-recall curve from 0.204 to 0.913. Temporal inference demonstrates meaningful transferability with IoU/F1 of 0.751/0.858 on 2017/2018 data and 0.691/0.817 on 2023 data. Large-scale validation reveals the intrinsic difficulty—the plantations occupied only 1,498.4 ha (1.72%) in 2017/2018 and 1,782.2 ha (2.04%) in 2023 out of 87,309.4 ha total.

Conclusion: The study successfully demonstrates that Christmas tree plantation mapping can be effectively addressed as a rare-target semantic segmentation problem with appropriate deep learning frameworks. The proposed Hard Negative Mining strategy is crucial for handling the visual confusion and class imbalance inherent in this task. The temporal transfer results (2017/2018 to 2023) show reasonable generalization capability, though performance degrades over time, highlighting the need for periodic model updates. The large-scale validation underscores the challenge of mapping rare targets occupying less than 2% of the landscape.

Abstract: Christmas tree plantations are economically relevant, yet a largely unexplored application domain in Remote Sensing (RS). Their delineation is challenging because of high planting density, short rotation cycles, visual confusion with surrounding vegetation, the availability of dense labels for one reference year only, and severe class imbalance at the landscape scale. Although Deep Learning (DL) methods have shown strong potential for vegetation mapping, existing approaches are typically designed for forests, generic plantation systems, or orchards, and do not explicitly address the structural specificity and hard-negative confusion that characterize Christmas tree plantations. In response to these challenges, this work makes three main contributions: (i) it frames Christmas tree plantation mapping as a distinct rare-target semantic segmentation problem; (ii) it introduces a Hard Negative Mining (HNM) strategy to improve discrimination against confusing background patterns; and (iii) it evaluates the proposed framework across complementary levels, including supervised testing, temporal transfer, and large-scale validation. On the 2020 test set held out, the best model, DeepLabV3 with a ResNet-34 encoder, achieves an IoU of 0.733 and an F1-score of 0.846. HNM substantially improves precision-recall behavior, increasing the area under the precision-recall curve from 0.204 to 0.913. Temporal inference further shows meaningful transferability, reaching IoU/F1 values of 0.751/0.858 on 2017/2018 and 0.691/0.817 on 2023. Large-scale validation further highlights the intrinsic difficulty of the task, as Christmas tree plantations occupied only a very small fraction of the extent of the common evaluation, corresponding to 1,498.4 ha (1.72\%) in 2017/2018 and 1,782.2 ha (2.04\%) in 2023 out of 87,309.4 ha in total.

</details>


### [86] [R2M-Bench: Evaluating Revisit Memory via Relative Consistency in Interactive Video World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27328)
*Qiwen Gu,Bingjie Gao,Rui Chen,Geng Li,Jifan Li,Qishuai Wen,Li Niu,Jing Tang,Xiangxiang Chu,Junqiao Zhao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: R2M-Bench introduces relative revisit memory evaluation to address ambiguity in absolute revisit scores for video world models. The benchmark compares return frames with matched controls (non-revisit and short-range pairs) to compute MemoryGain and Normalized Memory Ratio (NMR), distinguishing revisit-specific consistency from generic temporal stability. Across seven models, Overall NMR correlates with human judgments (Spearman's ρ=0.547), reducing the slow-motion shortcut compared to raw revisit similarity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Absolute revisit similarity scores are ambiguous because high similarity could come from either memory retention or minimal change during rollout. This creates sensitivity to rendering stability, repetitive content, and failed motion - making it hard to distinguish genuine scene memory from low-dynamic scenarios.

Method: R2M-Bench combines 100 reference scenes with three leave-and-return trajectories (300 instances). For each detected return, it compares the revisit pair with two controls: gap-matched non-revisit pairs measuring generic temporal stability, and short-range pairs estimating short-horizon consistency. This yields MemoryGain (revisit advantage over baseline) and NMR (normalized by short-to-baseline dynamic range). Evaluation covers appearance fidelity, scene/object identity, local geometry, and persistent state.

Result: Overall NMR correlates with human consistency judgments at Spearman's ρ=0.547 (95% CI [0.45,0.63]). Within-model correlation with generated motion is 0.072 for NMR vs 0.207 for raw revisit similarity, showing relative calibration substantially reduces the slow-motion shortcut. DreamX-World-Memo achieves highest Overall NMR.

Conclusion: Same-rollout relative calibration effectively distinguishes revisit-specific consistency from generic temporal stability, providing a practical methodology for evaluating video world model memory. The NMR metric offers a more human-aligned and motion-invariant assessment than absolute revisit scores.

Abstract: High similarity between first-visit and return frames does not necessarily show that a video world model remembered the scene; the intervening rollout may simply have changed very little. This ambiguity makes absolute revisit scores sensitive to rendering stability, repetitive content, and failed motion. We introduce \emph{R2M-Bench} (\textbf{R}elative \textbf{R}evisit \textbf{M}emory Benchmark), a benchmark of observable revisit-selective consistency. For every detected return, R2M-Bench compares the revisit pair with two controls from the same rollout: a gap-matched non-revisit pair that measures generic temporal stability and a short-range pair that estimates short-horizon consistency. These comparisons produce \emph{MemoryGain} (MG), the revisit advantage over the temporal baseline, and the \emph{Normalized Memory Ratio} (NMR), which normalizes this advantage by the short-to-baseline dynamic range. R2M-Bench combines 100 reference scenes with three leave-and-return trajectories to form 300 instances and evaluates appearance fidelity, scene and object identity, local geometry, and persistent state. Across seven action-conditioned video world models, Overall NMR correlates with human consistency judgments at Spearman's $ρ=0.547$ (95\% CI $[0.45,0.63]$). Its within-model correlation magnitude with generated motion is $0.072$, compared with $0.207$ for raw revisit similarity, indicating that relative calibration substantially reduces the slow-motion shortcut. DreamX-World-Memo achieves the highest Overall NMR among the evaluated video models. Together, these results support same-rollout relative calibration as a practical way to distinguish revisit-specific consistency from generic temporal stability.

</details>


### [87] [PAWBench: How Far Are We from Probabilistically Aligned World Modeling?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27345)
*Yuandong Pu,Le Zhuo,Sayak Paul,Gabriel Jorge Menezes,Avram Đorđević,Shiyang Li,Yifan Zhou,Bin Fu,Wenlong Zhang,Junjun He,Yu Qiao,Yihao Liu,Jingbo Xing,Xi Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces PAWBench, a benchmark to evaluate whether video generation models can serve as probabilistically aligned world models by correctly modeling the distribution of possible physical behaviors, rather than just generating plausible single trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current video generation models are increasingly used as world models, but existing evaluations only assess individual video plausibility without testing whether repeated generations recover the correct distribution of possible behaviors. The authors seek to determine how far current models are from probabilistically aligned world modeling.

Method: The authors formalize "probabilistic alignment" as a distributional criterion and introduce PAWBench (benchmark) and PAWEval (evaluation protocol). PAWEval converts repeated video rollouts into empirical distributions over physical behaviors. They test 11 models across 50 scenarios.

Result: Across 50 scenarios and eleven current systems, no model consistently matches the reference probabilities while recovering the range of valid behaviors. The authors also test whether language prompts, initial noise sampling, or model training can reshape the predictive distribution.

Conclusion: There exists a significant gap between current video generators and probabilistically aligned world modeling. This work provides a foundation for future efforts to develop world models that correctly capture the distribution of possible physical outcomes.

Abstract: Recent video generation models are increasingly framed as world models. Many physical processes can unfold in more than one valid way. Therefore, a world model should reproduce not only a plausible trajectory, but also the distribution of possible behaviors under the same initial observation and action. We call this distribution-level requirement probabilistic alignment. However, existing evaluations largely assess individual-video plausibility and do not test whether repeated generations recover the correct distribution. This raises a central question: how far are current video generators from probabilistically aligned world modeling? To answer it, we formalize probabilistic alignment as a distributional criterion for world models and introduce PAWBench, a benchmark for evaluating video generators as stochastic samplers of world dynamics. We further introduce PAWEval, an outcome-level protocol that converts repeated video rollouts into empirical distributions over possible physical behaviors. Across 50 scenarios and eleven current systems, no model consistently matches the reference probabilities while recovering the range of valid behaviors. Having established this gap, we test whether language prompts, initial noise sampling, or model training can reshape the model's predictive distribution. We believe our work can serve as a foundation for future efforts to move towards probabilistically aligned world modeling.

</details>


### [88] [KnockGS:interaction-Grounded Calibrationof Physical Gaussian Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27365)
*Chenchen Ge,Hanwen Shen,Bowen Jing,Jiyuan Cai,Xiaofeng Wang,Hongsen Lei,Weitao Zhou,Dandan Zhang,Haibao Yu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: KnockGS is a framework that estimates elasticity and density scales of 3D Gaussian objects from their observed dynamics under known applied forces, then uses these estimates to enable accurate physics-based simulation and rendering of deformable objects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing physics-integrated 3D Gaussian pipelines require material parameters to be manually specified or known a priori, limiting applicability when these parameters must be inferred from observed object dynamics.

Method: The framework treats force-induced response as a calibration signal: temporal response features are extracted from observed dynamics, two material scales (elasticity and density) are estimated from those features, and the estimate is frozen and written back into the same simulator for testing on unseen interactions.

Result: Across five held-out material targets, the method recovers scales substantially more accurately than response retrieval, global regression, or fixed default material. The frozen estimate remains predictive under interactions differing in direction and magnitude.

Conclusion: Interaction response carries sufficient information to calibrate material scales in physically grounded 3D Gaussian representations, representing a first step toward interactive PhysicalGS systems with consistent rendered appearance and simulated response.

Abstract: Physics-integrated 3D Gaussian representations now allow reconstructed deformable objects to be simulated and rendered under explicit material models. Existing pipelines, however, assume that material parameters are known or manually specified, limiting their applicability when these parameters must be inferred from observed object dynamics. We propose KnockGS, an interaction-response PhysicalGS framework that estimates the elasticity and density scales of a 3D Gaussian object from its dynamics under a known applied force. Rather than treating physical simulation only as a forward process, we turn the force-induced response into a calibration signal: temporal response features are xtracted from the observed dynamics, the two material scales are estimated from those features, and the estimate is then frozen and written back into the same simulator so that it can be tested on an interaction it was never fitted to.We evaluate the framework on both parameter recovery and response-level fidelity. The estimated scales are compared against hidden ground truth, and the re-simulated object is measured against the target using 3D particle trajectories, response-curve statistics, and rendered-frame quality. Across five held-out material targets, our method recovers the scales substantially more accurately than response retrieval, global regression, or a fixed default material, and the frozen estimate remains predictive under interactions that differ in direction and in magnitude. Interaction response therefore carries enough information to calibrate material scales in physically grounded 3D Gaussian representations.Our study is a first step toward interactive PhysicalGS systems that calibrate a Gaussian asset whose rendered appearance and simulated response are consistent.

</details>


### [89] [Successive Capacity Growth: Task-Complexity-Driven Width and Depth Expansion for Vision Transformer Encoders in JEPA World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27367)
*Frederik Berenz*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes Successive Capacity Growth (SCG), a method for dynamically growing JEPA world model encoders from a minimal configuration instead of using fixed over-provisioned architectures. SCG incrementally adds width or depth based on task demands, using function-preserving expansion and a regularizer (SIGReg) to maintain representation quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current JEPAs use fixed-size Vision Transformer encoders that are either over-provisioned (wasting parameters on simple tasks) or under-provisioned (insufficient for complex tasks). There is a need for adaptive architectures that can grow based on task complexity while maintaining parameter efficiency.

Method: SCG starts with a minimal encoder (1 head, 2 layers, 283K parameters) and grows incrementally in width (adding attention heads) or depth (adding transformer blocks). A task-agnostic test-and-verify mechanism exploits function-preserving expansion to safely trial architectural changes and rolls back if prediction loss doesn't improve. The Sketched Isotropic Gaussian Regularizer (SIGReg) ensures semantic dimensions remain statistically independent and aligned with the predictive objective, preventing collapse.

Result: On a 60-dimensional multi-object dynamics task: 20.3% improvement over fixed small baseline with 56x parameter efficiency vs scaling to fixed large model. On a 2D navigation task: single width expansion yields 23% improvement over fixed large model. Across three tested environments, adaptive encoder matches/exceeds fixed small baseline with zero false-positive expansions and bit-exact function preservation (ratio=1.0, absolute difference=0.0).

Conclusion: JEPA world model encoders can grow successively as task demands require, rather than being pre-allocated at maximum capacity. This approach achieves significant compute and data efficiency while maintaining representation quality, eliminating the need to guess optimal encoder size upfront.

Abstract: Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) for world modeling typically employ fixed-size Vision Transformer encoders that are over-provisioned for simple tasks and under-provisioned for complex ones, with significant redundancy across attention heads. We propose Successive Capacity Growth (SCG), a method that starts from a minimal encoder (1 head, 2 layers, 283K parameters) and grows incrementally in width (adding attention heads for low-level semantic capacity) or depth (adding transformer blocks for higher-order semantic abstraction), driven by a task-agnostic test-and-verify mechanism that exploits function-preserving expansion to safely trial architectural changes and roll back if they do not improve prediction loss. The Sketched Isotropic Gaussian Regularizer (SIGReg) ensures that all learned semantic dimensions remain statistically independent and aligned with the predictive objective, preventing collapse even as the architecture grows. On a 60-dimensional multi-object dynamics task, SCG naturally triggers depth expansion, improving prediction loss by 20.3% over the fixed small baseline with 56 times greater parameter efficiency than scaling to the fixed large model; on a 2D navigation task, a single width expansion yields even an 23% improvement over the fixed large model. Across all three tested environments of increasing complexity, the adaptive encoder matches or exceeds the fixed small baseline, with zero false-positive expansions and bit-exact function preservation (ratio = 1.0, absolute difference = 0.0). The take-away is that JEPA world model encoders need not be pre-allocated at maximum capacity - they can grow successively as the task demands, achieving significant compute and data efficiency while maintaining representation quality.

</details>


### [90] [LeVJEPA: Efficient & Scalable Video Pretraining without the Heuristics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27395)
*Lukas Kuhn,Lucas Maes,Giuseppe Serra,Quentin Le Lidec,Yann LeCun,Randall Balestriero,Florian Buettner*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LeVJEPA is a novel video encoder trained under a collapse-free objective (LeJEPA) that eliminates the need for architectural asymmetries (EMA target encoder, stop-gradient, predictor) or pixel-space reconstruction. It uses a single encoder with invariance loss over global/local views, regularized by SIGReg. The method achieves 5.6-20.8x less compute than V-JEPA 2 while matching or exceeding its performance, and exceeds the strongest video baseline by 7.6 points on ImageNet-1K.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video representation learning has remained computationally expensive. Existing self-supervised methods either require architectural asymmetries (EMA target encoder, stop-gradient, capacity-limited predictor) to prevent representation collapse, or rely on pixel-space reconstruction. The authors aim to eliminate these computational overheads while maintaining or improving performance.

Method: 1) Single encoder trained with invariance loss over global and local views of a video clip
2) Regularized by SIGReg (SIGnificance REGularizer) which provides provable guarantee against collapse
3) Architecture simplified to encoder + projector only (single hyperparameter)
4) Uniform random token dropping to reduce computational cost
5) Can optionally use block-causal attention (temporal ordering becomes property of encoder itself)

Result: 1) At matched epochs: matches/surpasses V-JEPA 2 across ViT-S/B/L at 5.6 to 20.8x less pretraining compute
2) At matched total FLOPs: exceeds strongest video baseline by 7.6 points on ImageNet-1K
3) Competitive on motion-centric benchmarks
4) Against compute-matched DINOv2: approaches image-pretrained encoder on appearance-centric evaluation while nearly doubling motion-centric accuracy

Conclusion: Once computational overhead is removed, video becomes a viable and in several respects preferable substrate for general-purpose visual pretraining. The collapse-free objective combined with efficient token dropping makes video a competitive alternative to image-based pretraining.

Abstract: Video carries the temporal structure of the physical world, yet learning representations from it has remained computationally expensive: prevailing self-supervised methods either prevent representation collapse through architectural asymmetries, coupling an exponential-moving-average target encoder, a stop-gradient, and a capacity-limited predictor, or circumvent it by reconstructing masked content in pixel space. We introduce LeVJEPA, the first video encoder trained under LeJEPA's collapse-free objective, which dispenses with both. A single encoder is trained with an invariance loss over global and local views of a clip, regularized by SIGReg, which excludes collapse with a provable guarantee. The architecture reduces to an encoder and a projector, and the objective to a single hyperparameter. This formulation admits two properties. First, the cost of pretraining is governed by the number of tokens the encoder observes; uniform random token dropping renders this number small while simultaneously improving downstream accuracy. At matched epochs on identical data, LeVJEPA matches or surpasses V-JEPA 2 across ViT-S/B/L at 5.6 to 20.8x less pretraining compute, and at matched total FLOPs it exceeds the strongest video baseline by 7.6 points on ImageNet-1K while remaining competitive on motion-centric benchmarks. Second, since no asymmetry between branches is required, the encoder can be trained with block-causal attention at no measurable accuracy cost: temporal ordering becomes a property of the encoder itself. Against a compute-matched DINOv2 trained on frames of the same videos, LeVJEPA approaches the image-pretrained encoder on appearance-centric evaluation while nearly doubling its motion-centric accuracy. These results indicate that, once its computational overhead is removed, video becomes a viable and in several respects preferable substrate for general-purpose visual pretraining.

</details>


### [91] [Reconstructing Humans and Objects in Interaction using Large Reconstruction Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27407)
*Agniv Chatterjee,Georgios Pavlakos*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MILO is a framework that leverages Large Reconstruction Models (LRMs) as geometric scaffolds to recover detailed 3D human-object interactions from a single image, rather than relying on traditional reprojection and contact constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 3D HOI reconstruction is challenging due to depth ambiguities, occlusions, and object shape variability. Existing approaches focus on fitting parametric models to 2D images, which is complex and limited.

Method: MILO uses LRMs to provide a geometric scaffold preserving human-object arrangement and proximity. The pipeline segments the LRM mesh into human and object components, fits a parametric body model (SMPL) to the human part, and optionally aligns an object template to the object part.

Result: MILO achieves strong reconstruction accuracy and outperforms existing baselines across multiple benchmarks and interaction scenarios.

Conclusion: The LRM-based approach simplifies the 3D HOI reconstruction problem by providing a powerful geometric scaffold, enabling accurate reconstruction from a single image with applications in AR/VR, robotics, and embodied AI.

Abstract: Estimation of Human-Object Interactions in 3D (3D HOI) is a fundamental problem in 3D computer vision with applications in AR/VR, robotics, and embodied AI. However, reconstructing these interactions in 3D remains challenging due to depth ambiguities, occlusions, and object shape variability. Existing approaches are primarily concerned with reprojection and contact constraints, fitting parametric human models and object templates to 2D images. In this paper, we explore a different avenue. We present MILO, a framework that leverages the visual capabilities of Large Reconstruction Models (LRMs) to recover detailed 3D human-object interactions from a single image. Our key observation is that LRMs provide a powerful geometric scaffold that preserves relative human-object arrangement and proximity cues. This significantly simplifies the reconstruction procedure, reframing the problem as interpreting the LRM mesh: we segment it into human and object components, fit a parametric body model to the human part, and optionally align an object template to the object part (if such a template is available). MILO achieves strong reconstruction accuracy and outperforms existing baselines across multiple benchmarks and interaction scenarios. Our code is available at https://ac5113.github.io/MILO.

</details>


### [92] [Retrieval Heads Meet Vision: Uncovering How VLMs Locate and Extract Visual Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27417)
*Chanho Park,Daehyeon Choi,Jihyun Lee,Minhyuk Sung*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper introduces Visual Retrieval Heads (VRHs) - a small subset of attention heads (~1.7-2.6%) in Vision-Language Models that are causally responsible for grounding text descriptions to image regions. These heads are analogous to retrieval heads found in LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While VLMs can locate image regions referred to by text prompts and route visual evidence to outputs, the internal mechanism behind this behavior was not understood. The authors sought to determine whether VLMs contain an analogous mechanism to retrieval heads in LLMs.

Method: The authors recast existing head-scoring methods under a unified design space over query tokens, key aggregation, and cross-sample aggregation. They discovered that scoring attention from output prediction tokens with a sum over the ground-truth referent region most reliably identifies causal heads. They tested across eleven VLMs and five referring-expression benchmarks.

Result: Masking only the top 20 VRHs reduces grounding accuracy by up to 80 percentage points, while masking the same number of random heads has little effect. VRHs generalize across visual reference tasks (attribute, spatial, counting, visual-math benchmarks), are functionally specific (preserving output format while corrupting localization), and are architecturally shared (transferring causally across VLMs with shared LLM backbones).

Conclusion: The study identifies VRHs as the causal mechanism for visual grounding in VLMs, demonstrating a causal-sparse-universal triad similar to text retrieval heads. These findings suggest VRHs are fundamental to how VLMs connect language to visual regions, with implications for understanding and interpreting VLM architectures.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) can locate an image region referred to by a text prompt and route the corresponding visual evidence to the output, yet the internal mechanism behind this behavior is not understood. Inspired by retrieval heads in large language models, we ask whether VLMs contain an analogous mechanism for visual retrieval. We answer affirmatively by introducing Visual Retrieval Heads (VRHs), a small subset of attention heads (about 1.7-2.6%) that are causally responsible for grounding text descriptions to image regions. To find them, we recast existing head-scoring methods under a unified design space over query tokens, key aggregation, and cross-sample aggregation. We then show that scoring attention from output prediction tokens with a sum over the ground-truth referent region most reliably identifies causal heads. Across eleven VLMs and five referring-expression benchmarks, masking only the top 20 VRHs reduces grounding accuracy by up to 80 percentage points, while masking the same number of random heads has little effect. Beyond replicating the causal-sparse-universal triad established for text retrieval heads, VRHs exhibit several properties not previously reported: they generalize across visual reference tasks, remaining causal on attribute, spatial, counting, and visual-math benchmarks despite being discovered through bounding-box prediction; they are functionally specific, preserving output format while corrupting localization; and they are architecturally shared, transferring causally across VLMs that share an LLM backbone but differ in vision encoder, projector, and instruction tuning.

</details>


### [93] [UrbanGround: From Local Perception to Spatial Agency in a Real-Scale City](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27456)
*Tianjie Ju,Zheng Wu,Yueqing Sun,Yuhan Cui,Bobo Li,Shengqiong Wu,Pengzhou Cheng,Haodong Zhao,Zongru Wu,Xinbei Ma,Doris Zhang,Kunling Li,Mong-Li Lee,Wynne Hsu,Hao Fei,Qi Gu,Gongshen Liu,Zhuosheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces UrbanGround, a 3D Hong Kong sandbox for testing whether multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can translate street-level visual perception into reliable urban navigation. The study evaluates MLLM agents across three progressively challenging spatial tasks: local scene grounding, extended navigation, and dynamic pedestrian-aware movement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current MLLMs can interpret street views, but it remains unclear whether this local perception can drive reliable action as agents move through complex real-scale urban environments. Existing benchmarks lack physically constrained, closed-loop testing in authentic city settings.

Method: UrbanGround is built from territory-wide 3D geospatial data of Hong Kong, providing first-person view exploration and interactive map navigation. The study evaluates MLLM agents through three research questions: (1) local scene grounding for spatial question answering after active observation, (2) navigation support as destinations become farther and less explicit, and (3) robustness to route changes and pedestrian motion.

Result: Contemporary MLLM agents demonstrate useful atomic abilities in visual recognition and short-range spatial reasoning. However, orientation and pedestrian-aware movement remain unreliable. The central failure occurs during extended exploration where local abilities fail to compose into sustained goal-directed behavior and errors accumulate without effective correction.

Conclusion: Current MLLM agents cannot reliably explore complex, open-ended urban environments over extended periods. The gap between atomic visual perception and sustained navigation remains a fundamental limitation that requires further research, with UrbanGround providing a benchmark for future development.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can interpret a street view, but urban agency depends on whether such local evidence remains useful after the agent starts to move. In this paper, we investigate how far current MLLM agents can turn local urban perception into reliable action in a complicated real-scale city. We propose UrbanGround, the first sandbox to make this question testable in a physically constrained replica of Hong Kong built from territory-wide 3D geospatial data. UrbanGround supports closed-loop interaction from a first-person view and provides an interactive map for navigation. Agents can directly enter the 3D city and explore from a first-person view. Our analysis follows the growth of the spatial problem through three research questions. We first test whether an agent can ground a local scene well enough to answer spatial questions after active observation. Then we ask whether that grounding supports navigation as destinations become farther away and less explicit. Finally, we examine whether the resulting behavior survives changes in route availability and pedestrian motion. Contemporary MLLM agents usually show useful atomic abilities in visual recognition and short-range spatial reasoning, while orientation and pedestrian-aware movement remain unreliable. Their central failure emerges over extended exploration, where local abilities do not compose into sustained goal-directed behavior and errors accumulate without effective correction. We hope UrbanGround will support broader study of how far current MLLM agents can explore reliably in complex, open-ended urban environments.

</details>

### [94] [EduRiskX: A Neuro-Symbolic Framework with F-Logic Reasoning for Early Academic Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26107)
*Yu Fu,Yongqi Kang,Yong Zhao,Rongfang Bie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EduRiskX is a neuro-symbolic framework combining temporal Transformers with F-Logic reasoning to predict student academic risk in online education, addressing limited early detection and interpretability issues in existing models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing student risk prediction models suffer from limited early detection capability and insufficient interpretability, creating a 'black-box' trust crisis that hinders adoption in real-world educational settings.

Method: EduRiskX integrates: (1) Temporal Transformer-based neural predictor with temporal attention, class-weighted loss, and dynamic weekly truncation for modeling longitudinal student activity sequences; (2) F-Logic symbolic reasoning module grounded in Engagement Theory and Student Integration Model to mimic human educator diagnostic logic; (3) Logistic regression-based fusion mechanism combining neural risk probability and symbolic confidence scores.

Result: On OULAD dataset with 80/10/10 student-level split, EduRiskX achieves 0.900 accuracy and 0.894 F1-score at Week 38, with average early detection at week 9.32 and detection rate of 94.30%. It outperforms state-of-the-art models (PatchTST, iTransformer) and baselines (LSTM, CNN) in recall and earlier risk identification.

Conclusion: The neuro-symbolic approach successfully balances predictive performance with interpretability through F-Logic rule-based explanations linking predictions to behavioral patterns and educational theories, enabling more trustworthy adoption in pedagogical settings.

Abstract: Predicting students' academic risk in online education is crucial for enabling timely interventions that can improve retention and learning outcomes. However, existing models often suffer from limited early detection capability and insufficient interpretability, leading to a "black-box" trust crisis that hinders their adoption in real-world pedagogical settings. To address these challenges, we propose EduRiskX, a neuro-symbolic framework that integrates a temporal Transformer-based predictor with F-Logic symbolic reasoning. The neural component models longitudinal student activity sequences using temporal attention, class-weighted loss, and dynamic weekly truncation. Acting as a data-driven expert system, an F-Logic rule base -- grounded in established educational theories (Engagement Theory and Student Integration Model) to mimic the diagnostic logic of human educators -- is constructed exclusively from the training data. The neural risk probability and the symbolic confidence score are then combined through a logistic regression-based fusion mechanism that learns the relative contribution of each signal. Experiments on the Open University Learning Analytics Dataset (OULAD) using a strict 80/10/10 student-level split show that EduRiskX achieves an accuracy of 0.900 and an F1-score of 0.894 at the end of the semester (Week 38), with an average early detection week of 9.32 and a detection rate of 94.30 percent. Compared with state-of-the-art time-series models (PatchTST, iTransformer) and common deep learning baselines (LSTM, CNN), EduRiskX yields improved recall and earlier risk identification under identical conditions. Beyond predictive performance, the F-Logic module provides structured rule-based explanations linking predictions to observable behavioral patterns and educational theories.

</details>


### [95] [Standalone LLM and a Pre-specified Agentic Pipeline for Explaining ICU Mortality Predictions: a Feasibility Study on the eICU Demo Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26109)
*Di Zhu,Chen Xie,Haoyun Zhang,Zihan Wei,Ziwei Wang,Jiazhao Shi,Ziyu Wang,Qiyang Xie*

Main category: cs.AI
