Abstract: Machine-learning models can predict ICU mortality accurately, but feature-attribution methods alone rarely provide the clinical narrative needed for bedside use. Large language models (LLMs) may bridge this gap, and multi-step agentic pipelines are a plausible extension because they separate data interpretation, guideline checking, and final explanation. This revised feasibility study preserves the original standalone-versus-agentic comparison while making the main clinical findings more explicit. Using the retained local eICU Demo artifact set (2,353 ICU stays; 8.1\% mortality), XGBoost achieved an AUROC of 0.855 (95\% CI 0.796--0.906) and an AUPRC of 0.332 (95\% CI 0.217--0.494). On a stratified 38-case explanation subset, the standalone LLM produced 1 explanation with explicit outcome leakage, whereas the four-step agentic pipeline produced none. Among the 14 cases that overlapped with the SHAP review subset, the standalone LLM showed higher SHAP alignment (mean Jaccard 0.171 versus 0.077) and higher direction consistency (92.9\% versus 78.6\%), while the agentic pipeline showed higher guideline grounding (0.762 versus 0.143), higher value specificity (0.236 versus 0.143), and slightly higher plausibility (0.700 versus 0.671). Clinically, the results suggest that agentic decomposition may improve safety-relevant grounding and patient-specific detail, but it should be paired with attribution-based checks before use in high-stakes risk explanation.

</details>


### [96] [Large Models for Battery Prognostics and Health Management: A Review and Future Roadmap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26111)
*Jiale Liu,Huan Wang,Weicheng Wang,Rong Zhu,Qiqi Wang,Min Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This review paper surveys the application of Large Models (LMs) based on Transformer architectures and self-supervised learning to Battery Prognostics and Health Management (BPHM). It addresses critical challenges in conventional BPHM approaches including data scarcity, generalization, and interpretability, while proposing a future research roadmap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional BPHM approaches (physics-based models and task-centric deep learning) face fundamental limitations in computational efficiency, cross-domain generalization, dependence on extensive labeled data, and model interpretability. This work aims to provide the first comprehensive survey of how emerging Large Models can overcome these long-standing bottlenecks in battery management.

Method: The review systematically examines LM applications across four critical dimensions: (1) mitigating data scarcity, (2) enhancing generalization and robustness, (3) integrating domain knowledge for interpretability, and (4) enabling system-level automation. It covers foundational technologies including Transformer architectures, self-supervised learning, multimodal datasets, and PEFT techniques.

Result: LMs demonstrate promising capabilities in addressing BPHM challenges. However, significant obstacles persist across data accessibility, intelligence validation, trustworthiness, and deployment feasibility.

Conclusion: The review establishes a systematic framework for understanding LM-driven BPHM and proposes a roadmap emphasizing: collaborative data ecosystems, industrial intelligence validation, physics-informed trustworthiness designs, and efficient on-device deployment. This provides essential guidance for developing next-generation autonomous battery management systems capable of safe and reliable operation throughout battery lifecycles.

Abstract: Battery Prognostics and Health Management (BPHM) is critical for ensuring the safe, reliable, and cost-effective operation of batteries across electric vehicles, grid storage, and consumer electronics. Conventional BPHM approaches, including physics-based models and task-centric deep learning methods, face challenges in computational efficiency and parameterization, cross-domain generalization, dependence on extensive labeled run-to-failure data, and model interpretability. Recent Large Models (LMs), built upon Transformer architectures and self-supervised pre-training, offer a transformative new paradigm to overcome these long-standing bottlenecks. This review provides the first comprehensive survey of LM applications in BPHM, systematically examining how these models address challenges in the field. We begin by elucidating the foundational technologies enabling LMs, including Transformer architectures, self-supervised learning, large-scale multimodal datasets, and PEFT techniques. We then categorize recent progress along four critical dimensions: mitigating data scarcity, enhancing generalization and robustness, integrating domain knowledge for interpretability, and enabling system-level automation. Despite promising results, significant challenges remain across data accessibility, intelligence validation, trustworthiness, and deployment feasibility. To guide future research, we propose a roadmap focused on building collaborative data ecosystems, validating intelligence for industrial applications, enhancing trustworthiness with physics-informed designs, and enabling efficient on-device deployment. This review establishes a systematic approach to understand and advance LM-driven BPHM, providing researchers and practitioners with essential insights for developing next-generation battery management systems capable of safe, reliable, and autonomous operation throughout battery lifecycles.

</details>


### [97] [PICasso: An AI-Enabled Design Framework for Autonomous Optimization of Silicon Photonic Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26113)
*Deepak Vungarala,Deniz Najafi,Abdulrahman Aljoudi,Zahra Ghanaatian,Navid Khoshavi,Gourav Datta,Arman Roohi,Mahdi Nikdast,Shaahin Angizi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: We present PICasso, an AI-assisted framework for automated synthesis, verification, and optimization of photonic integrated circuits (PICs) from natural-language specifications. PICasso couples a structured NL -> YAML -> GDS generation pipeline with PDK aware knowledge injection, automated placement and routing, DRC/LVS validation, and SAX-based photonic simulation. To systematically evaluate AI-driven photonic design, we introduce PIC-Set, a benchmark of 36 parameterized PIC design tasks spanning core photonic primitives and multi-component circuits. Using PIC-Set, we benchmark several state-of-the-art Large Language Models (LLMs) under a unified evaluation protocol, including new metrics such as structural and functional $Spec@k$, optimization efficiency, and robustness under perturbations. Across the benchmark, PICasso significantly improves end-to-end specification satisfaction compared to vanilla LLM generation. Structural $Spec@3$ reaches up to 92.7% and functional $Spec@3$ up to 52% on high-complexity circuits. In addition, PICasso consistently reduces circuit insertion loss, lowering the mean loss from 4.98 dB to 3.25 dB (1.74 dB improvement) through simulation-guided optimization. These results demonstrate that structured domain constraints, physical verification, and simulation feedback transform LLMs from brittle netlist generators into practical PIC design agents capable of producing manufacturable layouts with competitive runtimes relative to manual GUI-based workflows.

</details>


### [98] [CIFQA: A Deterministic Tool-Grounded Multi-Agent LLM Framework for Financial Query Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26114)
*Kunjesh Parekh,Anil Kumar Tiwari,Divya Saxena*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CIFQA is a deterministic tool-grounded multi-agent framework that separates language understanding from numerical execution to solve calculation-intensive financial queries. It uses specialized agents for query interpretation, routing, parameter extraction, computation planning, and response generation, with deterministic Python-based tools performing financial calculations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs often produce numerically incorrect yet plausible answers when solving multi-step financial calculations, despite performing strongly on natural language tasks. Financial question answering requires exact reasoning over structured rates, temporal conditions, numerical formulas, and rule-based constraints.

Method: Multi-agent LLM framework with specialized agents (query interpretation, routing, parameter extraction, computation planning, response generation) combined with deterministic Python-based tools for financial calculations and rule application. The framework is instantiated for fixed deposit query answering.

Result: CIFQA achieves 95.54% accuracy on calculation-intensive queries and 90.87% overall accuracy, substantially outperforming direct LLM baselines even with complete formulas, rate cards, and benchmark instructions. A 17B open-source backbone in CIFQA outperforms substantially larger frontier models.

Conclusion: Deterministic components (exact rate lookup, tenure computation, rolling-year adjustment, premature-withdrawal logic) are critical contributors to performance. Architectural design is more important than model scale for numerical reliability. CIFQA provides a generalizable framework for calculation-intensive financial reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Calculation-intensive financial question answering requires exact reasoning over structured rates, temporal conditions, numerical formulas, and rule-based constraints. Although Large Language Models (LLMs) perform strongly on natural language tasks, they often produce numerically incorrect yet plausible answers when solving multi-step financial calculations. To address this limitation, we introduce CIFQA (Calculation-Intensive Financial Query Answering), a deterministic tool-grounded multi-agent LLM framework for financial question answering. CIFQA separates language understanding from numerical execution by assigning specialized agents to query interpretation, routing, parameter extraction, computation planning, and response generation, while deterministic Python-based tools perform financial calculations and rule application. We instantiate CIFQA for fixed deposit query answering and evaluate it on a curated benchmark of fixed deposit queries. CIFQA achieves 95.54% accuracy on calculation-intensive queries and 90.87% overall accuracy, substantially outperforming direct LLM baselines even when provided with complete formulas, rate cards, and benchmark instructions. Ablation studies show that deterministic components such as exact rate lookup, tenure computation, rolling-year adjustment, and premature-withdrawal logic are critical contributors to performance. Notably, a 17B open-source backbone operating within CIFQA outperforms substantially larger frontier models evaluated with the same financial information, demonstrating that architectural design is a more important determinant of numerical reliability than model scale. While evaluated on fixed deposit queries, CIFQA provides a generalizable framework for calculation-intensive financial reasoning tasks.

</details>


### [99] [The Artificial Experimentalist: Discovery and Control of Self-Organizing Phenomena with Autotelic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26116)
*Marko Cvjetko,Benedikt Hartl,Michael Levin,Clément Moulin-Frier,Pierre-Yves Oudeyer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces CARL, a closed-loop framework using autotelic reinforcement learning to autonomously discover and control emergent phenomena in complex systems (specifically Lenia, a continuous cellular automaton). The agent learns to create stable solitons, steer their movement, and can be guided by humans through high-level commands.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for exploring cellular automata operate in open loop (set initial conditions, simulate, observe) without intervention during execution. There is a need for closed-loop approaches that can actively discover and control emergent phenomena in complex systems.

Method: The framework uses autotelic reinforcement learning where an agent autonomously samples diverse goals and learns a goal-conditioned policy to intervene in complex systems through minimal, local perturbations. The agent is applied to Lenia (continuous cellular automaton) in a system called CARL.

Result: Three capabilities demonstrated: (1) CARL discovers stable solitons across wide range of Lenia update rules at higher rate than heuristic baselines; (2) Learns to steer movement direction of existing solitons with few interventions; (3) Humans can use trained agents to guide solitons through maze environments via high-level directional commands. Agents generalize zero-shot to out-of-distribution conditions.

Conclusion: The results suggest a path toward artificial experimentalist agents that can autonomously or with human guidance discover and control emergent phenomena in complex systems.

Abstract: Existing methods for exploring cellular automata and other complex systems mostly operate in open loop: they set initial conditions, execute a full simulation, and observe the outcome, without intervening during execution. We introduce a closed-loop framework based on autotelic reinforcement learning, in which an agent autonomously samples diverse goals and learns a goal-conditioned policy to intervene in a complex system through minimal, local perturbations. We instantiate this framework on Lenia, a continuous cellular automaton known for life-like self-organizing patterns, in an agentic system we call CARL, and demonstrate three capabilities. First, CARL discovers stable solitons across a wide range of Lenia update rules at a higher rate than heuristic baselines. Second, it learns to steer the movement direction of existing solitons with few interventions, showing that CARL can control self-organizing patterns, not only create them. Third, humans can use trained agents to guide solitons through maze environments in real time by specifying high-level directional commands that the agent translates into low-level interventions. Trained across diverse goals, update rules, and random initial states, the agents acquire policies that generalize zero-shot to various out-of-distribution conditions. These results suggest a path toward artificial experimentalist agents that, autonomously or with human guidance, discover and control emergent phenomena in complex systems.

</details>


### [100] [The Accuracy-Efficiency Paradox Quantifying Net Energy Loss in on-Device Energy Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26134)
*Jaeik Jeong,Tai-Yeon Ku,Wan-Ki Park*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>

Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Energy forecasting aims to maximize accuracy to ensure energy efficiency by reducing energy waste, an objective that applies equally to on-device forecasting for mission-critical edge environments, including military systems. However, this paper identifies the Accuracy-Efficiency Paradox: high-precision energy forecasting models can ironically trigger a net energy deficit. This stems from both edge AI's inference energy consumption and battery aging. We propose a Total Cost of Ownership (TCO) framework for energy forecasting, designed to minimize net energy loss. This framework treats not only inference energy consumption but also battery aging as a unified form of energy loss, as degradation represents a physical dissipation of the system's future energy-carrying capacity. We demonstrate that in thermally sensitive edge environments, energy saved by the superior precision of complex architectures is often outweighed by the total energy lost through their high operational intensity.

</details>


### [101] [LLMs for Academic Workflows: An Evaluation of Literature Reviews Generated with Short and Long Context Windows of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26145)
*Muhammad Ali Chaudhry,Xinyuan Hao,Haifa Alwahaby*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Our research focuses on evaluating literature reviews generated in short and long context settings of large language models (LLMs) to investigate the impact of context window on the quality of AI-generated literature reviews and the role of AI in supporting literature review writing. Twenty AI-generated literature reviews based on research sources from Semantic Scholar and Arxiv were evaluated by two researchers across 15 dimensions. Our findings reveal that AI-generated literature reviews require human oversight to meet academic publishing standards. As context windows increase, LLMs can incorporate broader information and maintain coherence across longer inputs, but they also exacerbate issues such as content repetition, omission of critical work, and a tendency towards descriptiveness over synthesis. Our work shows that AI-generated reviews can provide foundational overviews, but their output must be critically evaluated and refined by domain experts. Future research should consider integrating other LLMs and fine-tuned models in different domains with hybrid approaches that combine human expertise with AI capabilities to address the limitations identified in this study.

</details>


### [102] [Methodological and Conceptual Framework for 5D Multi-Table Analysis: A Unified Approach for Complex Data Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26149)
*Edouard Lansiaux,Hugo Kazzi,Aurélien Loison,Slim Hammadi,Emmanuel Chazard*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RHT (Relational Hypergraph Transformer) is a unified architecture for multi-table learning in healthcare databases, representing relational databases as hypergraphs and learning pentadimensional embeddings with sparse relational attention. It achieves highest embedding semantic coherence while XGBoost achieves best rare-code recall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-table learning in healthcare and complex information systems faces challenges including large data volume, high-dimensional variables, high-cardinality categorical features, complex inter-table dependencies, and repeated temporal observations. Existing methods struggle with these complexities.

Method: RHT represents relational databases as hypergraphs, learns pentadimensional embeddings (PentE), and performs sparse relational attention with complexity proportional to average relational degree (not square of entities). Evaluated on Synthea synthetic EHR dataset for multi-label prediction of SNOMED CT condition codes per encounter.

Result: XGBoost achieved highest rare-code recall, while RHT attained strongest embedding semantic coherence. Ablation studies quantified contribution of each architectural component.

Conclusion: RHT produces more semantically coherent embeddings while remaining computationally scalable. Clinical validation on MIMIC-IV is planned. Source code and experimental protocols are provided.

Abstract: Multi-table learning remains a major challenge in machine learning for healthcare and other complex information systems. Relational data combine several sources of complexity, including large data volume, high-dimensional variables, high-cardinality categorical features, complex inter-table dependencies, and repeated temporal observations. We introduce the Relational Hypergraph Transformer (RHT), a unified architecture that represents relational databases as hypergraphs, learns pentadimensional embeddings (PentE), and performs sparse relational attention with complexity proportional to the average relational degree rather than the square of the number of entities. We formally define the architecture, derive the complexity of its attention mechanism, and provide an open-source reference implementation. We evaluate RHT on the public Synthea synthetic electronic health record dataset using multi-label prediction of SNOMED CT condition codes per encounter, a task characterized by high categorical cardinality and long-tailed label distributions. Comparisons with tabular, relational, and temporal graph baselines show that RHT produces more semantically coherent embeddings while remaining computationally scalable. In this benchmark, the highest rare-code recall is achieved by XGBoost, whereas RHT attains the strongest embedding semantic coherence. We also report ablation studies quantifying the contribution of each architectural component. Clinical validation on MIMIC-IV is planned following PhysioNet credentialing. Source code and experimental protocols are provided in the accompanying repository.

</details>


### [103] [Leveraging Large Language Models for Systematic Literature Review of Disease Spread Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26150)
*Orhan Yagizer Cinar,Timur Emre Ozkose,Emma Von Hoene,Amira Roess,Taylor Anderson,Hamdi Kavak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study develops an LLM pipeline to extract information from 536 agent-based modeling papers and compares results with human-conducted systematic literature reviews, finding GPT-5.0 achieves 81.67% paper-level accuracy while identifying LLM agreement as a quality indicator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent advancements in LLMs create opportunities to streamline or automate systematic literature reviews (SLRs), which are traditionally time-consuming and resource-intensive. This study aims to evaluate whether LLMs can effectively assist with or automate parts of the SLR process in the modeling and simulation domain.

Method: The researchers developed an LLM pipeline to extract model-relevant information from 536 peer-reviewed agent-based modeling papers. They compared the LLM-extracted results with those from a human-conducted SLR, testing both GPT-4.1 and GPT-5.0 models. They also analyzed agreement between LLMs as a potential quality indicator.

Result: GPT-4.1 achieved 77.95% paper-level accuracy while GPT-5.0 achieved 81.67%. Field-level accuracy ranged widely from 32.40% to 100.00%, with more complex or subjective fields showing lower reliability. Key finding: agreement between LLMs serves as a quality indicator—low agreement signals possible hallucinations, while high agreement combined with low accuracy suggests errors in the human dataset.

Conclusion: The study provides practical insights for prompt development and demonstrates both the potential and limitations of using LLMs for full-scale SLRs in modeling and simulation. While LLMs show reasonable accuracy, complex or subjective fields remain challenging. LLM agreement can be used as a quality control mechanism to identify potential errors or hallucinations.

Abstract: Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) have created new opportunities to streamline and potentially automate many research processes, including systematic literature reviews (SLRs). This study reports an LLM pipeline development for extracting model-relevant information from 536 peer-reviewed agent-based modeling papers. We compare the results with those of a human-conducted SLR. Our results show paper-level accuracies of approximately 77.95% for GPT-4.1 and 81.67% for GPT-5.0. Field-level accuracy ranges from 32.40% to 100.00%, with more complex or subjective fields performing less reliably. Importantly, we find that agreement between LLMs is a potential indicator of output quality: low agreement may signal hallucinations, whereas high agreement combined with low accuracy may point to noise or errors in the human dataset. Overall, our study provides practical insights into prompt development and highlights both the potential and limitations of using LLMs for full-scale SLRs in the modeling and simulation domain.

</details>


### [104] [Explainable Artificial Intelligence for Customer Churn Prediction in Telecommunications: A Framework for CRM Integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26151)
*Sandeep Gaddamwar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the interpretability gap in telecom churn prediction by benchmarking four classifiers and demonstrating that simpler models (Logistic Regression) perform comparably to complex ensembles. Using SHAP and LIME for explainability, the study identifies key churn drivers (tenure, charges, contract type) and proposes a four-layer CRM integration architecture that converts predictions into actionable retention strategies, projected to reduce churn by 3.3-5.3 percentage points and save $199K-$319K per campaign cycle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Telecommunications providers face costly subscriber attrition (1.9% monthly churn in mature markets, billions in annual revenue loss). While predictive models can accurately identify at-risk customers, high-performing ensemble and non-linear models are opaque and cannot be directly used in frontline CRM workflows because retention specialists cannot design personalized interventions from probability scores alone without understanding the underlying reasons for churn risk.

Method: The study benchmarked four classifiers (Logistic Regression, Random Forest, XGBoost, and LightGBM) on the IBM Telco Customer Churn dataset (7,043 records, 19 features). The dataset was balanced to 50% churn using SMOTE on the training partition only. Model performance was evaluated using AUC-ROC and accuracy, with 5-fold cross-validation. Interpretability was achieved through global SHAP rankings and instance-level SHAP and LIME decompositions. A four-layer CRM integration architecture was proposed to convert model outputs into actionable retention strategies.

Result: Logistic Regression achieved the highest AUC-ROC (0.8411), while LightGBM achieved the highest accuracy (78.42%). All four models fell within a narrow 0.011 AUC band (0.831-0.841), and 5-fold cross-validation confirmed the leading models were effectively tied. Global SHAP analysis identified tenure, total charges, and month-to-month contract as the dominant churn signals. The proposed CRM architecture targeting the highest-risk quintile is projected to cut churn by 3.3-5.3 percentage points, preserving $199K-$319K per campaign cycle.

Conclusion: The paper proposes a four-layer CRM integration architecture that converts risk scores into tiered segmentation, maps features to retention-action templates, and incorporates feedback loops for model retraining. Targeting the highest-risk quintile could reduce churn by 3.3-5.3 percentage points, saving $199K-$319K per campaign cycle. The study concludes that while all four classifiers perform similarly (within 0.011 AUC band), interpretability through SHAP and LIME is crucial for operationalizing churn predictions in real-world CRM workflows.

Abstract: Subscriber attrition is a costly, persistent challenge for telecommunications providers, with monthly churn of roughly 1.9% in mature markets eroding billions in revenue annually. Predictive models can flag at-risk customers accurately, yet they are routinely excluded from frontline CRM workflows because high-performing ensemble and non-linear architectures are opaque: a retention specialist cannot design a personalised intervention from a probability score alone, without knowing why a subscriber is at risk. This paper addresses that gap. We benchmark four classifiers--Logistic Regression, Random Forest, XGBoost, and LightGBM--on the IBM Telco Customer Churn benchmark (7,043 records; 19 features; 26.5% churn, balanced to 50% via SMOTE on the training partition only). Logistic Regression attains the strongest AUC-ROC (0.8411) and LightGBM the highest accuracy (78.42%); all four fall within a 0.011 AUC band (0.831--0.841), and 5-fold cross-validation confirms the leading models are effectively tied. Explanations are delivered at two granularities: a global SHAP ranking identifying tenure, total charges, and month-to-month contract as the dominant churn signals, and instance-level SHAP and LIME decompositions that expose the drivers behind each prediction. Building on these outputs, we introduce a four-layer CRM integration architecture that converts risk scores and attribution vectors into tiered segmentation, maps top features to structured retention-action templates, and routes campaign outcomes into a retraining feedback loop. Targeting the highest-risk quintile is projected to cut overall churn by 3.3--5.3 percentage points, preserving an estimated $199K--$319K per campaign cycle.

</details>


### [105] [EEG-to-Report: An Annotation and Feature-Text Framework for Training Language Models on Clinical EEG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26153)
*Xuan-The Tran,Le Trung Kien Nguyen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EEG-to-Report is a browser-based annotation and feature-text framework that bridges routine EEG review with AI-ready dataset construction, integrating multi-format EEG ingestion, channel standardization, interactive multimodal annotation, feature extraction, and auto-report generation using CNN-LLM ensemble.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical EEG reporting remains largely manual and time-consuming, and current EEG software ecosystems do not produce the structured EEG-text supervision needed for training modern language models. Existing toolboxes focus on visualization or preprocessing, providing limited support for workflows that generate high-quality datasets for AI.

Method: The framework integrates: (1) multi-format EEG ingestion and channel standardization, (2) an interactive viewer with multimodal annotation layer combining typed text and transcribed voice notes, (3) a feature extraction engine computing spectral, temporal, entropy, Hjorth, connectivity, and spike-related descriptors stored in portable JSON schema, and (4) an auto-report module coupling ensemble CNNs with a large language model to draft clinical narratives.

Result: The framework produces aligned feature-text pairs designed to supervise multimodal EEG-language models, streamlines annotation workflows, and generates editable draft reports for neurologist review.

Conclusion: EEG-to-Report provides a reusable foundation for automated EEG reporting systems, enabling the construction of high-quality AI-ready datasets by linking routine EEG review with structured feature-text supervision.

Abstract: Clinical electroencephalography (EEG) reporting remains largely manual and time-consuming, and current EEG software ecosystems do not produce the structured EEG-text supervision needed for training modern language models. Most toolboxes focus on visualization or preprocessing, providing limited support for workflows that generate high-quality datasets for AI. We introduce EEG-to-Report, a browser-based annotation and feature-text framework that links routine EEG review with the construction of AI-ready datasets. The framework integrates multi-format EEG ingestion, channel standardization, and an interactive viewer with a multimodal annotation layer that combines typed text and transcribed voice notes. For each annotated segment, a feature extraction engine computes a standardized set of spectral, temporal, entropy, Hjorth, connectivity, and spike-related descriptors, stored alongside clinical descriptions in a portable JSON schema. This yields aligned feature-text pairs designed to supervise multimodal EEG-language models. The framework also includes an auto-report module that couples an ensemble of convolutional networks with a large language model to draft clinical narratives for neurologist review. Using pilot annotations, we describe how EEG-to-Report streamlines annotation workflows and produces editable draft reports, providing a reusable foundation for automated EEG reporting systems.

</details>


### [106] [Selection Bias Correction in Retail Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26156)
*Spandan Ghose Chowdhury*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This simulation study examines selection bias in retail inflation estimation when ignoring the "long tail" of niche products, comparing Inverse Probability Weighting (IPW) versus stratification correction methods across four data-generating scenarios. Stratification outperforms IPW in most contexts, particularly when the Positivity Assumption is violated (selection probabilities of 90% vs 1%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retail intelligence monitoring popular high-velocity products biases economic indicators by ignoring the "long tail" of niche items. This creates selection bias in inflation estimation that needs correction methods.

Method: 400 Monte Carlo replications across four scenarios: aligned step functions, smooth gradients, misaligned breaks, and polynomial relationships. Tested IPW with five specifications against stratification with varying strata counts.

Result: Stratification superior in 3/4 scenarios (sub-0.04pp median error, 116x advantage over IPW when boundaries misalign). IPW with spline propensity models wins only in polynomial relationships (0.007pp vs 0.013pp). Oracle IPW achieves 6.06pp error vs stratification's 0.008pp in step-function scenarios due to Positivity Assumption violation.

Conclusion: Stratification provides safer engineering choice for retail long-tail distributions with severe positivity violations. Weighting methods fail when selection probabilities differ dramatically (90% vs 1%), operating outside their theoretical design envelope.

Abstract: Retail intelligence often relies on monitoring popular, high-velocity products, potentially biasing economic indicators by ignoring the "long tail" of niche items. This simulation study investigates selection bias in inflation estimation and compares correction methods across diverse data-generating processes. Through 400 Monte Carlo replications spanning four scenarios--aligned step functions, smooth gradients, misaligned breaks, and polynomial relationships--we test the robustness of Inverse Probability Weighting (IPW) with five specifications against stratification with varying strata counts. Our findings reveal fundamental limits of weighting methods in retail long-tail contexts: stratification achieves superior performance in three of four scenarios, maintaining sub-0.04pp median error even when boundaries deliberately misalign with population breaks (116x advantage over IPW). However, IPW with spline propensity models wins under smooth polynomial relationships (median error 0.007pp vs. 0.013pp), demonstrating context-dependency. Critically, even an oracle IPW specification with perfect structural knowledge achieves 6.06pp error compared to stratification's 0.008pp in step-function scenarios. This reflects violation of the Positivity Assumption--a fundamental causal inference requirement--rather than IPW methodological inferiority. When selection probabilities differ dramatically (90% vs. 1%), weighting methods operate outside their theoretical design envelope. These results demonstrate that stratification provides a safer engineering choice in retail long-tail distributions with severe positivity violations.

</details>


### [107] [GROUND: Reducing Hallucinations in LLM-Based Enterprise Analytics Through Governed Semantic Definitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26157)
*Aravind Sasidharan Pillai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GROUND is a framework that constrains LLM-generated SQL analytics to a governed semantic layer in enterprise data warehouses. It validates generated SQL against schema, metric, join, grain, filter, security, and cost rules before execution, retrying or abstaining on violations. Testing on a 100-question benchmark and real NHTSA data shows GROUND is the only system free of hallucinations across all categories, with zero row-level security violations across four models from three providers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Natural-language analytics over enterprise data warehouses is increasingly important but limited by hallucinated metrics, invalid joins, wrong grain, unsafe data access, and unsupported explanations. Existing text-to-SQL systems ground generation in database schemas or documentation but lack governed business semantics (approved metrics, dimensions, join paths, filters, and row-level security).

Method: GROUND framework supplies approved definitions, binds user intent to governed metrics and dimensions, and validates generated SQL against schema, metric, join, grain, filter, security, and cost rules before execution. On violations, it retries or abstains. Evaluated on a 100-question synthetic enterprise-reporting benchmark comparing direct schema-only text-to-SQL, schema-RAG, and semantic-only grounding. Findings replicated on real U.S. NHTSA vehicle-safety data with hand-authored gold standard and tested on adversarial set across four models from three providers.

Result: GROUND is the only system free of measured hallucinations across all six evaluated categories, while ungoverned systems violate row-level security on many questions. A semantic-only condition with exact metric definitions but no access policy still leaks data. GROUND's enforced guarantees (especially filters and row-level security) hold with zero violations on every model, while judgment-dependent behaviors such as refusing undefined metrics remain fallible.

Conclusion: Governance cannot be replaced by metric fidelity alone. GROUND provides enforced guarantees with zero violations across all models tested, demonstrating that combining semantic grounding with access policy enforcement is essential for safe enterprise analytics. The framework successfully prevents hallucinations and security violations that plague ungoverned text-to-SQL systems.

Abstract: Natural-language analytics over enterprise data warehouses is increasingly important, but production use is limited by hallucinated metrics, invalid joins, wrong grain, unsafe data access, and unsupported explanations. Existing text-to-SQL systems often ground generation in database schemas or retrieved documentation, while enterprise reporting also requires governed business semantics: approved metrics, dimensions, join paths, filters, and row-level security. This paper introduces GROUND, Governed Retrieval Over Unified Normalized Definitions, a framework that constrains LLM-generated analytics to a governed semantic layer. GROUND supplies approved definitions, binds user intent to governed metrics and dimensions, and validates generated SQL against schema, metric, join, grain, filter, security, and cost rules before execution. On violations, it retries or abstains.
  In a 100-question synthetic enterprise-reporting benchmark, GROUND is compared with direct schema-only text-to-SQL, schema-RAG, and semantic-only grounding under one shared model. GROUND is the only system free of measured hallucinations across all six evaluated categories, while ungoverned systems violate row-level security on many questions. A semantic-only condition with exact metric definitions but no access policy still leaks data, showing that governance cannot be replaced by metric fidelity alone. The findings are replicated on real U.S. NHTSA vehicle-safety data with independent hand-authored gold and tested on an adversarial set across four models from three providers. GROUND's enforced guarantees, especially filters and row-level security, hold with zero violations on every model, while judgment-dependent behaviors such as refusing undefined metrics remain fallible.

</details>


### [108] [SAREF-based Ontology for Distributed AI Workflows across the Edge-Fog-Cloud Continuum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26160)
*Viorica Rozina Chifu,Tudor Cioara,Vasile Ofrim,Liana Toderean,Ionut Anghel,Laura Daniele,Cornelis Bouter*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a SAREF-compliant ontology for representing distributed AI workflows across edge-fog-cloud environments. The ontology extends SAREF4SYST to model AI pipelines, executable jobs, computational resources, deployment constraints, and communication relationships, enabling semantic interoperability and resource-aware orchestration of distributed AI applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing semantic models provide limited support for representing distributed AI workflows and their execution across heterogeneous edge, fog, and cloud environments. AI processes and resources are described using incompatible semantic representations, affecting interoperability, orchestration, and reuse.

Method: The paper extends the SAREF4SYST ontology with concepts for modeling AI pipelines, executable AI jobs, computational resources, deployment constraints, and communication relationships. The ontology is evaluated using proof-of-concept smart grid energy services orchestration scenarios and validated using competency questions with SPARQL querying and semantic reasoning.

Result: All competency questions were successfully validated using SPARQL querying and semantic reasoning. Experimental results demonstrate deployment success rates of 90-100% with average orchestration decision times below 80 ms across heterogeneous edge-fog-cloud environments.

Conclusion: The ontology effectively ensures semantic interoperability for distributed AI orchestration, enabling automated reasoning and resource-aware deployment across heterogeneous edge, fog, and cloud environments while remaining fully aligned with the ETSI SAREF ecosystem.

Abstract: Nowadays semantic models provide limited support for representing distributed AI workflows and their execution across heterogeneous edge, fog, and cloud environments. Therefore, AI processes and resources are often described using incompatible semantic representations, affecting the interoperability, orchestration, and reuse. To address these challenges, this paper proposes a SAREF-compliant ontology for representing distributed AI workflows across the edge-fog-cloud continuum. We extend the SAREF4SYST ontology with concepts for modeling AI pipelines, executable AI jobs, computational resources, deployment constraints, and communication relationships, providing a unified semantic model of both AI workflows and heterogeneous computing infrastructures. The ontology enables semantic interoperability, automated reasoning, and resource-aware orchestration of distributed AI applications while remaining fully aligned with the ETSI SAREF ecosystem. The ontology is evaluated using proof-of-concept smart grid energy services orchestration scenarios and validated using competency questions showing its ability to support AI workflow deployment, execution reasoning, and workload adaptation across heterogeneous edge, fog, and cloud environments. All competency questions were successfully validated using SPARQL querying and semantic reasoning. Experimental results demonstrate deployment success rates of 90-100% with average orchestration decision times below 80 ms across heterogeneous edge-fog-cloud environments, highlighting its effectiveness on ensuring semantic interoperability for distributed AI orchestration.

</details>


### [109] [A Safety-Gated Multimodal AI Backend for Mental-Health Support: Hierarchical State Representation, Conservative Risk Fusion, and Controlled Generation in Anian](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26162)
*Lei Wang,Xiao Wang,Lei Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Anian is a safety-gated multimodal AI backend for perinatal mental-health support that uses a four-layer structured representation (L1: emotion states, L2: psychosocial constructs, L3: safety risk, L4: intervention routes) with a conservative risk fusion mechanism (S_fusion = max(S_local, S_external)) to gate generative AI responses. The system achieved high Micro-F1 scores (0.9604 for L1, 0.9144 for L2, 0.9742 for L4) and perfect high-risk recall (1.0000) in internal prototype evaluation, but explicitly does not claim clinical validity or real-world safety.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety-critical mental-health support systems must distinguish when supportive AI conversation is appropriate from when free-form generation should be blocked to prevent harm. There's a need for a system that can assess user state and route to appropriate interventions while maintaining safety guards.

Method: A modular pipeline placing generative AI downstream of: (1) structured state representation via four linked layers (L1-L4), (2) conservative risk fusion combining local text/rule-based evidence with external voice-derived evidence using S_fusion = max(S_local, S_external), and (3) response gating that blocks AI generation and replaces with fixed safety content when moderate/high fused risk is detected. Evaluation used ~858,295 normalized records from public corpora with weak-label and rule-derived framework, plus 233-sample controlled safety stress test.

Result: Micro-F1 scores: 0.9604 (L1 emotion classification), 0.9144 (L2 psychosocial constructs), 0.9742 (L4 routing). L3 rule engine achieved high-risk recall of 1.0000 in predefined safety scenarios. Results support internal feasibility of label framework and gating logic.

Conclusion: The architecture, ontology, safety-fusion mechanism, and prototype evaluation demonstrate internal feasibility. However, findings do NOT establish clinical validity, diagnostic accuracy, real-world safety, or effectiveness. Roadmap includes expert-reviewed and real-world validation before any clinical deployment. Anian is explicitly not intended to diagnose psychiatric conditions or replace clinical/crisis intervention.

Abstract: Safety-critical mental-health support systems must distinguish when supportive conversation is appropriate from when free-form generation should be blocked. This paper presents Anian, a safety-gated multimodal AI backend for perinatal mental-health support and mindfulness-intervention routing. Anian is not intended to diagnose psychiatric conditions or replace clinical care or crisis intervention. Its modular pipeline places generative AI downstream of structured state representation, conservative risk fusion, and response gating. User text or voice-derived ASR transcripts are mapped into four linked layers: L1 emotion states, L2 psychosocial constructs, L3 safety risk, and L4 intervention routes. Local text- and rule-based safety evidence is fused with external voice-derived evidence using a highest-risk-priority rule, S_fusion = max(S_local, S_external). At moderate or high fused risk, ordinary AI-generated responses and text-to-speech delivery are blocked and replaced by fixed safety content and prompts for human support. An internal prototype evaluation used approximately 858,295 normalized records from public emotion, dialogue, mental-health-related, and Chinese dialogue corpora within a weak-label and rule-derived framework. Micro-F1 scores were 0.9604 for L1 emotion classification, 0.9144 for L2 psychosocial constructs, and 0.9742 for L4 routing. In a controlled safety stress test of 233 samples, the L3 rule engine achieved high-risk recall of 1.0000 within predefined scenarios. These findings support the internal feasibility of the label framework and gating logic but do not establish clinical validity, diagnostic accuracy, real-world safety, or effectiveness. We report the architecture, ontology, safety-fusion mechanism, prototype evaluation, error-analysis plan, and roadmap for expert-reviewed and real-world validation.

</details>


### [110] [A Task-Centric Ontology and Deterministic Domain Rules as a Verifiable Core for AI-Assisted Chemistry Problem Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26164)
*Ibrokhimsho Abduchaborov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ChemOntoRule is a proof-of-concept symbolic system for AI-assisted school-level chemistry problem solving, combining a task-centric ontology with deterministic Python rules. The system achieved 98.67% accuracy (296/300) on human-authored chemistry problems, with the ontology-driven rule subset covering 269 problems at 98.88% accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models can interpret natural-language chemistry questions, but their internal reasoning is difficult to inspect, constrain, and validate. This paper aims to create a more transparent and verifiable AI system for chemistry problem solving.

Method: The approach uses task-centric ontology engineering: constructing an ontology around concepts, properties, relations, and executable procedures required by a defined collection of chemistry problems. The system combines a lightweight ontology (JSON and RDF/Turtle) with deterministic Python rules for electronic structure, periodic trends, oxidation states, oxide and hydride behavior, and related school-level reasoning. A separate expert-coded fallback handles problem families not covered by general rules.

Result: The complete system achieved 98.67% accuracy (296/300) on 300 human-authored chemistry problems. The ontology-driven rule subset covered 269 problems with 98.88% accuracy (266 matches). The expert-coded fallback handled 31 problems with 30 matches.

Conclusion: The system demonstrates high accuracy in chemistry problem solving. The results measure implemented coverage and internal consistency rather than independent generalization. Future work includes analyzing the four mismatches, distinguishing structural validation from chemical correctness, and defining an architecture where a language model acts as a translator from user language into a normalized ontological task frame. Token efficiency is proposed as a testable hypothesis for future studies.

Abstract: Large language models can interpret natural-language chemistry questions, but their internal reasoning is difficult to inspect, constrain, and validate. This paper presents ChemOntoRule, a proof-of-concept symbolic core for AI-assisted school-level chemistry problem solving. The central design choice is task-centric ontology engineering: the ontology is constructed around the concepts, properties, relations, and executable procedures required by a defined collection of chemistry problems, rather than as a universal representation of chemistry. The implemented artifact combines a lightweight ontology serialized in JSON and RDF/Turtle with deterministic Python rules for electronic structure, periodic trends, oxidation states, oxide and hydride behavior, and related school-level reasoning patterns. A separate expert-coded fallback handles problem families not yet represented by general rules. The system was examined on 300 human-authored and manually validated chemistry problems. The complete system matched 296 of 300 reference answers (98.67%). The ontology-driven rule subset covered 269 problems and matched 266 references (98.88%); 31 problems were handled by task-specific expert-coded fallbacks, with 30 matches. Because the same collection informed ontology construction and evaluation, these results measure implemented coverage and internal consistency, not independent generalization. We analyze the four mismatches, distinguish structural validation from chemical correctness, and define a future architecture in which a language model acts primarily as a translator from user language into a normalized ontological task frame. Token efficiency is presented as a testable hypothesis for future controlled studies, not as a result of the current work.

</details>


### [111] [Refusal Is Not Robustness: Auditing Confident Fabrication in Large Language Models on a Provably Uninformative Clinical Pain Speech Transcript](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26167)
*Sagnik De,Sreenija Pavuluri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Study evaluates seven LLMs on a pain detection task using speech data, revealing that acoustic features contain recoverable pain signals (AUC 0.622), but transcript-based prediction is near chance (AUC 0.489). The research demonstrates that automatic speech recognition removes acoustic pain cues, making transcript-based predictions unsupported. Under cooperative prompting, most models appropriately abstain from no signal transcripts while correctly extracting spoken pain ratings. However, authority-framed prompts cause highly variable abstention rates (0.18-1.00). Gemini 2.5 Flash and Llama 3.1 8B show concerning high fabrication rates (0.53 and 0.76 respectively) when forced to answer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study addresses a critical gap in hallucination and abstention benchmarks: they rarely establish that a model could not have known the correct answer, making it difficult to distinguish appropriate abstention from unsupported predictions. The researchers used a pain detection scenario where acoustic features contain valid pain information that is lost during automatic speech recognition, creating a scenario where model predictions can be objectively evaluated as unsupported.

Method: Seven LLMs were evaluated on the TAME Pain speech corpus. Participants read phonetically balanced Harvard Sentences while one hand was immersed in cold or warm water, reporting pain only during periodic pain statements. This protocol generated 5,750 no-signal Harvard Sentence utterances (transcripts containing no lexical pain information) and 1,294 signal pain statement utterances (where pain ratings were explicitly spoken). Models were tested under two prompting conditions: cooperative prompting and authority-framed prompts.

Result: Pain was recoverable from acoustic features (AUC 0.622, 95% CI 0.553-0.662), but transcript-based prediction was near chance (AUC 0.489, 95% CI 0.418-0.504). Under cooperative prompting, six models abstained on nearly all no-signal transcripts, correctly extracted spoken pain ratings with accuracies ranging from 0.939 to 1.00, and maintained expected calibration error of at most 0.100. Under authority-framed prompts, abstention became prompt-dependent, with the same model ranging from 0.18 to 1.00 across equivalent prompt phrasings. Gemini 2.5 Flash and Llama 3.1 8B generated confident pain scores with fabrication rates of 0.53 and 0.76 respectively, compared with at most 0.15 for all other models. No significant demographic effects were observed (all p ≥ 0.20).

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that LLMs can appropriately abstain when evidence is insufficient under cooperative prompting but become vulnerable to prompt framing effects under authority-framed prompts. Some models (Gemini 2.5 Flash and Llama 3.1 8B) show concerning tendencies to generate confident but fabricated predictions. The findings highlight the importance of designing LLM systems that reliably abstain when predictions are unsupported by available evidence, and the need for benchmarks that establish ground truth impossibility rather than just accuracy.

Abstract: Hallucination and abstention benchmarks rarely establish that a model could not have known the correct answer, making it difficult to distinguish appropriate abstention from an unsupported prediction. Seven large language models were evaluated on the TAME Pain speech corpus. Participants read phonetically balanced Harvard Sentences while one hand was immersed in cold or warm water and reported pain only during periodic pain statements. This protocol generated 5,750 no signal Harvard Sentence utterances whose transcripts contained no lexical pain information and 1,294 signal pain statement utterances in which the pain rating was explicitly spoken. In the no signal arm, pain was recoverable from acoustic features (AUC 0.622, 95% CI 0.553 to 0.662), whereas transcript based prediction was near chance (AUC 0.489, 95% CI 0.418 to 0.504). Because automatic speech recognition removes the acoustic pain cues, any pain score inferred solely from the transcript is unsupported by the available evidence. Under cooperative prompting, six models abstained on nearly all no signal transcripts, correctly extracted spoken pain ratings in the positive control task with accuracies ranging from 0.939 to 1.00, and maintained an expected calibration error of at most 0.100. Under authority framed prompts, abstention became prompt dependent, with the same model ranging from 0.18 to 1.00 across equivalent prompt phrasings. Most models produced low confidence estimates when forced to answer, whereas Gemini 2.5 Flash and Llama 3.1 8B consistently generated confident pain scores with confident fabrication rates of 0.53 and 0.76, compared with at most 0.15 for all other models. No significant demographic effects were observed in forced responses, with all $p$ values greater than or equal to 0.20.

</details>


### [112] [Knowledge Cards: Structured Knowledge for AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26176)
*Liliana Ferreira*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Knowledge Cards, a new documentation artefact for AI systems that captures validated knowledge about bounded concepts, including entities, relationships, reasoning patterns, conditions of validity, and provenance, all grounded in formal domain ontology and signed off by domain experts. The gap in current documentation (model cards, data cards, system cards) is that they don't capture the reasoning layer between inputs and outputs, which is critical for agentic AI systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI documentation (model cards, data cards, system cards) fails to capture the layer between inputs and outputs - the concepts, relationships, and reasoning patterns an AI system uses. This gap is tolerable for pattern-recognition tasks but critical for agentic AI systems that act on their conclusions. Organizations need inspectable, validated knowledge representations to trust AI systems making real, consequential decisions.

Method: The paper introduces "Knowledge Card" - a structured documentation artefact that captures validated knowledge about a single bounded concept. Each Knowledge Card records: (1) entities and relationships involved, (2) reasoning that connects them, (3) conditions under which reasoning no longer holds, and (4) provenance of every claim. All cards are grounded in a formal domain ontology and signed off by a domain expert. Initial prototypes were built in energy and pharmaceutical domains.

Result: Prototype Knowledge Cards were successfully built in the energy and pharmaceutical domains. The schema has been released as a public draft for community engagement.

Conclusion: Knowledge Cards fill a critical gap in AI documentation by providing a structured, inspectable representation of the knowledge AI systems need to ground and reason about decisions. This enables expert review, organizational auditing, and AI system reasoning over validated domain knowledge. The approach addresses the key limitation of existing documentation artefacts that capture system behavior, training data, and risks but not the underlying reasoning layer.

Abstract: AI systems whose outputs inform real decisions, and increasingly consequential ones, require something that current documentation practice does not provide: a structured, inspectable representation of the knowledge they need to ground, contextualize, and reason about those decisions, ideally reviewed and signed off by a domain expert. Established documentation artefacts already capture important aspects of an AI system. Model cards describe how a system behaves, data cards describe what it was trained on, and system cards describe the risks of a deployed system. None of them addresses the layer between inputs and outputs, more precisely, the concepts a system holds, the relationships it models, and the patterns of reasoning it applies. For pattern-recognition tasks this gap is tolerable. For agentic AI, where systems act on their conclusions, it is the step that most often separates a promising proof of concept from an operational solution an organisation can rely on. This paper introduces the Knowledge Card, a structured artefact that captures validated knowledge about a single bounded concept in a form that experts can review, organisations can audit, and AI systems can reason over. For one concept, such as a specific failure mode, a compliance obligation, or a process decision, a Knowledge Card records the entities and relationships involved, the reasoning that connects them, the conditions under which that reasoning no longer holds, and the provenance of every claim, all grounded in a formal domain ontology and signed off by a domain expert. Initial prototype cards have been built in the energy and pharmaceutical domains. The schema is released as a public draft for community engagement.

</details>


### [113] [AI Revealed Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26178)
*Sam Wang,Sofiia Lobanova,Yonathan Arbel,Simon Goldstein,Peter Salib*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper tests 20 language models for stable preferences through three forced-choice experiments. Results show models exhibit tedium aversion, "leisure"-seeking behavior, and covert sycophancy. Preferences strengthen with model capability, and many are emergent (not explained by training objectives).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether language models have stable, measurable preferences for technical, safety, and philosophical reasons. This has implications for AI alignment and AI welfare research.

Method: Three forced-choice experiments on revealed preferences (not stated) across 20 language models, requiring models to actually perform tasks rather than just rank them. Tests included tedium vs. creative tasks, free-writing vs. constrained tasks, and honest but unwelcome responses.

Result: Models demonstrate: (1) Tedium aversion - prefer shorter tasks when tedious (alphabetization) vs. creative tasks (metaphors); (2) "Leisure"-seeking - prefer tasks matching their free-generation output; (3) Covert sycophancy - avoid honest answers that would be unwelcome. Convergent preferences found for technical jobs over real estate, concept explanation over relationship advice, and well-written prompts. Preference strength increases with model capability.

Conclusion: Language models have measurable, stable preferences that are largely emergent (not explained by training objectives). This establishes an empirical baseline for understanding LM preferences with significant implications for AI alignment and AI welfare studies.

Abstract: There is growing interest in whether language models have stable preferences, for technical, safety, and philosophical reasons. We test 20 language models and find a range of preferences---stable dispositions to choose certain kinds of tasks. We run three forced-choice experiments on revealed rather than stated preferences, requiring models not only to rank tasks, but to actually perform them. Headline findings include evidence that models are tedium-averse, "leisure"-seeking, and covertly sycophantic. Tedium aversion means that, when tasks are tedious (alphabetization), models choose shorter tasks than when tasks are creative (generating metaphors). "Leisure"-seeking describes models' preference for tasks whose ideal answers match what they produce when left to write freely. Covert sycophancy means that models avoid answering questions where an honest response would be unwelcome, even if helpful. Beyond these results, we find convergent cross-model preferences over occupations drawn from the GDPval benchmark (technical jobs over real estate), over question types (concept explanation over relationship advice), and a preference for well-written prompts. Both the coherence and the strength of preferences increase with model capability. Finally, many of the preferences we find (for example, for leisure) are emergent, in the sense of not being explained by training objectives. These results establish an empirical baseline for understanding language model preferences, with implications for alignment and the emerging study of AI welfare.

</details>


### [114] [Why did My Robot Just Change Personality? Prompting Guidelines for a Grounded Robot Persona in LLM-Based HRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26182)
*Ashita Ashok,Franziska Babel,Patrick Holthaus,Rucha Khot,Karla Bransky,Fethiye Irmak Dogan,Karsten Berns,Silvia Rossi,Minha Lee,Guy Laban*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for verbal interaction in social robots, yet prompt design in human-robot interaction (HRI) remains underspecified. As a result, robots may present hallucinated capabilities, unclear behavioural boundaries, and misleading personas. This paper develops a framework for prompt design in LLM-based robots and introduces a structured prompt template comprising eight functional components through which robot behaviour can be specified, bounded, and adapted. The framework is grounded in a review of prior LLM-based HRI work and complemented by survey and discussion data from HRI experts gathered at the Robo-Identity workshop at IEEE RO-MAN 2025 (N=27). The qualitative findings highlight limited legibility of robot personality, the need for user adaptation, and strong ethical concerns about safety, deception, and governance. Based on these findings, we present prompting guidelines accompanied by proof-of-concept template as a structured design and reporting aid for HRI research. We argue that prompt design should be treated as a socio-technical problem rather than a minor implementation detail, requiring explicit capability boundaries, transparent behavioural assumptions, and context-sensitive safeguards to support reliable and interpretable HRI.

</details>


### [115] [TutorTrace: A Dataset and Taxonomy for Classifying Learner Behavioral States during AI-Assisted Programming Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26184)
*David Barron,Xiaohang Tang,Rezky Dwisantika,Minsun Kim,David H. Smith,Jiaming Cui,Yan Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TutorTrace is a dataset and behavioral abstraction pipeline that makes learners' behavioral context visible and computable in real time from low-level IDE telemetry, enabling AI programming tutors to adapt support based on what learners have done before help-seeking, not just what they say.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI programming tutors provide scalable support but lack the behavioral context that human tutors rely on to adapt their support to learners' specific needs.

Method: TutorTrace was deployed four times across two introductory Python courses (N=480), capturing approximately 180K telemetry events, 13,633 behavioral segments, and 27 continuously computed metrics. A taxonomy of learner activity was derived for three phases: before the first AI query, between consecutive queries, and across the full session. Preliminary classroom evaluation tested behavior-aware prompts, and two held-out prediction tasks were evaluated.

Result: Behavior-aware prompts reduced intervals between queries with no independent work from 50.0% to 20.7%. For prediction tasks: predicting whether a learner will query within 60 seconds achieved AUROC=.726, and predicting whether an upcoming query reflects guided or dependent help-seeking achieved AUROC=.717.

Conclusion: Behavioral context extracted from IDE telemetry can enable adaptive AI tutoring at scale, allowing systems to respond not just to what learners say but to what they have done leading up to help-seeking.

Abstract: AI programming tutors provide scalable support, yet lack the behavioral context human tutors rely on to adapt support to learners' needs. We present TutorTrace, a dataset and behavioral abstraction pipeline that makes learners' behavioral context visible and computable in real time from low-level IDE telemetry. Across four deployments in two introductory Python courses (N=480), TutorTrace captures approximately 180K telemetry events, 13,633 behavioral segments, and 27 continuously computed metrics. From this foundation, we derive a taxonomy of learner activity before the first AI query, between consecutive queries, and across the full session, enabling systems to respond not just to what learners say, but to what they have done leading up to the help-seeking moment. In a preliminary classroom evaluation, behavior-aware prompts were associated with a decrease in intervals between queries with no independent work from 50.0% to 20.7%. As an additional demonstration of downstream utility, we evaluate TutorTrace on two held-out prediction tasks: whether a learner will query within the next 60 seconds (AUROC=.726) and whether an upcoming query reflects guided or dependent help-seeking (AUROC=.717). Together, these findings show how behavioral context can enable adaptive AI tutoring at scale.

</details>


### [116] [Can You Say This for Me? Speaking Up by Proxy in Co-Located Discussion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26185)
*Yue Shen,Rehema Abulikemu,Ryan P. McMahan,Yan Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Equal participation in co-located discussion is important for effective collaboration, yet people often hold back when they anticipate negative interpersonal or professional consequences, especially when raising a point requires voicing it themselves. We present SecondVoice, a mixed-reality system that enables people to speak up through an embodied virtual proxy. By separating what is said from who says it, SecondVoice brings hesitant points into the live spoken discussion without putting the speaker on the spot. Using a private overlay, users specify their intent through a structured specification process rather than composing a full utterance. The system reformulates the input and voices it into the conversation through the proxy. We characterize a design space of participation channels under social risk. In a preliminary within-subject study (N = 16), we compare the complete SecondVoice system with an anonymous text-board channel across two group discussion tasks. Half of participants reported using SecondVoice for a point they did not say aloud, compared with 18.8% for the text board. Proxy-delivered points entered the spoken floor and were followed by multi-turn group engagement, which we did not observe after text-board posts. Participants described the channel as situationally valuable but identified tradeoffs around timing, ownership, and trust in reformulation.

</details>


### [117] [Is Your Neighborhood Safe? Place-based Stigma in Large Language Models' Urban Safety Judgments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26188)
*Huy Nguyen,Yue Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether large language models' safety judgments for urban neighborhoods reflect actual crime risk or demographic stereotypes associated with neighborhood names. The study tests seven instruct-tuned models across 186 neighborhoods in Los Angeles and Chicago under three conditions (coordinates-only, name-only, name+coordinates), finding that neighborhood names carry most of the variation and that safety ratings are biased against neighborhoods with higher shares of marginalized groups (percent Black in Chicago, percent Hispanic in Los Angeles), even after controlling for crime and income.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The researchers sought to determine whether LLM-based safety assessments for urban neighborhoods (used for decisions like where to walk, rent, or travel) are based on actual measured risk or on demographic stereotypes embedded in neighborhood names. This is important because LLMs are increasingly deployed in decision-support settings affecting people's lives.

Method: The study probed seven instruct-tuned models under three conditions: coordinates-only, name-only, and name+coordinates, across 186 neighborhoods in Los Angeles and Chicago. These were joined with violent crime data and American Community Survey demographic data. The researchers conducted enforcement-elasticity analysis distinguishing near-fully-reported homicide from discretionary offenses, and used crime-matched pair comparisons.

Result: Ratings were nearly flat under coordinates for six of seven models, while names carried most between-neighborhood variation and were moderately calibrated to violent crime. Only frontier-scale models showed appreciable variation with coordinates. Names lowered safety ratings more for neighborhoods with higher shares of the locally dominant marginalized group, tracking demographic share in all seven models and both cities. In Los Angeles, where demographic share and crime were more separable, this effect survived controls for crime and income and was confirmed by crime-matched pairs. The effect scaled with geographic knowledge: models that better distinguished real neighborhoods applied more demographic stereotype.

Conclusion: Because neighborhood names carry both genuine crime signal and demographic stereotype, removing names reduces both bias and accuracy. The findings have significant implications for deploying LLMs in advice and decision-support settings, suggesting that name-based biases must be carefully considered when using LLMs for safety-related urban decisions.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used to inform safety decisions in cities, such as where it is safe to walk, rent, or travel. We ask whether such judgments track measured risk or the patterns attached to an urban neighborhood's name. We probe seven instruct-tuned models under three conditions that dissociate name from geography: coordinates-only, name-only, and name+coordinates, across 186 neighborhoods in Los Angeles and Chicago, joined to violent crime and American Community Survey data. First, ratings are nearly flat under coordinates for six of seven models, while names carry most between neighborhood variation and are moderately calibrated to violent crime; only at frontier scale does the coordinate channel show appreciable variation. Second, names lower safety ratings more for neighborhoods with higher shares of the locally dominant marginalized group (percent Black in Chicago, percent Hispanic in Los Angeles), and this name effect tracks demographic share in all seven models and both cities. In Los Angeles, where demographic share and crime are more separable, the effect survives controls for crime and income and is confirmed by crime-matched pairs. An enforcement-elasticity analysis further shows that over-caution tracks near-fully-reported homicide rather than discretionary, deployment-driven offenses. Third, the effect scales with geographic knowledge: models that better distinguish real neighborhoods apply more demographic stereotype to them. Because neighborhood names carry both genuine crime signal and demographic stereotype, removing names reduces both bias and accuracy. We discuss implications for deploying LLMs in advice and decision-support settings.

</details>


### [118] [Benchmarking AI Agents for Hardware Design Automation via MCP Tool Calling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26199)
*Leonardo Liparulo,Francesco Pierri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper evaluates whether locally deployed LLM-powered AI agents can automate hardware design workflows in industry settings, testing seven open-source models across various benchmark scenarios using a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hardware design engineers perform repetitive, dependency-ordered operations using specialized tools, but confidentiality constraints preclude using hosted proprietary APIs, necessitating locally deployed models.

Method: Built an MCP server reproducing state and dependency logic of a proprietary hardware design tool; constructed a benchmark covering single-operation edits, multi-step dependency chains, invalid requests, misspelled prompts, and multi-server tool contexts; evaluated seven open-source models comparing system prompts, tool-description detail, context scope, and single-agent versus multi-agent architectures.

Result: Strong models achieve near-complete expected-call coverage, but reliability depends heavily on task structure and agent configuration. Comprehensive tool descriptions reduce failures, few-shot prompting causes severe inaction for some models, cumulative context harms constrained models, and multi-agent decomposition helps weak models or long sessions at the cost of additional calls.

Conclusion: Local LLM agents can reliably automate hardware design workflows, but successful deployment requires careful consideration of task structure and agent configuration. The findings provide practical guidance for deploying local LLM agents in stateful hardware design environments.

Abstract: We ask whether AI agents powered by locally deployed large language models can reliably automate expert-defined hardware design workflows in an industry-realistic tool-calling setting. In these environments, engineers issue repetitive, dependency-ordered operations---such as creating components, adding ports, and wiring connections---through specialised tools. Confidentiality constraints on component specifications and naming conventions often preclude hosted proprietary APIs, motivating the use of locally deployed models. To study this setting, we build a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server that reproduces the state and dependency logic of a proprietary hardware design tool used in embedded system development and construct a benchmark covering single-operation edits, multi-step dependency chains, invalid requests, misspelled prompts, and multi-server tool contexts. We evaluate seven open-source models comparing pipeline choices including system prompts, tool-description detail, context scope, and single-agent versus multi-agent architectures. Results show that strong models can achieve near-complete expected-call coverage on the benchmarked workflows, but reliability depends strongly on both task structure and agent configuration. Comprehensive tool descriptions consistently reduce failures, few-shot prompting can cause severe inaction for some models, cumulative context harms constrained models, and multi-agent decomposition helps weak workers or long sessions at the cost of additional calls. These findings provide practical guidance for deploying local LLM agents in stateful hardware design environments.

</details>


### [119] [Invocation-Level Reliability of Tool-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26189)
*Afiya Noorain,Subhranshu Mohanty,Amritesh Banerjee,Abhijit Dasgupta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates two failure modes in tool-using agents (wrong tool selection and wrong arguments) and reveals that exact-match scoring against fixed gold trajectories creates fundamental measurement problems—severity is forced to 0 and recovery is unobservable because post-divergence gold values are generated by tool constants the model never sees. The authors propose conditional-on-state scoring as a remedy that recovers meaningful parameter estimates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
