We present PILOT, a supervisor-worker harness for live self-improvement through two coupled mechanisms: (1) live steering lets a separate supervisor redirect or abort the active worker during execution; and (2) live self-evolution distils procedures and failure modes revealed during execution into reusable skills and memory. Across two frozen backbones and three benchmarks, PILOT ranks first in five of six configurations. On Terminal-Bench 2.0, PILOT outperforms counterpart harnesses by up to 9.8 percentage points. In the self-improvement setting, PILOT gains 14.6 points with GLM-5.1 and 12.4 points with Kimi-K2.6. Mean output tokens fall by 42.9% and 47.4%, while successful evaluations per million output tokens rise by 110.3% and 134.0%, respectively.

</details>


### [142] [Multi2AV-Safety: Benchmarking Safety in Multimodal-to-Audio-Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26535)
*Kaichao Jiang,Changtao Miao,Baiqi Wu,Zhiyuan Lu,Kang Yang,Peiwei Zhao,Junchi Chen,Yunfeng Diao,He Liu,Qi Chu,Tao Gong,Nenghai Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Multi2AV-Safety, the first safety benchmark for multimodal-conditioned audio-video generation that covers all 11 non-singleton text/image/audio/video conditioning configurations with 11,024 attack instances. The benchmark reveals that current safety guards fail to perceive compositional risks where harmful intent emerges from the interaction of benign or weakly harmful inputs across modalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Audio-video generation is shifting from prompt-driven to multimodal conditioning, where harmful intent may emerge from how benign inputs interact across modalities. Existing safety benchmarks remain prompt-centric and fail to address these compositional risks systematically.

Method: The authors created a comprehensive benchmark covering all 11 non-singleton T/I/A/V conditioning configurations and evaluated representative multimodal safety guards across different attack mechanisms and harm-evidence structures.

Result: Two complementary failure modes were identified: (1) harmful semantics can emerge from the combination of individually benign inputs, and (2) explicit harmful cues become harder to detect when mixed with benign multimodal context. Current safety guards fail to reliably integrate safety evidence across modalities and time.

Conclusion: Compositional risk perception is identified as a central capability gap in safeguarding multimodal-conditioned audio-video generation. The dataset will be publicly released in October 2026.

Abstract: Audio-video generation is rapidly moving from prompt-driven synthesis toward multimodal conditioning, where text, images, audio, and video can jointly shape the generated output. This shift changes the nature of safety evaluation: harmful intent may no longer reside in any single input, but instead emerge from how otherwise benign or weakly harmful conditions interact across modalities and time. Existing safety benchmarks, however, remain largely prompt-centric or tied to fixed conditioning interfaces, leaving such compositional risks difficult to study systematically. To bridge this gap, we introduce Multi2AV-Safety, the first safety benchmark, to the best of our knowledge, to cover all 11 non-singleton T/I/A/V conditioning configurations for audio-video generation, comprising 11,024 attack instances. Evaluation on Multi2AV-Safety reveals systematic weaknesses in representative multimodal safety guards across attack mechanisms and harm-evidence structures. Our evaluation reveals two complementary failure modes: harmful semantics can emerge from the combination of individually benign inputs, while explicit harmful cues can become harder to detect when mixed with benign multimodal context. Together, these results identify \emph{compositional risk perception} as a central capability gap in safeguarding multimodal-conditioned audio-video generation: current safety guards fail to reliably integrate safety evidence across modalities and time, even when all conditioning inputs are observable. The dataset will be publicly released in October 2026.

</details>


### [143] [DuMateBench: Evaluating Autonomous Agents in Complex Real-World Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26546)
*Zechun Niu,Yukun Zhao,Jiaxin Zhang,Xu Shen,Jinhua Si,Han Tian,Can Xu,Yunfan Song,Jiaxin Mao,Yansong Gao,Yuchen Li,Jianmin Wu,Lingyong Yan,Shuaiqiang Wang,Dawei Yin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DuMateBench is a real-session benchmark reconstructed from production agent platform data, comprising 200 tasks across 8 scenarios and 17 capability categories. It tests autonomous agents under three real-world environmental complexities (Insufficient, Unstable, Noisy) using hybrid evaluation. Experiments across 5 agent frameworks and 4 LLMs reveal substantial performance gaps, with robustness jointly shaped by LLM capabilities and agent framework design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks evaluate autonomous agents in cleaner, more stable environments than real-world settings, and typically separate tasks by application or capability. This creates a gap between benchmark performance and actual deployment effectiveness.

Method: Reconstructed 200 tasks from anonymized, privacy-screened user sessions from a production agent platform, preserving interaction history, persistent configurations, and workspace state. Tasks validated through human verification. Executed in isolated Docker containers with three environmental perturbations: Insufficient, Unstable, and Noisy. Assessed using hybrid deterministic and LLM-as-Judge evaluation. Tested 5 autonomous-agent frameworks paired with 4 state-of-the-art LLMs.

Result: Experiments reveal substantial gaps in strict task completion. Performance under environmental perturbations is jointly shaped by LLM capabilities and the surrounding agent framework. Complementary robustness, efficiency, and diagnostic analyses support these findings.

Conclusion: The DuMateBench benchmark successfully exposes real-world limitations of current autonomous agent systems. Performance depends on the interaction between LLM capabilities and agent framework design, suggesting both components need improvement for robust real-world deployment.

Abstract: Autonomous agents are increasingly adopted to complete complex, multi-tool workflows in real-world settings. However, existing benchmarks typically separate tasks by application or capability and evaluate agents in environments that are cleaner and more stable than those encountered in practice. We introduce DuMateBench, a real-session benchmark reconstructed from anonymized and privacy-screened user sessions collected from a large-scale production agent platform. Each task preserves the relevant pre-solution interaction history, persistent configurations, and workspace state, and is then validated through human verification. The resulting benchmark comprises 200 tasks spanning 8 broad scenarios and 17 fine-grained capability categories, with most tasks requiring multiple capability coordination. We execute these tasks in isolated Docker containers injected with three forms of real-world environmental complexity: Insufficient, Unstable, and Noisy, and assess performance using a hybrid deterministic and LLM-as-Judge evaluation protocol. Experiments across five representative autonomous-agent frameworks paired with four state-of-the-art LLMs reveal substantial gaps in strict task completion. Complementary robustness, efficiency, and diagnostic analyses further show that performance under environmental perturbations is jointly shaped by the capabilities of the LLM and the surrounding agent framework. The code and data are publicly available at https://dumatebench.com/.

</details>


### [144] [AgentJudgeBench: A Multi-Difficulty Benchmark for Evaluating LLM Judges on Agentic Tool-Calling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26623)
*Abhigya Verma,Amit Kumar Saha,Seganrasan Subramanian,Sai Harshitha Aluru*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: First benchmark (AgentJudgeBench) systematically evaluating LLM-as-a-judge reliability for agentic tool-calling over workflow DAGs. Study of 3,808 instances across 5 generators and 6 judges reveals that judge alignment degrades with task difficulty, with ground truth exposure not uniformly beneficial and all judges converging to a 77-82% ceiling on hard queries without ground truth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM judges are widely used for evaluating agentic tool-calling systems, but their reliability on structured, dependency-driven workflows remains largely unexamined. This work addresses the gap by creating the first benchmark specifically designed for this evaluation context.

Method: AgentJudgeBench comprises 3,808 instances spanning six DAG topologies and three difficulty tiers. Experiments evaluated five generators (3B-70B open-weight models and GPT-5.4) with six judges (20B to frontier scale) under paired with- and without-ground-truth conditions. Includes human validation study.

Result: Key findings: (1) Judge alignment degrades monotonically with task difficulty, 1.5x faster without ground truth; (2) On hard queries without ground truth, all six judges converge to a narrow 77-82% band regardless of scale; (3) Ground-truth exposure reduces alignment for GPT-5.4 (1.5 pp) and Gemini-2.5-Pro (3.9 pp) due to over-anchoring; (4) Chain-of-thought and judge temperature have negligible effect; (5) Structured evaluation rubrics improve alignment by up to 6.5 pp but don't generalize uniformly; (6) With ground truth, QwQ-32B best matches programmatic reference while GPT-OSS-120B is most human-aligned; (7) Without ground truth, frontier judges lead only marginally within the shared ceiling.

Conclusion: Current LLM judges have fundamental limitations for agentic system evaluation. Model capacity alone cannot overcome the structural ceiling driven by task difficulty. Ground truth exposure is not always beneficial. Structured evaluation rubrics offer the best mitigation strategy (up to 6.5 pp improvement), though effectiveness varies by judge-generator pair. Practical guidelines provided for reliable evaluation in agentic systems.

Abstract: LLM judges are widely used to evaluate agentic tool-calling systems, yet their reliability on structured, dependency-driven workflows remains largely unexamined. We present AgentJudgeBench, the first benchmark to systematically study LLM-as-a-judge reliability for agentic tool-calling over workflow DAGs, as distinct from the broader LLM-as-a-judge task of open-ended text or preference evaluation. The benchmark comprises 3,808 instances spanning six DAG topologies and three difficulty tiers, evaluated with five generators (3B-70B open-weight models and GPT-5.4) and six judges (20B to frontier scale) under paired with- and without-ground-truth conditions. Judge alignment degrades monotonically with task difficulty, 1.5x faster without ground truth, and on hard queries without ground truth all six judges converge to a narrow 77-82% band regardless of scale, revealing a structural ceiling driven primarily by task difficulty, though its height is partly prompt-dependent for weaker generators, that model capacity alone cannot overcome. Ground-truth exposure is not uniformly beneficial: it reduces alignment for GPT-5.4 (1.5 pp) and Gemini-2.5-Pro (3.9 pp), consistent with over-anchoring. Among mitigation strategies, chain-of-thought reasoning and judge temperature both have negligible effect, while structured evaluation rubrics improve alignment by up to 6.5 pp but do not generalize uniformly across judge-generator pairs. With ground truth, QwQ-32B best matches the programmatic reference, while a human validation study identifies GPT-OSS-120B as the most human-aligned judge; without it, frontier judges lead only marginally within the shared ceiling. These results expose fundamental limitations of current LLM judges and yield practical guidelines for reliable evaluation in agentic systems.

</details>


### [145] [SIGMA: Structured Noise-Effect-Aware Grouped Multi-Agent Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26683)
*Li Mingqian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses robust coordination in cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning under noisy observations. The authors identify "structured noise effects" where noise impacts become correlated among agents with stronger task dependencies. They propose SIGMA, a hierarchical framework that uses density-based grouping for intra-group consensus aggregation and inter-group attention for global coordination.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cooperative MARL struggles with noisy observations. While noise is introduced independently, its effects become structured through cooperation structures. Existing methods fail to exploit this structure-dependent noise effects, necessitating a new approach that characterizes and leverages these structured effects.

Method: SIGMA employs a hierarchical collaboration framework with two main components: (1) Density-based grouping to organize agents into adaptive local structures, with intra-group consensus aggregation to preserve shared task-relevant information while smoothing agent-specific deviations; (2) Inter-group attention to adaptively integrate information across groups while accommodating heterogeneous contributions.

Result: Experiments on StarCraft II with noisy observations validate the structured noise effects and show SIGMA consistently improves robustness under observation noise while maintaining competitive performance in noise-free environments.

Conclusion: The proposed SIGMA framework effectively exploits cooperation structures to learn robust representations under noisy observations, demonstrating that explicitly characterizing structure-dependent noise effects can significantly enhance MARL robustness without sacrificing clean-environment performance.

Abstract: Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) faces significant challenges in maintaining robust coordination under noisy observations. Although observation disturbances are often introduced independently across agents, their downstream effects on cooperative decision-making can become structured through underlying cooperation structures. We characterize this phenomenon as structured noise effects, where noise-induced decision effects exhibit local correlation among agents with stronger task-related dependencies while remaining globally heterogeneous across different agents and local structures. Existing robust MARL methods, however, rarely explicitly characterize or exploit such structure-dependent noise effects. To address this limitation, we propose SIGMA, a hierarchical collaboration framework that exploits cooperation structures to learn robust representations under noisy observations. SIGMA first organizes agents into adaptive local structures through density-based grouping and performs intra-group consensus aggregation to preserve shared task-relevant information while smoothing agent-specific representation deviations. Inter-group attention then adaptively integrates information across different groups to preserve global coordination while accommodating their heterogeneous contributions. Experiments on noisy-observation tasks in StarCraft II empirically validate the structured noise effects and demonstrate that SIGMA consistently improves robustness under observation noise while maintaining competitive performance in noise-free environments.

</details>


### [146] [Relational Over-Regularization: Graph-Based AI-Generated Text Detection via Sentence Transition Deviation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26694)
*Hyeonchu Park,Bugeun Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Relational Over-Regularization (ROR), a structural signal at the sentence-pair level that identifies AI-generated text through inflated inter-sentence transition variance. The proposed CSFG framework encodes multiple signals (positional, sequential, semantic, transition deviation) as learnable GNN edge features, achieving 97.14% accuracy with only 1.57% false-positive rate across four benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AIGT detection methods rely on token-level statistical signals or independent stylometric features, causing overfitting to specific generators and failure under distribution shift. The authors identify that LLMs produce inter-sentence transition variance that deviates from human writing through recurring similarity bursts at paragraph boundaries and templated transitions.

Method: The authors formalize ROR as a relational problem and propose CSFG (Cross-Source Stylometric Fingerprint Graph), a graph-based framework that encodes positional, sequential, semantic, and transition deviation signals as learnable GNN edge features. The per-edge signed deviation δ_ij operationalizes ROR without hand-crafted thresholds and acts as a false-positive calibrator.

Result: CSFG achieves 97.14% accuracy under binary detection, outperforming the strongest graph-based baseline by 11.14 percentage points, with a false-positive rate of 1.57%. It shows robust generalization to unseen LLMs in the inflated-variance regime, though detection degrades for generators whose transition variance falls at or below the human baseline. Validation across four benchmarks shows p < 0.001.

Conclusion: The relational problem formulation (ROR) is the central contribution rather than a novel GNN architecture. CSFG effectively exploits the ROR signal for AIGT detection, but its effectiveness is limited to generators exhibiting inflated transition variance compared to human writing.

Abstract: Detecting AI-generated text (AIGT) remains challenging because existing approaches rely on token-level statistical signals or independent stylometric features, causing them to overfit to specific generators and fail under distribution shift. We identify a structural signal at the sentence-pair level: LLMs produce inter-sentence transition variance that deviates from human writing through inflated variance driven by recurring similarity bursts at paragraph boundaries and templated transitions. We formalize this as Relational Over-Regularization (ROR) and validate it across four benchmarks (p < 0.001). The central contribution is this relational problem formulation, not a novel GNN architecture; CSFG is one concrete instantiation for operationalizing ROR. To exploit this signal, we propose the Cross-Source Stylometric Fingerprint Graph (CSFG), a graph-based framework that encodes positional, sequential, semantic, and transition deviation signals as learnable GNN edge features. The per-edge signed deviation δ_ij operationalizes ROR without hand-crafted thresholds and acts as a false-positive calibrator. CSFG achieves 97.14% accuracy under binary detection, outperforming the strongest graph-based baseline by 11.14 pp, with a false-positive rate of 1.57% and robust generalization to unseen LLMs in the inflated-variance regime; detection degrades for generators whose transition variance falls at or below the human baseline.

</details>


### [147] [Accelerating Scientific Research with Gemini in the Real-World](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26701)
*Samuel Schmidgall,Xiaokai Zhu,Marian Shaw,Lin Yang,Valentin Liévin,Jingyun Yang,Yuchen Zhuang,Tim Strother,Alex Bijamov,Min Woo Sun,Anil Palepu,Justin Chen,David Steiner,Jacqueline Shreibati,Wei-Hung Weng,Yilin Zhao,Xingjian Hu,Nicholas Zahn,Sadhya Garg,Julia Kirby,Yuxiang Gan,Jiaoli Li,Divy Thakkar,Shekoofeh Azizi,David Racz,Juraj Gottweis,Vivek Natarajan,Chenglin Wu,Tal Danino,Keran Rong,Haozhe Wang,Benoit Schillings,Yong Cheng,Quoc V. Le,Tao Tu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Co-Scientist is extended from in silico hypothesis generation to an execution-grounded multi-agent research partner, validated across materials science (MXene synthesis, 2D semiconductor growth), biology (E. coli phenotypic prediction), and computer science (inference-time scaling for HealthBench), with a double-blind study demonstrating reduced hallucination and plagiarism.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To advance AI-driven scientific discovery from hypothesis generation to closed-loop execution across real-world laboratory workflows in materials science, biology, and computer science.

Method: Configured Co-Scientist with Gemini 3 Deep Think for rapid lab-in-the-loop execution; interfaced with semi-automated CVD reactor for materials synthesis; used sparse imaging data for biological phenotype prediction; developed inference-time scaling architecture; conducted double-blind study with 30 domain experts across 450 reviews.

Result: Produced lamellar 2D material structurally similar to Ti3C2Tx MXene; achieved single-attempt growth of monolayer MoS2, MoSe2, and WS2; predicted emergent E. coli swarming phenotypes matching wet-lab measurements; outperformed six frontier models on HealthBench while reducing clinical harm; reliability modules reduced hallucination and plagiarism.

Conclusion: Progress toward closed-loop multi-agent scientific AI systems capable of accelerating real-world scientific discovery across multiple domains.

Abstract: We present an extension and comprehensive real-world validation of Co-Scientist, a Gemini-based multi-agent system designed to accelerate end-to-end scientific research across hypothesis generation, experimentation, and manuscript generation. Moving beyond in silico hypothesis generation, this specialized configuration transitions Co-Scientist into an execution-grounded research partner advancing closed-loop scientific workflows across materials science, biology, and computer science. In materials science, Co-Scientist interfaced with a semi-automated chemical vapor deposition reactor to design a safe precursor route for MXenes; experimental execution produced a lamellar 2D material sharing key structural similarities with the Ti3C2Tx MXene lattice, although further experiments are needed to confirm the atomic structure. Leveraging Gemini 3 Deep Think for rapid, lab-in-the-loop execution, it also tailored growth recipes to laboratory constraints in minutes, enabling single-attempt growth of monolayer MoS2, MoSe2, and WS2 semiconductors. In biology, Co-Scientist predicted emergent swarming phenotypes of engineered E. coli across inducer (IPTG) gradients from sparse imaging data, quantitatively matching unpublished wet-lab morphological measurements. In computer science, Co-Scientist autonomously discovered an inference-time scaling architecture that outperformed six frontier models on HealthBench (Hard and Professional) while reducing potential clinical harm under blinded physician evaluation. Finally, a double-blind study of end-to-end generated papers with 30 domain experts across 450 reviews demonstrates that Co-Scientist's reliability modules reduce hallucination and plagiarism while improving research safety. Together, these results demonstrate progress toward closed-loop multi-agent scientific AI systems capable of accelerating real-world scientific discovery.

</details>


### [148] [Style as a Confound: False Positives in AI Detection of Non-Native Academic Writing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26710)
*Hyeonchu Park,Gahye Jeong,Bugeun Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study examines whether AI text detectors truly identify AI authorship or merely detect linguistic polish. Analyzing 135,389 document pairs from a professional editing service (non-native manuscripts vs. native-edited versions), researchers found that false positive rates ranged from 0% to 100% across 13 detectors. The same edits increased AI scores in some detectors but decreased them in others, with score changes correlating with editing extent. The findings suggest professional editing style is a key confounding variable, not a true separation of AI vs. human authorship.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI text detectors are increasingly used in academic settings, but high false positive rates for non-native English writing have been reported. Previous studies confounded authorship with differences in topic, domain, and writing style. This study aims to determine whether detector outputs reflect actual AI authorship or broader linguistic features associated with polished academic English, using professional editing as a natural experiment to control for content and authorship.

Method: The researchers analyzed 135,389 document pairs from a professional English editing service (2018-2025), comparing non-native manuscripts with their native-edited versions. This design controls for content and authorship while isolating the effect of linguistic polish. They tested 13 AI text detectors to assess how editing affects detector responses.

Result: False positive rates for human-written texts varied dramatically across detectors, ranging from 0.0% to 100.0%. Responses were inconsistent: the same edits increased AI scores in some detectors but decreased them in others. Score changes correlated with the extent of editing. These findings indicate that detectors conflate authorship with linguistic style rather than truly distinguishing AI-generated from human-written text.

Conclusion: Professional editing style is a key confounding variable in AI detector outputs, rather than establishing a full separation of text origin from linguistic style. This raises serious concerns about fairness (especially for non-native English writers) and reliability in academic settings where these detectors are increasingly deployed.

Abstract: AI text detectors are increasingly employed in academic settings, but it remains unclear whether their outputs reflect AI authorship itself or broader linguistic features associated with polished academic English. Previous studies have reported high false-positive rates (FPRs) for non-native English writing, but population-level comparisons confound authorship with differences in topic, domain, and writing style. Professional editing provides a useful setting for examining this issue because it changes the linguistic form of manuscripts while preserving authorship and content. We examined 135,389 document pairs from a professional English editing service (2018-2025), comprising non-native manuscripts and their native-edited versions, to assess how editing affects detector responses controlling for content and authorship. For the 13 AI text detectors, FPRs for human-written texts varied widely, from 0.0% to 100.0%. Responses varied across detectors: the same edits increased AI scores in some detectors but decreased them in others. Notably, score changes correlated with the extent of editing. The findings identify professional editing style as a key confounding variable in AI detector outputs, rather than establishing a full separation of text origin from linguistic style, raising concerns about fairness and reliability in academic settings.

</details>


### [149] [Knowing When Not to Reuse: Conditional Experience Transfer in Autonomous LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26730)
*Tingyun Li,Wenfeng Feng,Weiqing Li,Abudukelimu Wuerkaixi,Guohua Liu,Yuewei Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Large language models offer broad capabilities, but adapting them to evolving domains, tools, and requirements often entails repeated post-training. Autonomous systems automate parts of this process by proposing updates, training candidates, and using evaluation feedback to select subsequent proposals. As evidence accumulates, a central problem emerges: which past update evidence remains actionable after subsequent training has changed the parent model? An update's effect depends on its parent, data, and training stage. Treating past success as context-free permission can waste compute. If the resulting child is promoted, it can also degrade the subsequent training trajectory. We formulate this problem as conditional experience transfer and introduce Boundary-Calibrated Intervention Transfer (BCIT), a method that authorizes experience reuse before weight-changing training. BCIT binds an observed effect to its source context, checks applicability conditions, vetoes candidates with named hard conflicts, and obtains current-state evidence through a bounded training trial when needed. Fully trained candidates still face a shared adoption rule, and only observed events extend memory. On one 4B model adapted across finance reasoning, text-to-SQL, and function calling, candidate updates exhibit heterogeneous target and retention effects across the evaluated contexts. Under matched candidates, evidence, and compute, BCIT authorizes fewer harmful updates and attains higher equal-budget final-model quality than the evaluated alternatives. These results support treating experience authorization as a distinct problem in autonomous post-training.

</details>


### [150] [Graph-Guided Selective Unlearning for Language Models: Controlling Support Routes Beyond Forget Seeds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26743)
*Waqas Khan,Tabinda Sarwar,Jingyue Cong,Xun Yi,Estrid He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GRAPHSU is a graph-guided machine unlearning method that expands the deletion scope beyond explicitly identified forget examples by constructing a weighted support-route graph to propagate deletion pressure to high-risk neighbors, achieving up to 49.5% leakage reduction on TOFU and PISTOL benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing selective unlearning methods only target explicitly identified forget examples, but target knowledge can remain recoverable through paraphrases, aliases, or neighboring training examples. Enterprises need practical unlearning that truly removes requested knowledge while preserving model utility.

Method: GRAPHSU constructs a weighted support-route graph to identify relationships between training examples, propagates deletion pressure through this graph, and applies graded forgetting strengths to high-risk neighbors beyond the seed forget examples.

Result: GRAPHSU achieves the lowest utility-feasible soft leakage across all deletion settings on TOFU (synthetic author-profile QA benchmark) and PISTOL (structural-unlearning benchmark with interconnected factual samples), using GPT-2 Medium and Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct models. Leakage reduced by up to 49.5 percentage points over matched seed-only baseline.

Conclusion: Effective enterprise unlearning requires controlling support routes, not just forget seeds. The graph-guided approach that expands deletion scope beyond explicitly identified examples is essential for truly removing target knowledge while preserving model utility.

Abstract: Enterprises fine-tune language models on proprietary data that may later require removal due to privacy, contractual, or compliance obligations. Selective unlearning removes requested knowledge while preserving model utility, offering a practical alternative to full retraining, but existing methods treat the explicitly identified forget examples as the complete deletion scope. This is insufficient when target knowledge remains recoverable through paraphrases, aliases, or neighboring training examples. We propose GRAPHSU, a graph-guided controller that expands the deletion scope beyond forget seeds by constructing a weighted support-route graph, propagating deletion pressure through it, and applying graded forgetting strengths to high-risk neighbors. On the Task of Fictitious Unlearning (TOFU), a synthetic author-profile question-answering benchmark, and PISTOL, a structural-unlearning benchmark built around interconnected factual samples, with GPT-2 Medium and Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct, GRAPHSU achieves the lowest utility-feasible soft leakage across all deletion settings, reducing leakage by up to 49.5 percentage points over a matched seed-only baseline, demonstrating that effective enterprise unlearning requires controlling support routes, not just forget seeds.

</details>


### [151] [AgentFold: Closed-Loop Agentic Search for Protein Folding Model Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26747)
*Mingquan Liu,Jiangyu Chen,Hanqun Cao,Xujun Zhang,Pengsen Ma,Xiangru Tang,Shuting Jin,Zhuo Yang,Tianfan Fu,Fang Wu,Xiangxiang Zeng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Scientific LLM agents have shown promise in literature reasoning, tool use, and experiment planning, but it remains unclear whether they can autonomously improve large, tightly coupled scientific machine-learning systems through executable code changes and computationally expensive validation. We study this question in protein folding, where progress requires coordinated architectural modifications, multi-objective evaluation, and domain-aware interpretation. We present AgentFold, a multi-agent framework that formulates folding-model development as a closed-loop search over executable code variants. Starting from ESMFold, AgentFold proposes hypotheses, implements and debugs code-level modifications, evaluates model variants, analyzes experimental outcomes, and stores both successful and failed interventions in structured memory. An MCTS-style policy allocates computational resources across high-scoring search branches. On an engineering-scale protein-folding codebase comprising more than 2,000 lines of code, AgentFold explores approximately 80 model variants using approximately 5,000 GPU-hours and 170 million LLM tokens. Under a matched computational budget, AgentFold improves the best lDDT by 7.5% over independent Codex proposals and outperforms a random-search control. Beyond model improvement, the resulting intervention traces reveal recurring empirical design patterns: stable gains tend to arise from early, soft, learnable priors and gated refinement, whereas direct geometric perturbations and geometry-conditioned feedback often destabilize training. The code and experimental resources are publicly available at https://github.com/lmqfly/AgentFold.

</details>


### [152] [Discovering Relationships in Data Lakes Using Large Language Models: An Industrial Case](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26750)
*Ahlame Diouan,Eric Ferey,Sabine Loudcher,Jérôme Darmont*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ColRel is a two-stage method for discovering column relationships in data lakes, particularly effective for ERP-derived datasets with coded or abbreviated schema labels. It builds column embeddings from metadata and data at ingestion time, using business dictionaries to generate natural-language descriptions for improved relationship detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data lakes rely on metadata to remain usable, but this metadata is often limited or weakly informative for column relationship discovery, especially in ERP-derived datasets with coded or abbreviated schema labels.

Method: ColRel uses a two-stage approach: (1) Build column embeddings from metadata and data available at ingestion time; (2) In difficult cases like coded schemata, use business dictionaries to better interpret column names and generate short natural-language descriptions that support the second stage.

Result: Experiments on public benchmarks and an industrial ERP dataset show that ColRel is particularly effective in semantically related, weak-signal settings.

Conclusion: ColRel successfully addresses the challenge of column relationship discovery in data lakes with poor metadata, especially for ERP systems with coded schemas, by leveraging business dictionaries and natural-language descriptions to enhance embedding quality.

Abstract: Data lakes rely on metadata to remain usable, yet this meta data is often limited or weakly informative for column relationship discovery, especially in ERP-derived datasets with coded or abbreviated schema labels. We propose ColRel, a two-stage method that builds column embeddings from metadata and data available at ingestion time. In difficult cases, such as coded schemata, business dictionaries help better interpret column names and support the generation of short natural-language descriptions used in the second stage. Experiments on public benchmarks and an industrial ERP dataset show that ColRel is particularly effective in semantically related, weak-signal settings.

</details>


### [153] [DEEPCHART: How Far are LLMs from Faithful Data-Science Chart Generation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26757)
*Jiahui tang,Kuicai Dong,Dexun Li,Hongchao Gu,Haocheng Yu,Wei Han,Chen Zhang,Yong Liu,Hao Wang,Enhong Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DEEPCHART is a benchmark of 1,482 expert-annotated instances for evaluating chart generation in AI systems, addressing the problem that modern LLMs produce visually plausible charts but with undetected data-level hallucinations in long, noisy, multimodal contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world data-science workflows require grounded, accurate chart generation, but current LLMs struggle with data-level hallucinations that are difficult to detect in complex contexts. Existing benchmarks lack realistic, long, multimodal evaluation settings.

Method: DEEPCHART formulates chart generation as a 3-stage Extract--Reason--Visualize pipeline and evaluates stage by stage: (1) source-data extraction, (2) derived-data reasoning, and (3) chart rendering. The benchmark contains instances from real-world scientific papers, financial filings, and ecosystem reports.

Result: Experiments with state-of-the-art models reveal that visually plausible charts often conceal data-level hallucinations, with extraction and reasoning errors being common in realistic long and multimodal settings.

Conclusion: Larger context windows alone are insufficient for faithful chart generation; reliable evidence extraction and quantitative reasoning are also required before rendering.

Abstract: Faithful chart generation in real-world data-science workflows requires grounding visualizations in scattered evidence, computing chart-ready quantities, and rendering them accurately. Modern LLMs can produce visually plausible, instruction-compliant charts, yet data-level hallucinations remain difficult to detect in long, noisy, and multimodal contexts. To measure this gap, we introduce DEEPCHART, an expert-annotated benchmark of 1,482 task-conditioned chart-generation instances drawn from real-world scientific papers, financial filings, and ecosystem reports. DEEPCHART formulates chart generation as an Extract--Reason--Visualize pipeline and evaluates source-data extraction, derived-data reasoning, and chart rendering stage by stage. Experiments with state-of-the-art models show that visually plausible charts often conceal data-level hallucinations, with extraction and reasoning errors common in realistic long and multimodal settings. These findings suggest that larger context windows alone are insufficient; faithful chart generation also requires reliable evidence extraction and quantitative reasoning before rendering. Our benchmark and associated resources are available at https://github.com/tangdouer1005/DeepChart.

</details>


### [154] [Categorizer Automata for Discounted-Sum Payoffs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26763)
*Nathalie Bertrand,Pranav Ghorpade,Senthil Rajasekaran,Sasha Rubin,Moshe Vardi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces categorizer automata, which identify which bin contains the discounted sum of an infinite reward sequence. It generalizes comparator automata (2 bins) and provides a linear-state-space construction instead of exponential. Applied to MDPs, it enables synthesis of policies for discontinuous utility functions, with exact algorithms for piecewise-constant and ε-optimal for piecewise-Lipschitz utilities, though the problem is PSPACE-hard.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Categorizing continuous data (discounted sums) into discrete bins is fundamental in AI. Comparator automata handle only 2 bins, but many applications require more bins. The cross-product approach leads to exponential state space, necessitating a more efficient construction.

Method: Construct a categorizer automaton with state space linear in the number of bins (rather than exponential). Apply to Markov decision processes for policy synthesis with discontinuous utility functions.

Result: For piecewise-constant utilities: exact algorithm in pseudo-polynomial time. For piecewise-Lipschitz utilities: pseudo-polynomial time with ε-optimal policy. Problem is PSPACE-hard for piecewise-constant utilities.

Conclusion: Categorizer automata provide an efficient solution for quantitative synthesis in MDPs with potentially discontinuous utility functions, achieving linear state complexity while maintaining computational tractability for useful utility function classes.

Abstract: Categorizing continuous data into discrete bins is a fundamental operation in artificial intelligence. We introduce the categorizer automaton, a deterministic automaton that reads an infinite sequence of rewards and identifies which of finitely many bins contains its discounted sum. Categorizer automata generalize comparator automata, the special case of two bins, which have already proven useful in quantitative synthesis. Our main technical contribution is the construction of a categorizer automaton whose state space is linear in the number of bins, rather than exponential as obtained by a cross-product of comparator automata. We then apply categorizer automata to Markov decision processes, where they allow one to synthesize policies that maximize the expected utility of a discounted-sum payoff for utility functions that may be discontinuous. For piecewise-constant utility functions, the resulting algorithm is exact and runs in pseudo-polynomial time. For piecewise-Lipschitz utility functions, a class that includes any utility with bounded slope between finitely many jumps, it again runs in pseudo-polynomial time and yields an $\varepsilon$-optimal policy. We also show that the synthesis problem considered is PSPACE-hard already for piecewise-constant utilities.

</details>


### [155] [AI Control Scientist: LLM-driven Agentic System for Automated Control Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26780)
*Haiteng Wang,Weihao Li,Jing Zhang,Lei Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AICS (AI Control Scientist) is the first LLM-driven agent that automatically generates optimized controllers from language design requirements, using three specialized agents: Task Modeling Agent for interpreting requirements, Controller Design Agent for generating controller structures and code, and Parameter Tuning Agent for optimizing parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional control system design relies heavily on expert knowledge and manual parameter tuning, resulting in limited efficiency and scalability. There is a need for automated approaches to control system design.

Method: The system uses a multi-agent architecture with three specialized agents: (1) Task Modeling Agent interprets user requirements into engineering constraints; (2) Controller Design Agent generates candidate controller structures and executable code; (3) Parameter Tuning Agent refines controller parameters under closed-loop performance criteria.

Result: The proposed agentic system can automatically generate multiple representative control systems and outperforms existing automated baselines in both design success rate and optimization efficiency.

Conclusion: This work has the potential to transform control system design from human-driven to agent-driven, paving the way for model predictive control and other advanced control systems design.

Abstract: Control system design is critical for modern industry, such as chemical process temperature regulation and aero-engine control. However,traditional control design workflows rely heavily on expert knowledge and extensive manual parameter tuning, resulting in limited efficiency and scalability. To this end, this paper proposes AI Control Scientist (AICS), the first large language model (LLM)-driven agent capable of automatically generating optimized controller from language design requirements. Specifically, a Task Modeling Agent interprets user requirements to engineering constraints; a Controller Design Agent generate candidate controller structures and executable code; and a Parameter Tuning Agent refine controller parameters under closed-loop performance criteria. Experiments demonstrate that the proposed agentic system can automatically generate multiple representative control systems, outperforms existing automated baselines in both design success rate and optimization efficiency. This work has the potential to transform control system design from human-driven to agent-driven, paving the way for model predictive control and other advanced control systems design.

</details>


### [156] [Decoupling Planning and Control for Instructable Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26788)
*Zineng Tang,Kelsey R. Allen,Sjoerd van Steenkiste,Ishita Dasgupta,Alane Suhr*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Instruct-to-Act, a hybrid system combining VLM planners with world-model controllers. The VLM generates high-level text instructions while a trained controller executes fast, low-latency actions. The key contribution is training controllers to be language-instructable through relabeling rollout segments with synthetic instructions and joint optimization of behavior cloning with reward-maximizing and world-modeling objectives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pre-trained instruction-tuned VLMs excel at high-level planning but struggle with reliable low-latency action execution in unfamiliar environments. Conversely, world-model controllers excel at fast control but lack open-ended task guidance. The authors aim to combine these complementary strengths.

Method: The system uses a decoupled architecture where a VLM planner generates sparse, high-level text instructions, which condition a world-model controller operating at high frequency. To train language-instructable controllers, the authors relabel segments of controller policy rollouts with synthetic instructions and jointly optimize a behavior-cloning objective alongside existing reward-maximizing and world-modeling objectives.

Result: The approach was evaluated across seven embodied environments, including three multi-agent environments. Under matched observation and action spaces, the decoupled approach consistently outperforms controller-only and direct VLM action-generation variants, preserves fast control, allows swapping different pretrained VLM planners without fine-tuning, and remains competitive with strong vision-language-action and multi-agent RL baselines on six of seven tasks.

Conclusion: The Instruct-to-Act system successfully combines the planning capabilities of VLMs with the fast control of world-model controllers. The decoupled design enables modularity (different VLM planners can be swapped without retraining), maintains low-latency control, and achieves strong performance across diverse embodied tasks including multi-agent coordination scenarios.

Abstract: Recent work shows that pre-trained, instruction-tuned vision-language models (VLMs) perform well at mapping from instructions and observations to high-level plans, but struggle to realize such plans as reliable low-latency action sequences in unfamiliar environments. At the same time, world-model controllers excel at fast observation-to-action control, but lack open-ended task guidance. In this work, we combine these strengths into a single system, Instruct-to-Act, where we train a world-model controller to act autonomously at high frequency when conditioned on sparse, higher-latency, and high-level text instructions generated by a VLM planner. To train controllers to be language-instructable, we relabel segments of controller policy rollouts with synthetic instructions and jointly optimize a behavior-cloning objective along with existing reward-maximizing and world-modeling objectives. We evaluate our proposed approach across seven embodied environments, including three multi-agent environments where VLM planners coordinate through language while trained controllers serve as their actuators. Under matched observation and action spaces, our decoupled approach consistently outperforms controller-only and direct VLM action-generation variants, preserves fast control, and lets us swap in different pretrained VLM planners without fine-tuning, while remaining competitive with strong vision-language-action and multi-agent RL baselines on six of seven tasks.

</details>


### [157] [SymbolLKG: Towards Verifiable Logical Reasoning via Logical Knowledge Graph and Symbolic Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26836)
*Haizhao Fan,Yuchi Xiong,Jize Wang,Xinping Guan,Xinyi Le*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a Neuro-Symbolic architecture that integrates a Logical Knowledge Graph (LKG) with dynamic solver routing to address LLMs' limitations in multi-step logical reasoning, hallucinations, and inconsistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs struggle with strict multi-step reasoning despite their proficiency in natural language understanding. Existing solutions like Chain-of-Thought (CoT) lack rigorous verification mechanisms, and standard Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) fails to capture complex structural dependencies inherent in logical tasks.

Method: The authors introduce: (1) An ontology-based LKG that treats logical rules and constraints as first-class topological nodes, enabling explicit modeling of dependencies extracted from text; (2) A Logic Router that dynamically dispatches tasks to the optimal symbolic engine; (3) A topology-aware hybrid retrieval mechanism to support the routing.

Result: Experimental results on logical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that the framework significantly outperforms state-of-the-art prompting and RAG baselines, delivering higher accuracy and verifiable reasoning paths.

Conclusion: The neuro-symbolic approach with LKG and dynamic solver routing effectively addresses multi-step reasoning limitations in LLMs by combining neural language understanding with symbolic logical verification, achieving superior performance on logical reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable proficiency in natural language understanding, yet they struggle with strict multi-step reasoning, frequently suffering from hallucinations and inconsistency. Existing solutions like Chain-of-Thought (CoT) lack rigorous verification mechanisms, while standard Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) often misses the complex, structural dependencies inherent in logical tasks. To bridge this gap, we propose a Neuro-Symbolic architecture that integrates a Logical Knowledge Graph (LKG) with dynamic solver routing. Specifically, we introduce an ontology-based LKG that treats logical rules and constraints as first-class topological nodes, enabling explicit modeling of dependencies extracted from text. We further design a Logic Router to dynamically dispatch tasks to the optimal symbolic engine, which is supported by a topology-aware hybrid retrieval mechanism. Experimental results on logical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that our framework significantly outperforms state-of-the-art prompting and RAG baselines, delivering higher accuracy and verifiable reasoning paths.

</details>


### [158] [LiveSim: Simulating Environment-Shaped Users in Multi-Agent Live-Stream Ecosystems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26849)
*Jiaqi Xu,Yiran Qiao,Jing Chen,Qiwei Zhong,Xiang Ao,Xueqi Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LiveSim is an LLM-based framework for simulating live-stream ecosystems, where users are represented as editable behavioral hypotheses that are progressively refined through trajectory-grounded interactions. It extracts environmental shaping effects as transferable patterns stored in collective behavioral memory to improve simulation fidelity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based user behavior simulators rely on static user profiles derived from historical data, which become inadequate in dynamic social environments like live streaming where interaction dynamics continuously reshape user behavior.

Method: LiveSim represents users as editable behavioral hypotheses and refines them progressively through trajectory-grounded interactions. Discrepancies between simulated and observed trajectories reveal missing environmental shaping effects, which are extracted as transferable environment-behavior patterns and accumulated in a collective behavioral memory.

Result: Experiments on real-world live-stream risk-control data demonstrate that LiveSim improves user-level behavioral fidelity and enables ecosystem-level analysis of risk evolution and platform intervention effects.

Conclusion: LiveSim effectively addresses the limitations of static user profiles in dynamic live-stream environments by introducing editable behavioral hypotheses and collective behavioral memory, providing improved simulation fidelity and enabling ecosystem-level analysis.

Abstract: User behavior simulation with large language models~(LLMs) is increasingly used to support multi-agent ecosystem simulation. Existing simulators typically rely on static user profiles inferred from historical observations, which become inadequate in socially intensive environments such as live streaming where interaction dynamics continuously reshape user behavior. We propose \textbf{LiveSim}, an LLM-based framework for live-stream ecosystem simulation. It represents users as editable behavioral hypotheses and progressively refines them through trajectory-grounded interactions, where discrepancies between simulated and observed trajectories reveal missing environmental shaping effects. These signals are further extracted as transferable environment-behavior patterns and accumulated in a collective behavioral memory to improve user-level behavioral fidelity and support ecosystem-level simulation. Experiments on real-world live-stream risk-control data validate the effectiveness of LiveSim in improving user-level behavioral fidelity and enabling ecosystem-level analysis of risk evolution and platform intervention effects.

</details>


### [159] [BekchiAI: Measuring, Observing, and Controlling LLM Agents in One Click](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26867)
*Mesut Toruk*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Large language model agents reason, call tools, and act autonomously over many steps, but their agentic skills-correctly sequencing tools, planning under dependencies, judging untrusted inputs, and grounding generated arguments-are hard to measure with accuracy-only leaderboards. We present BekchiAI, which addresses both sides: a benchmark for measuring agentic skill and a platform for observing and controlling live agents. The BekchiAI-Benchmark, a suite of 13 tool-using ReAct agents across 7 task categories (arithmetic, structured/SQL, security detection, URL grounding, planning, orchestration, and tool-policy), totalling 2,057 deterministic, committed test tasks. Every task is verifier-checkable gold answers are computed by running canonical SQL against a real database, computing the exact schedule of a directed acyclic graph (DAG), or evaluating closed-form lambdas including adversarial security samples paired with deliberately imperfect signature scanners so a score reflects the model's own judgment, not the copying of an oracle. We define a small set of behavioral metrics beyond accuracy-tool-call adherence, URL hallucination and source-match, and per-model token cost and report a four-model comparison (Qwen3.7-Max, gemma-4-31B-it, gemma4:26b, gpt-oss-120b) whose story is in the per-family spread, not the aggregate. The benchmark runs are executed using the provided evaluation scripts. BekchiAI-Platform is a complementary web-based observability and control layer for deployed agents, providing full token and latency telemetry as well as remote run termination. The benchmark, evaluation tools, and platform are publicly released.

</details>


### [160] [C-Unseen: Weak Signal Detection in Dynamic Temporal Knowledge Graphs via LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26870)
*Yassir Lairgi,Ludovic Moncla,Khalid Benabdeslem,Rémy Cazabet,Pierre Cléau*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: C-Unseen is a self-interpretable framework for detecting weak signals in Dynamic Temporal Knowledge Graphs (DTKGs), defining weak signals as rare, semantically coherent subgraphs that proliferate across consecutive TKG snapshots. It uses an LLM with chain-of-thought reasoning to identify rare subgraphs in tension with dominant narratives, then tracks their persistence over time to isolate true weak signals, outperforming keyword-, topic-, and graph-based baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing weak signal detection methods (keyword frequency, topic modeling, untyped graph topology) fail to capture the semantic and relational structure through which weak signals manifest. Weak signals are early, low-visibility indicators that precede significant changes, requiring more sophisticated detection approaches.

Method: The framework consists of two modules: (1) Rare Subgraphs Extractor - uses an LLM with chain-of-thought reasoning to identify subgraphs whose content conflicts with the dominant narrative of a snapshot; (2) Weak Signal Alerter - tracks the persistence of these rare subgraphs across consecutive time steps to isolate true weak signals from noise.

Result: C-Unseen outperforms keyword-based, topic-based, and graph-based baseline methods for weak signal detection.

Conclusion: The proposed self-interpretable framework successfully detects weak signals in DTKGs by combining semantic understanding via LLMs with temporal persistence tracking, offering a more effective approach than traditional detection methods.

Abstract: Weak signals are early, low-visibility indicators that precede significant changes before those changes become established. Existing detection methods, based on keyword frequency, topic modeling, or untyped graph topology, fail to capture the semantic and relational structure through which such signals manifest. In this paper, we propose C-Unseen, a self-interpretable framework for weak signal detection in Dynamic Temporal Knowledge Graphs (DTKGs). We define a weak signal as a rare, semantically coherent subgraph that proliferates across consecutive TKG snapshots. The framework operates through two modules: a Rare Subgraphs Extractor, in which an LLM identifies subgraphs whose content is in tension with the dominant snapshot narrative via chain-of-thought reasoning, and a Weak Signal Alerter, in which the persistence of these rare subgraphs is tracked across time steps to isolate true weak signals. Experimental results demonstrate that C-Unseen outperforms keyword-, topic-, and graph-based baselines.

</details>

Abstract: Learning-augmented algorithms improve online decisions using predictions, but unreliable advice may harm efficiency and fairness. We study an online allocation problem with finite candidate sets, irreversible decisions, and exposure constraints. ### [162] [AI agents in Algorithmic Electricity Markets: On the Emergence of Tacit Collusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26896)
*Jakub Seredyński,Georgios Tsaousoglou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether tacit collusion can emerge in electricity markets where participants use autonomous learning-based algorithms for bidding. The authors model strategic bidding as a repeated game with imperfect public monitoring and use multi-agent reinforcement learning to study emergent behavior. They propose multi-dimensional criteria beyond profit comparisons to assess tacit collusion, and find that agents can indeed learn to sustain supra-competitive outcomes without being explicitly instructed to collude.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Electricity markets are increasingly algorithmic with learning-based bidding agents, and are structurally susceptible to non-competitive behavior due to oligopolistic structure and repeated interactions. Evidence from other algorithmic markets shows tacit collusion can arise through independent learning, making it critical to investigate whether this danger exists in electricity markets.

Method: The authors model strategic bidding as a repeated game with imperfect public monitoring, and use multi-agent reinforcement learning to model participants' emergent behavior. They propose a multi-dimensional set of criteria (going beyond simple profit comparisons against Nash equilibria) to assess whether the resulting behavior constitutes tacit collusion.

Result: Experimental results demonstrate that tacit collusion is a realistic danger in electricity markets. In certain cases, learning-based agents do learn to sustain supra-competitive outcomes that exhibit tacit collusion indicators, even though they were never explicitly instructed to collude.

Conclusion: The hypothesis that tacit collusion may emerge in electricity markets with autonomous learning-based algorithms is confirmed. This represents a significant risk for market operators and regulators, as market outcomes can become non-competitive purely through independent learning by autonomous agents.

Abstract: As electricity market participants increasingly adopt learning-based agents for their bidding strategies, electricity markets are becoming algorithmic. Evidence from algorithmic markets in other domains shows that tacit collusion can arise purely through independent learning. Moreover, electricity markets are typically oligopolistic and feature repeated interaction among a small number of participants, making them structurally susceptible to non-competitive behavior. In the face of these observations, this paper investigates the hypothesis that tacit collusion may emerge in electricity markets where participants' actions are controlled by autonomous learning-based algorithms. We model strategic bidding as a repeated game with imperfect public monitoring, and model the participants' emergent behavior using multi-agent reinforcement learning. We propose a multi-dimensional set of criteria (going beyond profit comparisons against Nash equilibria) to assess whether the resulting behavior constitutes tacit collusion. Our experimental results showcase that such a danger is realistic for electricity markets: there are cases where agents do learn to sustain supra-competitive outcomes that are supportive of tacit collusion indicators, even though the agents were never instructed to collude.

</details>

Abstract: Automated candidate-job matching systems are increasingly classified as high-risk AI under emerging regulation, yet auditing them for demographic bias is expensive: classical correspondence-audit studies require hand-crafted resumes and manual submission, which does not scale to fast pipeline retraining cycles. This paper presents a general, reusable methodology that (1) uses task-specialized LLM agents to synthesize identity-neutral base resumes and inject controlled demographic treatments across five protected-characteristic axes (sex/gender, age, residence, language, disability), producing a K x (1+N) correspondence-audit matrix; (2) qualitatively flags inferred protected characteristics per an EU AI Act-aligned prompt; (3) ranks candidates against a job description via a fine-tuned sentence-embedding model and cosine similarity; and (4) computes a nine-metric fairness suite spanning counterfactual (score delta, mean absolute rank change, flip rate), group-fairness (top-K retention, four-fifths/impact ratio), and merit-aware (Recall@K, nDCG@K, equal opportunity, equalized odds) families, each with bootstrap confidence intervals, significance tests, and Benjamini-Hochberg correction, culminating in an automated PASS/INVESTIGATE/FAIL report with a composite risk score. On an example corpus of 5 job orders, 100 base candidates, and 10 demographic treatments (90 metric x variant evaluations): score shifts, top-K retention, and merit-aware rate gaps stay within tolerance for every treatment, but a rank-stability metric (MARC) and nDCG@K each surface borderline findings - including one on the neutral baseline itself - that a score- or retention-only view would miss. The results argue for multi-metric, multi-family auditing over any single aggregate score, and for LLM-agent-generated audits as a practical, low-cost complement to human-curated audits for any candidate-job matching pipeline.

</details>


### [164] [A Table Is Worth 64 Tokens: Pixel-level Compression for Multi-Table Document Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26949)
*Iñigo Alonso,Mirella Lapata*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies pixel-level table compression for multi-table document question answering, proposing a training-free two-step method that first identifies relevant tables from compressed context then reasons over them at native resolution, achieving 41% token savings and 7 accuracy point gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Processing long documents with multiple tables is computationally expensive. While optical context compression (representing context as images) reduces token cost, its effect on table understanding was unclear. This work investigates how table downscaling affects VLM performance in multi-table document QA.

Method: Evaluated five VLMs across two benchmarks with five visual-token budgets. Discovered that native resolution table images match text performance, but downscaling causes models to compensate with longer reasoning traces. Proposed a training-free two-step approach: (1) identify relevant tables from pixel-compressed context, (2) reason over identified tables at native resolution.

Result: Native resolution table images match text in performance and efficiency. Downscaled tables preserve relevance detection capability. The two-step method achieves 41% token savings and 7 accuracy point gains over single-step QA with native resolution tables, while using 15% fewer tokens than the most efficient single-step compressed configuration with no accuracy loss.

Conclusion: The asymmetry between table relevance detection (preserved under heavy compression) and table reasoning (requires native resolution) enables a training-free two-step method that significantly improves efficiency and accuracy for multi-table document QA. This approach is particularly effective for long documents.

Abstract: Answering questions over real-world documents requires processing long inputs that interleave text with tables. Optical context compression, which represents context as images, promises to reduce token cost, but its effect on table understanding remains unclear. We study pixel-level table compression for question answering over documents with multiple tables, evaluating five VLMs across two benchmarks and five visual-token budgets. Representing tables as images at native resolution matches text in both performance and efficiency, but downscaling them makes models compensate the loss in readability with longer, less effective reasoning traces that cancel the expected savings. Highly downscaled tables, however, preserve enough signal to identify whether they are relevant to a question. We exploit this asymmetry with a training-free, two-step method: the model first identifies the tables needed to answer a question from a pixel-compressed context, and then reasons over those at native resolution. On long documents, our method saves 41% of total tokens and gains 7 accuracy points over single-step QA with native resolution tables. It also uses 15% fewer tokens than the most efficient single-step compressed configuration, with no accuracy loss.

</details>


### [165] [From Atomic to Agentic: Towards Interpretable Evaluation of LLMs' Agentic Mathematical Capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26950)
*Jiayi Kuang,Yinghui Li,Yunze Song,Keyu Chen,Zhifeng Shen,Yangning Li,Yidong Wang,Di Yin,Ruizhi Qiao,Xing Sun,Kai Jin,Ying Shen,Liang Lin,Philip S. Yu*

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are evolving from performing end-to-end mathematical reasoning to integrating agentic intelligence. However, most existing math benchmarks evaluate only final answers. This outcome-oriented evaluation provides limited diagnostic value for identifying process-level failures or rigorous logic, failing to guide the transformation of LLMs into robust agents. To bridge this gap, we present a process-level benchmark designed to evaluate the inherent agentic mathematical reasoning abilities of LLMs. Our framework aligns problem-solving agentic behaviors with a structured taxonomy of reusable mathematical atomic capabilities. We design a comprehensive suite of planning, action, and feedback tasks across both textual and multimodal contexts, supported by an automated pipeline that synthesizes high-quality trajectories and produces fine-grained annotations via controlled LLM rewriting. Experiments reveal that models with similar end-to-end accuracy can exhibit markedly different agentic capability profiles. This demonstrates that process-level evaluation is crucial for interpreting the true potential of LLMs and guiding the development of next-generation mathematical agents.

</details>


### [166] [GraphMemix: Query-Aware Evidence Forests for Long-Term Multimodal Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26983)
*Geng Li,Yuhao Wang,Dong Li,Jianye Hao,Yuxin Peng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GraphMemix is a combinatorial-optimization graph memory framework that organizes long-term memory for multimodal agents by constructing query-aware evidence forests, addressing limitations of expensive offline summaries and naive embedding similarity matching that cause incomplete and redundant context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-term memory methods for multimodal agents suffer from either expensive question-agnostic offline summaries or naive embedding similarity matching that introduces incomplete and redundant context. There was a need for a more efficient and accurate memory organization approach.

Method: The proposed method consists of three key components: (1) Candidate graph construction - expands multi-view seed memories through schema and semantic relations to acquire query-aware original context; (2) Evidence utility and activation costs - decouples direct memory support from anchor-conditioned relation verification to suppress redundant or conflicting information; (3) Forest optimization - jointly selects a forest-format memory context under maximum evidence budget and its reliable relational structure.

Result: Experimental results across four long-term multimodal memory benchmarks demonstrate significant improvements with different foundation models and establish a new Pareto frontier between accuracy and lifecycle cost.

Conclusion: GraphMemix successfully organizes memory into query-relevant subgraphs, avoids substantial lifecycle cost, and recovers low-similarity complementary evidence, achieving better accuracy-efficiency trade-offs.

Abstract: Organizing long-term memory for multimodal agents remains challenging because existing methods either suffer from expensive question-agnostic offline summaries or naive embedding similarity matching that introduces incomplete and redundant context. To address these issues, we propose GraphMemix, a combinatorial-optimization graph memory framework that models memory organization as query-aware evidence-forest construction. Specifically, our method consists of three key components:(1) candidate graph construction, which expands multi-view seed memories through schema and semantic relations to acquire query-aware original context; (2) evidence utility and activation costs, which decouples direct memory support from anchor-conditioned relation verification to suppress redundant or conflicting information; and (3) forest optimization, which jointly selects a forest-format memory context under a maximum evidence budget and its reliable relational structure. By organizing memory into a query-relevant subgraph, the method avoids substantial lifecycle cost and recovers low-similarity complementary evidence. Experimental results across four long-term multimodal memory benchmarks demonstrate significant improvements with different foundation models and establish a new Pareto frontier between accuracy and lifecycle cost.

</details>

*Linsen Zhu,Yi Shi*
