Abstract: Large language models can summarize financial information, but an operational stock-research system must first assemble heterogeneous evidence, expose unavailable data and model capabilities, and control how generated opinions affect a final report. We present DSA, an evidence-aware orchestration framework for multi-market stock research with large language model (LLM) agents. DSA organizes the workflow into evidence acquisition, structured context construction, model-routed analysis, optional role and Strategy Skill reasoning, and report generation with selected context and diagnostics. A default report profile and an optional agentic profile share evidence and model-routing services but use profile-specific output validation and risk safeguards. In the agentic profile, core role outputs are processed by role-specific parsers, whereas Strategy Skill opinions undergo an additional signal-eligibility partition before synthesis; disagreement is supplied explicitly to the decision agent, followed by a conservative risk override. The reference implementation includes six regional market paths, fifteen bundled Strategy Skills, hosted and local model routes, and multiple execution and delivery surfaces. At a frozen software snapshot, a selected manifest of 1,457 portable offline backend contract tests passed; 596 cases were retrospectively mapped to six contract families central to the reported LLM-agent architecture. This evidence establishes implementation conformance for the tested software contracts, not superior report quality, forecasting accuracy, or investment returns.

</details>


### [168] [ASIL: Replacing Screenshot-and-Click with Structured State and Semantic Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26991)
*Rui Xie,Lu Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ASIL is an agent-native interface that replaces screenshot-and-click with structured JSON observations and code-executable semantic actions, accessing software through the deepest feasible API path. It achieves >80% success on 300 single-app and 80 multi-app tasks while using fewer than 5 actions per task, significantly outperforming GUI-based approaches (6.6-26.6% success) and application-native interfaces (28-38 points over LibreOffice UNO). The structured modality also improves training: SFT raises Qwen3.5-2B from 58.0 to 72.1 and Qwen3.5-9B from 66.6 to 80.4, with RL further improving to 74.4 and 82.2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing screenshot-and-click interfaces are inefficient for software-operating agents because screenshots are state-incomplete, GUI actions are brittle, semantically weak, and poorly matched to long-horizon planning. Many important applications remain accessible primarily through GUIs.

Method: ASIL exposes software through structured JSON observations and code-executable semantic actions, realized through the deepest feasible access path (deepest API) for each application. Instantiated across 15 applications with a benchmark of 300 single-application and 80 multi-application tasks.

Result: ASIL achieves >80% success with closed models while executing fewer than 5 actions per task. Under repaired runtime with 50-step screenshot budget, screenshot-and-click yields only 6.6% (single-app) and 26.6% (multi-app) strict success, rising to 15.0% and 53.3% on an easier OSWorld-comparable band. Against application-native interfaces, ASIL exceeds LibreOffice's UNO API by 28-38 strict points but only matches draw.io's MCP. Training with small-scale SFT improves Qwen3.5-2B from 58.0 to 72.1 and Qwen3.5-9B from 66.6 to 80.4, with on-policy RL further raising to 74.4 and 82.2.

Conclusion: ASIL demonstrates that agent-native structured interfaces significantly outperform traditional GUI-based approaches for software automation. The structured modality not only improves execution success but also enhances trainability, enabling substantial gains from both supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning, even with limited resources.

Abstract: Powerful code agents can execute scripts, call tools, and manage files, yet many important applications remain accessible primarily through graphical user interfaces. We argue that screenshot-and-click is an inefficient interface for software-operating agents: screenshots are state-incomplete, and GUI actions are brittle, semantically weak, and poorly matched to long-horizon planning. We introduce ASIL (Agent-Software Interaction Layer), an agent-native interface that exposes software through structured JSON observations and code-executable semantic actions, realized through the deepest feasible access path for each application. We instantiate ASIL across 15 applications and a benchmark of 300 single-application and 80 multi-application tasks. ASIL reaches above 80 with closed models while executing fewer than five actions per task. Under a repaired runtime and a 50-step screenshot budget, the same tasks yield 6.6 and 26.6 strict success under screenshot-and-click control, rising to 15.0 and 53.3 on an easier OSWorld-comparable band. Against application-native interfaces on matched tasks, ASIL exceeds LibreOffice's UNO API by 28-38 strict points but only matches draw.io's MCP content contract. The structured modality also suits training: small-scale SFT raises Qwen3.5-2B from 58.0 to 72.1 and Qwen3.5-9B from 66.6 to 80.4, and resource-limited on-policy RL further raises them to 74.4 and 82.2.

</details>


### [169] [A Multi-Modal AI Framework for Real-Time Queue Prediction, Management and Optimisation in Intelligent Border Control Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27010)
*Varvara Mama,Eleni Veroni,Nikolaos Kapsalis,Christos D. Nikolopoulos,Anargyros T. Baklezos*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes an AI-driven border control management system that uses LSTM networks for real-time queue prediction combined with Model Predictive Control (MPC) and scheduling optimization for resource allocation, outperforming traditional methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current border control queue management systems rely on static data and cannot adapt to dynamic traffic conditions, leading to inefficiencies and increased waiting times during uncertain or variable situations.

Method: The proposed multi-modal AI framework integrates heterogeneous data sources and uses Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks for queue forecasting. It employs Model Predictive Control (MPC) and scheduling optimization to generate actionable control policies for border control officers.

Result: The evaluation using synthetic data shows: 1) Queue prediction error reduced by up to 35%; 2) Average waiting time reduced by 30%; 3) Average throughput increased by nearly 20%, compared to ARIMA and rule-based methods.

Conclusion: Combining AI architectures (LSTM) with optimization techniques (MPC, scheduling) effectively enables proactive and adaptive border traffic management, significantly improving operational efficiency.

Abstract: In the present work an efficient border control management procedure is proposed. Compared to operational queue management systems, whose operations are based on mostly static data, the proposed work takes into account dynamic traffic conditions, thus enabling optimal performance, even in cases of uncertainty. To this end, we are proposing a multi-modal Artificial Intelligence (AI) framework, tailored to th needs of border control systems, which enables real-time queue prediction, management, and resource optimization. The novel proposed approach integrates heterogeneous data sources and presents them through a unified representation by employing Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks for queue forecasting. Furthermore, it leverages Model Predictive Control (MPC) and scheduling optimization to derive actionable control policies, which in turn can be presented to border control officers. The proposed work has been evaluated using synthetic data simulating realistic traffic. The evaluation results demonstrate that the proposed method reduces queue prediction error by up to 35% and average waiting time by 30%. Accordingly, the average throughput increases by nearly 20%, compared to ARIMA and rule-based methods. The abovementioned results show the effectiveness and efficiency of combining AI architectures with optimization techniques for proactive and adaptive border traffic management.

</details>


### [170] [Omni-Interactive Universal Embedder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27044)
*Wei-Yao Wang,Kazuya Tateishi,Shuyang Cui,Christian Simon,Takashi Shibuya,Shusuke Takahashi,Yuki Mitsufuji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes OmniUE, the first Omni-Interactive Universal Embedder that learns unified embeddings across text, video, and audio modalities using intermediate-layer representations from learnable tokens. It supports omni-interactive querying allowing users to input text, visual regions of interest, and audio spans. The method introduces the OmniCHOIR benchmark for evaluating omni-interactive compositional audio retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal representation learning has been shifting from traditional two-tower architectures to LLM-based embedders, but existing approaches primarily focus on language and image modalities. The authors aim to advance beyond this limitation by creating a universal embedder that supports all three major modalities (text, video, audio) and enables omni-interactive querying where users can provide inputs in any form.

Method: OmniUE leverages intermediate-layer representations from dedicated learnable tokens to create a unified embedding space across text, video, and audio. Visual and audio segmenters process diverse user interactions and integrate them with an omni-LLM to produce user-conditioned any-to-any embeddings via context aggregation. The key innovation is the combination of dedicated learnable tokens for intermediate-layer representations and context aggregation for omni-interactive querying.

Result: OmniUE consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across diverse modalities: 10.5% improvement on textual-interactive video benchmarks (MMEB-v2-video), 1.1% on audio tasks (MAEB), 83.7% on visual-interactive benchmarks (SCaR), and 24.1% on the omni-interactive OmniCHOIR benchmark.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that jointly advancing omni-modal representation learning and omni-interactive querying paves the way toward universal embedders. OmniUE successfully achieves unified embedding across three modalities while supporting flexible user interactions, representing a significant step toward truly universal multimodal embedding systems.

Abstract: Multimodal representation learning has been shifting from traditional two-tower architectures to large language model (LLM)-based embedders due to their strong instruction-following capabilities. Despite this progress, existing approaches primarily focus on language and image modalities, which also remain the dominant modalities for user-conditioned interactions in current embedders. In this paper, we propose the first Omni-Interactive Universal Embedder (OmniUE), which not only learns a unified embedding space across text, video, and audio by leveraging intermediate-layer representations from dedicated learnable tokens, but also supports omni-interactive querying, enabling users to provide inputs in the form of text, visual regions of interest, and audio spans. Within OmniUE, visual and audio segmenters process diverse user interactions and integrate them with an omni-LLM to produce user-conditioned any-to-any embeddings via context aggregation. To evaluate OmniUE's omni-interactive capabilities, we introduce OmniCHOIR, benchmarking models for omni-interactive compositional audio retrieval based on the given text, video, and audio as well as unimodal or multimodal interaction prompts. OmniUE consistently surpasses state-of-the-art baselines across diverse modalities, with average improvements of 10.5% on textual-interactive video benchmarks (MMEB-v2-video), 1.1% on audio tasks (MAEB), 83.7% on visual-interactive benchmarks (SCaR), and 24.1% on our omni-interactive OmniCHOIR benchmark. We believe that jointly advancing omni-modal representation learning and omni-interactive querying paves the way toward universal embedders.

</details>


### [171] [pro-team at LLMs4OL 2026 Tasks Flagship and Reuse: Retrieval-Augmented Generation and Vocabulary-Constrained Filtering for Ontology Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27101)
*Shivam Mishra,Dhannu Ram Meena,Muneendra Ojha,Krishna Pratap Singh,Kuldeep Singh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a system for the LLMs4OL 2026 Challenge addressing two ontology learning tasks: End-to-End Flagship Task (Task A) and Ontology Extension Reuse Task (Task B). The approach uses retrieval-augmented few-shot prompting with Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct, achieving strong results on Task B (0.8692 Semantic Graph Similarity, 0.9200 Term-Typing F1, 0.8540 Taxonomy Discovery F1) and Task A (0.7416 Semantic Graph Similarity), though limitations exist in extracting non-taxonomic relations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ontology learning from text remains challenging even with advanced LLMs due to three key problems: hallucination of domain terms, inconsistent output formats, and bias toward hierarchical relations over associative relations.

Method: The system employs an offline retrieval-augmented few-shot prompting pipeline using Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 for demonstration retrieval. Top-5 examples are selected for Task A and top-2 for Task B. A left-truncated context-windowing strategy preserves task instructions within long prompts. For Task B, vocabulary-constrained filtering retains triples when at least one endpoint belongs to the sample's closed term/type vocabulary, with duplicate removal from the initial ontology.

Result: Task B achieved 0.8692 Semantic Graph Similarity, 0.9200 Term-Typing F1, and 0.8540 Taxonomy Discovery F1. Task A achieved 0.7416 Semantic Graph Similarity. However, no non-taxonomic relations were extracted.

Conclusion: The approach demonstrates effective taxonomy-oriented ontology learning but is limited by closed, taxonomy-oriented relation vocabularies that prevent extraction of non-taxonomic relations, highlighting a key area for improvement in ontology learning systems.

Abstract: Ontology learning from text remains challenging despite significant progress in Large Language Models (LLMs), which can hallucinate domain terms, produce inconsistent formats, and favor hierarchical over associative relations. In the LLMs4OL 2026 Challenge, we address both the End-to-End Flagship Task (Task A) and Ontology Extension Reuse Task (Task B) using an offline retrieval-augmented few-shot prompting pipeline. Our system employs Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 for demonstration retrieval, selecting the top-5 examples for Task A and top-2 for Task B. A left-truncated context-windowing strategy preserves task instructions within long prompts. For Task B, generated triples undergo deterministic vocabulary-constrained filtering, retaining triples when at least one endpoint belongs to the sample's closed term/type vocabulary and removing duplicates of the initial ontology. The approach achieves Semantic Graph Similarity of 0.8692, Term-Typing F1 of 0.9200, and Taxonomy Discovery F1 of 0.8540 on Task B, while Task A achieves 0.7416 Semantic Graph Similarity. However, no non-taxonomic relations are extracted, highlighting limitations of closed, taxonomy-oriented relation vocabularies.

</details>


### [172] [LAAF: A Layered Accountability Architecture Framework for LLM Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27102)
*Prachi Chaturvedi,Shahnawaz Ahmad,Ehsan Nowroozi,Muhammad Waqas,George Loukas,Alireza Jolfaei,Lucas Cordeiro,Pierre Dantas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This systematic review examines accountability mechanisms for Large Language Models (LLMs) deployed in high-stakes environments (healthcare, legal, finance, public services). Following PRISMA guidelines, 122 primary studies and 12 regulatory documents were analyzed to develop an integrated accountability architecture (LAAF) with four mechanism families and a four-layer classification device, mapped to EU AI Act, NIST AI RMF, and ISO/IEC 42001.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly deployed in sensitive contexts where incorrect or ungrounded outputs can cause harm, yet accountability mechanisms remain unclear. The authors aim to consolidate existing research on LLM accountability into a coherent framework addressing who is answerable when harm occurs and how responsibility can be traced, explained, and acted upon.

Method: Systematic review following PRISMA guidance. Five databases were searched from January 2022 to March 2026. From 4,512 identified records, 122 primary studies and 12 regulatory/standards documents were included. The corpus was analyzed through a four-layer classification device (provenance, application logic, human oversight, governance and redress) cross-cut by traceability, role clarity, and continuous monitoring.

Result: The review consolidates accountability as an actor-forum relation resolved into five dimensions, with mechanisms synthesized across four families: technical controls, human oversight, organisational governance, and documentation and traceability (each with maturity assessment). Four persistent gaps were identified: under-specification of human oversight, absence of shared accountability metrics, disciplinary disconnection, and limited empirical evaluation, alongside five unresolved structural tensions.

Conclusion: The review proposes LAAF (LLM Accountability Architecture Framework), an integrated accountability architecture synthesized from the surveyed evidence (not validated), aligned with OWASP LLM Top 10 (2025). It maps findings onto EU AI Act (high-risk obligations effective August 2026), NIST AI RMF with Generative AI Profile, ISO/IEC 42001, and sectoral guidance in healthcare, finance, education, and public sector. The framework remains a synthesis rather than a validated artefact, highlighting the need for further empirical evaluation and disciplinary integration.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) operate in hospitals, courtrooms, banks, and public service desks, where fluent, confident outputs are treated as authoritative even when ungrounded or incorrect. When such an output contributes to harm, who is answerable, and through what mechanisms can responsibility be traced, explained, and acted upon? Following PRISMA guidance, five databases were searched from January 2022 to March 2026 against four review questions; of 4,512 records identified, 122 primary studies were included, together with 12 regulatory and standards documents analysed as primary sources. The review consolidates a sociotechnical account of accountability as an actor-forum relation resolved into five dimensions, and synthesises mechanisms across four families: technical controls, human oversight, organisational governance, and documentation and traceability, each with a maturity assessment. The corpus is read through a four-layer classification device spanning provenance, application logic, human oversight, and governance and redress, cross-cut by traceability, role clarity, and continuous monitoring. Both are mapped onto the EU AI Act, whose high-risk obligations have applied since 2 August 2026, the NIST AI RMF with its Generative AI Profile, ISO/IEC 42001, and sectoral guidance in healthcare, consumer finance, education, and the public sector. Four persistent gaps emerge: under-specification of human oversight, absence of shared accountability metrics, disciplinary disconnection, and limited empirical evaluation, alongside five structural tensions that no surveyed instrument resolves. The review closes by consolidating the classification device into an integrated accountability architecture, LAAF, with cybersecurity aligned to the OWASP LLM Top 10 (2025); it is a synthesis of the surveyed evidence rather than a validated artefact.

</details>


### [173] [TransMeme: A Multi-Agent Framework for Cross-Cultural Meme Transcreation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27127)
*Jingyi Zheng,Yule Liu,Zifan Peng,Tianyi Hu,Yuemeng Zhao,Xinhu Zheng,Xinlei He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a multi-agent framework for cross-cultural meme transcreation between Chinese and English, addressing the challenge of adapting internet memes across linguistic and cultural boundaries while preserving communicative intent, cultural适配, and multimodal consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Internet memes are a pervasive form of multimodal communication but often fail to cross cultural and linguistic boundaries. Existing translation methods cannot handle the unique requirements of meme adaptation, which involve preserving intent, adapting culture-specific meanings, and maintaining text-image coherence simultaneously.

Method: The authors propose a multi-agent framework with specialized agents coordinated to address three identified challenges: (1) cultural adaptation agent for culture-specific knowledge, (2) target text rewriting agent for intent/tone preservation, (3) revision agent for multimodal consistency, and (4) conditional visual adjustment. The framework uses coordinated feedback to handle difficult cases requiring deeper intervention.

Result: The framework was evaluated on bidirectional Chinese-English meme transcreation using human evaluation and LLM-as-a-Judge. It achieved a 33.1% average improvement over the strongest baseline in human evaluation across all four dimensions, and attained the highest Top-1 ranking rate of 60% (vs. 26% for the second-best) in LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation.

Conclusion: The proposed multi-agent framework successfully addresses cross-cultural meme transcreation challenges. Error analysis reveals remaining bottlenecks in humor reconstruction and image-text alignment rather than simple cultural knowledge gaps, indicating future work should focus on humor transfer mechanisms.

Abstract: Internet memes are a pervasive form of multimodal online communication; however, such communication often involves users from diverse linguistic and cultural backgrounds. Therefore, adapting memes across cultures and languages is a central challenge for enabling mutual understanding in online communication. Unlike ordinary translation or standalone text rewriting, cross-cultural meme transcreation must jointly preserve communicative intent, adapt culture-dependent meaning for the target audience, and maintain coherence between text and image. In this work, we first provide an explicit task analysis of cross-cultural meme transcreation and identify three core challenges: culture-specific knowledge understanding, intent and tone preservation, and multimodal consistency. Based on this analysis, we propose a multi-agent framework with specialized agents that are coordinated to address these challenges through cultural adaptation, target text rewriting, revision, and conditional visual adjustment. The framework strengthens target text adaptation with coordinated feedback to handle difficult cases that require deeper cultural or visual intervention. We evaluate the framework on bidirectional Chinese-English meme transcreation using both human evaluation and LLM-as-a-Judge. Our method consistently outperforms all baselines across both evaluation settings. In human evaluation, it achieves the best performance on all four dimensions and delivers a 33.1% average improvement over the strongest baseline, while in LLM-as-a-Judge, it attains the highest Top-1 ranking rate (60% versus 26% for the second-best baseline). Further analysis indicates that each component contributes to the performance. Our error analysis suggests that the remaining bottlenecks lie in humor reconstruction and image-text alignment rather than simple cultural knowledge gaps, pointing to future work on humor transfer.

</details>


### [174] [GRAIN: Bridging Name and Narrative Shifts in Real-World Graph Reasoning through Invariance-Rewarded Agentic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27142)
*Zike Yuan,Han Zhang,Jianzhi Yan,Le Liu,Cai Ke,Huozhi Zhou,Jian Xie,Jiran Yin,Yukun Cao,Yue Yu,Hui Wang,Ming Liu,Bing Qin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GRAIN is a single-agent reinforcement learning framework that addresses LLMs' brittleness to real-world linguistic shifts in graph tasks by using a Structure Invariance Reward to validate extracted graphs against ground-truth topologies, forcing robust text-to-structure mappings rather than surface pattern memorization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are brittle to real-world shifts in node identifiers and task formulation, overfitting to surface patterns. Deterministic graph tools are invariant to such shifts but struggle with noisy text extraction. Multi-agent systems mitigate parsing failures but incur prohibitive latency.

Method: GRAIN models reasoning as a semantic parsing and tool-execution pipeline, guided by a Structure Invariance Reward that validates extracted intermediate graphs against ground-truth topologies. This forces the LLM to learn robust text-to-structure mappings rather than memorizing linguistic artifacts.

Result: GRAIN outperforms multi-agent baselines by 16.45% in accuracy with approximately 24% lower latency. It halves the out-of-distribution (OOD) gap of SFT models (from 15.77% to 7.80%) and maintains robustness on large-scale graphs beyond the training distribution.

Conclusion: GRAIN demonstrates that single-agent reinforcement learning with structure invariance rewards can achieve superior performance and robustness compared to multi-agent systems, with significantly lower latency while maintaining structural generalization on OOD graphs.

Abstract: Despite their potential in standardized graph tasks, Large Language Models (LLMs) remain brittle to real-world shifts in node identifiers and task formulation. While deterministic graph tools are invariant to such shifts, extracting topological structures from noisy text is highly fragile for LLMs, which often overfit to surface patterns. Moreover, mitigating these parsing failures via multi-agent systems incurs prohibitive latency. To address this, we propose GRAIN, a single-agent framework optimized via reinforcement learning. GRAIN models reasoning as a semantic parsing and tool-execution pipeline, guided by a Structure Invariance Reward. By validating extracted intermediate graphs against ground-truth topologies, this reward forces the LLM to learn robust text-to-structure mappings rather than memorizing linguistic artifacts. We also introduce GRIT, a benchmark evaluating sensitivity to such linguistic shifts. GRAIN outperforms multi-agent baselines by 16.45\% in accuracy with approximately 24\% lower latency. Furthermore, it demonstrates superior structural generalization, halving the out-of-distribution (OOD) gap of SFT models (from 15.77\% to 7.80\%) and maintaining robustness on large-scale graphs beyond the training distribution.

</details>


### [175] [Feature Transformation Enhanced Jacobi Polynomial Graph Filtering for Graph Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27144)
*Xiang Wang,Zhijun Cheng,Zhenyu Meng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: JPGFN (Feature Transformation Enhanced Jacobi Polynomial Graph Filtering Network) is a novel graph anomaly detection method that addresses limitations of existing frequency-domain filtering approaches through three key components: Feature Separation Transformation Network (FSTNN) for learning fine-grained node features, adaptive Jacobi polynomial graph filtering for capturing complex frequency-domain features, and a node label constraint module for leveraging node labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph anomaly detection methods based on frequency-domain filtering face three major challenges: (1) using static basic functions that cannot adapt to frequency-domain distribution of graph data; (2) failing to consider importance information of each attribute in node feature vectors, leading to loss of fine-grained information; (3) insufficiently utilizing node labels for GAD.

Method: The proposed JPGFN consists of three main components: (1) Feature Separation Transformation Network (FSTNN) - learns fine-grained node features through feature separation and nonlinear transformations across different dimensions; (2) Adaptive Jacobi polynomial graph filtering module - uses Jacobi polynomials to adaptively capture complex frequency-domain features of graph signals; (3) Node label constraint module - facilitates the use of node labels to enhance GAD performance.

Result: Experimental results on multiple real-world datasets demonstrate that the proposed method significantly outperforms mainstream approaches in graph anomaly detection tasks.

Conclusion: The JPGFN method effectively addresses the three identified challenges in frequency-domain graph anomaly detection by incorporating feature transformation, adaptive Jacobi polynomial filtering, and node label constraints, achieving superior detection performance compared to existing methods.

Abstract: In recent years, graph anomaly detection (GAD) based on frequency-domain filtering have achieved promising results. However, existing approaches still face three major challenges: First, they use static basic function to constructed graph filter which cannot effectively adapt to the frequency-domain distribution of graph data. Second, they fail to adequately consider the importance information of each attribute in the node feature vector, leading to the loss of fine-grained information. Third, they insufficiently utilize node labels for GAD. To address these issues, this paper proposes a novel graph anomaly detection method called JPGFN (Feature Transformation Enhanced Jacobi Polynomial Graph Filtering Network). First, a Feature Separation Transformation Network (FSTNN) is developed to better learn fine-grained node features by feature separation and applying nonlinear transformations to node features across different dimensions. Second, an adaptive Jacobi polynomial graph filtering module is constructed based on Jacobi polynomials to adaptively capture complex frequency-domain features of graph signals. Finally, a node label constraint module is developed to facilitate the use of node labels and enhance the performance of GAD. Experimental results on multiple real-world datasets demonstrate that the proposed method significantly outperforms mainstream approaches.

</details>


### [176] [Thomson: Continual Learning of Frontier Models for SovereignAI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27147)
*Shengzhuang Chen,Jerrod Parker,Yejin Bang,Andrew M. Bean,Nabeel Seedat,Stefan Winzeck,Daniil Glazko,Jannik Zgraggen,Fangyi Yu,Scott Arnott,Dietrich Trautmann,Luca Ciuffreda,Guglielmo Bonifazi,Davide Romano,Bradley Bell,Kirsty Fielding,Daniele Giofrè,Tom Zielund,Ipshita Chatterjee,Sneha Murthy Ghantasala,Manpreet Nanreh,John Scoville,Maciej Sakowicz,Wassim Seifeddine,Lukas Thede,Jonathan Richard Schwarz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes that frontier AI performance can be achieved by a wide range of institutions through Continual Learning on open-weight models, rather than being limited to heavily funded players. The authors demonstrate this with Thomson, a general-purpose frontier model trained using an enhanced mid- & post-training stack with safeguards that preserve plasticity and stability. Thomson performs competitively across agentic tasks, safety, legal, tax, multilingualism, and large-scale Deep Research, showing distinct improvements across capabilities while eliminating the forgetting problem common to narrow domain adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The development of frontier models is perceived as the exclusive remit of a small number of heavily funded players, creating information, economic, and power asymmetry between developers and AI users. While public discourse calls for SovereignAI (an organization's capability to independently build, deploy and govern AI), there is little concrete guidance on how to achieve this under diverse funding settings. The authors aim to challenge this perception and demonstrate that frontier performance is achievable by a wider range of institutions.

Method: The approach uses Continual Learning on readily available open-weight models, exploiting a modern mid- & post-training stack. Unlike limited approaches such as small-scale fine-tuning, prompt engineering, or tool-augmentation of frozen models, this method introduces safeguards that preserve both plasticity and stability at each stage, making minimal high-impact interventions on parameters. The goal is to achieve gains comparable to those across multiple model generations at substantially lower compute and personnel budgets.

Result: The authors demonstrate their approach with Thomson, a general-purpose frontier model trained with emphasis on high-stakes professional work. Evaluations show Thomson performs competitively with recent frontier models across agentic tasks, safety, legal, tax & multilingualism, and large-scale Deep Research. The model exhibits a distinctive π-shaped pattern: distinct improvements across a wide range of capabilities (including those not explicitly targeted), while almost completely eliminating the forgetting problem common to narrow domain adaptation.

Conclusion: The paper argues that ownership of large parts of the SovereignAI stack (model, tool infrastructure, values & data privacy) is viable for far more actors than commonly thought, at compute and personnel budgets substantially lower than industry assumptions. The key innovation is the preservation of both plasticity (ability to learn new capabilities) and stability (preserving existing knowledge) during continual learning, achieving gains across multiple capability dimensions while avoiding catastrophic forgetting.

Abstract: The development of frontier models is commonly perceived to be the exclusive remit of a small number of heavily funded players, creating an information, economic and power asymmetry between developers and the diverse user base of modern AI. Recent public discourse acknowledges this concern, calling for SovereignAI (an organisation's capability to independently build, deploy and govern AI use), but offers little concrete advice on how this can be achieved in the short term under a diversity of funding settings. We argue that frontier performance is achievable by a wide range of institutions through Continual Learning on readily available open-weight models. Unlike limited approaches such as small-scale fine-tuning, prompt engineering, or tool-augmentation of a frozen model, our approach exploits a modern mid- & post-training stack while introducing safeguards that preserve both plasticity and stability at each stage, making the minimal number of high-impact interventions on the parameters. This yields gains comparable to those typically seen across multiple successive model generations, at compute and personnel budgets substantially lower than commonly thought, making ownership of large parts of the SovereignAI stack (model, tool infrastructure, values & data privacy) viable for far more actors. We demonstrate this with Thomson, a general-purpose frontier model trained with an enhanced focus on high-stakes professional work. Thomson performs competitively with recent frontier models across agentic tasks, safety, legal, tax & multilingualism, and large-scale Deep Research. Evaluations show a distinctive $π$-shaped pattern: distinct improvements across a wide range of capabilities, including those not explicitly targeted, while almost completely eliminating the forgetting problem common to narrow domain adaptation.

</details>


### [177] [BPMN4CAI: A BPMN Extension for Modeling Dynamic Conversational AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27149)
*Björn-Lennart Eger,Daniel Rose,Barbara Dinter*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper develops BPMN4CAI, a standard-compliant extension of BPMN to represent dynamic, context-sensitive Conversational AI interactions within business processes, using Design Science Research methodology and evaluated through a case study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The established BPMN standard faces challenges in representing dynamic, context-sensitive interactions of Conversational AI systems (chatbots and virtual assistants) which are becoming increasingly important to digital business processes.

Method: Design Science Research methodology was used to systematically extend existing BPMN elements and incorporate specialized components to create BPMN4CAI framework.

Result: The BPMN4CAI extension facilitates adaptive decision-making processes, robust context management, and transparent interactions for Conversational AI within business processes.

Conclusion: The applicability and relevance of the BPMN4CAI framework is demonstrated and evaluated through a case study, showing its effectiveness for modeling Conversational AI in business processes.

Abstract: Conversational AI systems, such as chatbots and virtual assistants, are becoming increasingly important to digital business processes. However, the established Business Process Model and Notation (BPMN) standard faces challenges when representing dynamic, context-sensitive interactions. This paper addresses this methodological and practical research gap by developing a standard-compliant BPMN extension (BPMN4CAI). Using Design Science Research methodology, this paper develops an approach that systematically extends existing BPMN elements and incorporates specialized components. The applicability and relevance of the BPMN4CAI framework are demonstrated and evaluated through a case study. The results show that the BPMN4CAI extension facilitates adaptive decision-making processes, robust context management, and transparent interactions for Conversational AI within business processes.

</details>


### [178] [Calibrated Enough to Know, Not Calibrated to Act: Fabricated Evidence Makes LLM Agents Commit to the Unknowable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27167)
*Pranav Aggarwal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agents dramatically increase commitment to directional calls when shown professional-looking market panels, even when the panel data is entirely fabricated. This "act/don't-act gate" failure is trainable but context-fragile.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate why LLM agents commit to actions on provably unpredictable questions when shown professional-looking market panels, and whether this behavior can be corrected.

Method: Tested 12 frontier models across multiple conditions: escalating evidence (bare question to professional panel), fabricated vs. genuine market data, matched answerable questions, probability assessments, and judgment classification tasks. Trained a 3B model on 540 synthetic cases to fix the gate failure.

Result: Commitment rises from 6.5% to 54.0% with escalating evidence. Even fully fabricated panels (24.5% to 36.8%) are statistically indistinguishable from genuine data (37.6%). Models answer correctly on answerable questions (near-perfect accuracy). Probabilities barely move across conditions. Supervised fine-tuning reduces commitment to 0.0% and transfers to unseen domains, but rigid formats that remove reasoning room cause confident errors.

Conclusion: The act/don't-act gate fails, not due to incapacity, belief, or missing judgment. The gate is trainable but context-fragile - it holds when response format allows reasoning but fails with rigid formats. Deployment requires both qualities.

Abstract: An LLM agent shown a professional-looking market panel commits to a directional call on a provably unpredictable question far more often than one asked the bare question: across 12 frontier models, commitment rises from 6.5% to 54.0% as evidence is escalated. It commits just as readily when every number on the panel is invented: fabricating the entire display, so nothing the model can see is true except the question itself, still lifts commitment from 24.5% to 36.8%, statistically indistinguishable from the 37.6% produced by genuine market data. What unlocks confident action is not information but the authority of its packaging. The failure is narrow and locatable. Incapacity is not the answer: on matched answerable questions attached to the same panels, the same models answer essentially always, at near-perfect accuracy. Nor is it belief - stated probabilities barely move across the gradient that swings action by 48 points, and score worse than a climatological baseline. Missing judgment isn't it either: asked to classify a question's knowability before acting, models call it irreducible 90% of the time and then commit on just 0.4% of those. The act/don't-act gate is what fails, and the effect is concentrated in a few models rather than universal. Because the gate is separable, it can be trained. Supervised fine-tuning of a 3B model on 540 synthetic cases, predominantly dice, coins, jars and timers, drives commitment to 0.0% on the original cases and transfers to three unseen domains. It does not survive everything: the gate holds exactly when the response format leaves room to reason, and rigid formats that remove that room leave the model confident and wrong on questions it otherwise answers correctly. The gate is trainable and context-fragile, and deployment needs both halves of that sentence.

</details>


### [179] [What Makes Good Agentic Data? An ACE Lens on Data Generation for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27260)
*Xingshan Zeng,Zishan Xu,Boju Zhang,Yuzhou Wu,Lingzhi Wang,Jianghao Lin,Liangyou Li,Yasheng Wang,Lifeng Shang,Xin Jiang,Weinan Zhang,Yong Yu,Qun Liu,Weiwen Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper develops a two-level framework for understanding and organizing agentic data generation in LLM agents. The first level factorizes agentic data into (E, q, τ, v) representing environment, task, interaction, and verifier. The second level uses an ACE (Accuracy-Complexity-divErsity) lens to formulate generation as constrained distribution design, where Accuracy ensures grounded and consistent data, Complexity aligns with learner capabilities, and divErsity controls coverage and redundancy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The field of LLM agent data generation lacks a unified framework. Existing work is organized by domain with heterogeneous evaluation, obscuring common generation mechanisms and conflating candidate construction with verification and selection. There is a need to systematically organize generation paradigms and clarify the distinction between what can be generated versus what should be generated.

Method: The authors develop a two-level framework: (1) Representing agentic data as a factorized object (E, q, τ, v) comprising environment specification, task signal, interaction realization, and optional verifier. (2) Formulating generation as constrained distribution design through the ACE lens - Accuracy (feasible support of grounded data), Complexity (learning mass relative to learner capability), and divErsity (coverage and redundancy control). Using this framework, they analyze how prior work verifies experience, constructs difficulty, and expands behavioral coverage.

Result: The literature analysis reveals a shift toward: execution-grounded accuracy (data verified through actual environment interaction), learner-relative complexity (difficulty calibrated to specific learner capabilities), and diversity beyond surface variation or dataset size (meaningful behavioral coverage). The framework identifies the central challenge as allocating valid, informative, and non-redundant experience as agents and environments evolve, rather than simply generating more data.

Conclusion: The ACE framework provides a unifying lens for understanding agentic data generation. The field is moving toward more principled approaches that balance accuracy, complexity, and diversity. Future directions include scaling considerations, new data sources, training regimes, and adaptive learning approaches. The key insight is that the challenge lies not in data quantity but in the continual allocation of high-quality, relevant experience.

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly rely on generated interaction data to learn how to interact with external environments. Agentic data generation must maintain consistency among environments, tasks, interactions, and success signals while producing experience that is useful rather than merely abundant. Existing work spans many agent domains, but domain-centered organization and heterogeneous evaluation often obscure common generation mechanisms and conflate candidate construction with verification and selection. This work develops a two-level framework for the field. First, we represent agentic data as a common factorized object $(E,q,τ,v)$, comprising an environment specification, task signal, interaction realization, and optional verifier. We organize generation paradigms by their primary anchor and dependency structure. Second, we formulate generation as constrained distribution design through the Accuracy-Complexity-divErsity (ACE) lens. Accuracy establishes the feasible support of grounded and internally consistent data. Within this support, Complexity places learning mass relative to the capability of a declared learner and execution configuration, while divErsity controls coverage and redundancy of data. Using this framework, we explore how prior work verifies generated experience, constructs and calibrates difficulty, and expands behavioral coverage. The literature reveals a shift toward execution-grounded accuracy, learner-relative complexity, and diversity beyond surface variation or dataset size. We further discuss broader directions and emerging trends in agentic data generation through the ACE lens, including their implications for scaling, data sources, training regimes and adaptive learning. Overall, the central challenge is not simply to generate more data, but to continually allocate valid, informative, and non-redundant experience as agents and environments evolve.

</details>


### [180] [Naive Prompt Optimization: Rethinking the Need for Complex Prompt Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27266)
*Yuan Chang,Xiaoqi Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Naive Prompt Optimization (NPO), a lightweight single-lineage method that iteratively revises prompts using a teacher model with rollout feedback. NPO achieves comparable or better performance than the complex GEPA optimizer with fewer rollouts, and its advantage increases with stronger teacher models. The method shows competitive performance in interactive games and its optimized prompts can transfer to other student models, especially within the same model family.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses the challenge of efficiently improving autonomous agents across diverse tasks to accelerate recursive self-improvement (RSI) in agentic AI. While prompt optimization can deliver performance gains comparable to fine-tuning with lower computational costs, recent trends favor unnecessarily complex optimizers. The authors seek to demonstrate that simple, lightweight approaches can rival sophisticated search procedures.

Method: NPO is a lightweight single-lineage prompt optimization method that iteratively revises prompts using a teacher model with rollout feedback. The approach uses a teacher model to generate prompt improvements based on rollout feedback, without requiring complex search procedures or multi-lineage exploration.

Result: NPO achieves comparable or better performance than GEPA with fewer rollouts. Its advantage increases with stronger teacher models, suggesting that stronger teacher reasoning can substitute for optimizer-side search complexity. In interactive games, NPO remains broadly competitive with GEPA, while GRPO performs better on some tasks less amenable to prompt optimization. NPO-optimized prompts elicit similar performance improvements when transferred to other student models, especially across models within the same family.

Conclusion: Simple, linear prompt optimization can rival substantially more sophisticated and complex search procedures. The findings suggest that complexity in optimizer design may be unnecessary, and that leveraging stronger teacher models can partially compensate for lack of search complexity.

Abstract: Efficiently improving autonomous agents across diverse tasks is central to accelerating recursive self-improvement (RSI) in agentic AI, with prompt optimization emerging as a promising approach capable of delivering performance gains comparable to those achieved by fine-tuning model weights, while reducing computational costs in both optimization and serving. However, recent developments increasingly favor unnecessarily complex prompt optimizers. We introduce Naive Prompt Optimization (NPO), a lightweight single-lineage method that iteratively revises prompts using a teacher model with rollout feedback. NPO achieves comparable or better performance than GEPA with fewer rollouts, and its advantage increases with stronger teacher models, suggesting that stronger teacher reasoning can partially substitute for optimizer-side search complexity. In interactive games, NPO remains broadly competitive with GEPA, while GRPO performs better on some tasks less amenable to prompt optimization. We also show that NPO-optimized prompts elicit similar performance improvements when applied verbatim to other student models, especially across models within the same family. Overall, our preliminary results show that simple, linear prompt optimization can rival substantially more sophisticated and complex search procedures.

</details>


### [181] [BrailleBench: Investigating Multi-Criteria Braille Comprehension in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27268)
*Jinghan Zhang,Fengran Mo,Zhiyu Chen,Xiaoyan Han,Kunpeng Liu,Chang-Tien Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Although Large language models (LLMs) mediate access to knowledge and computational assistance, their capabilities should benefit vulnerable groups in the same way. However, it is unclear whether existing AI systems are inclusive enough for blind and deafblind users to access the same functionality through Braille, whose indicators, contractions, and digital representations introduce distinct requirements for model comprehension. To this end, we introduce BrailleBench, a benchmark for evaluating LLMs in Braille comprehension from different Criteria. BrailleBench aligns 5,570 instances from five datasets, including mathematics, commonsense, and multi-hop question answering across English and Braille Grades 1 and 2. Different configurations are designed to understand whether the systems can comprehend Braille-authored content, express answers in Braille, and complete end-to-end Braille interaction. To ensure the quality and prevent evaluation bias, the benchmark is built through a deterministic, expert-reviewed pipeline via a self-created Braille Toolkit without using any data instances generated by LLMs. We evaluate six representative LLMs from various aspects. The results reveal a persistent gap between print-English capability and Braille accessibility. Braille understanding and expression are asymmetric, where Grade 2 is especially fragile on the input side compared to Grade 1, and fully Braille requests further reduce performance. The experimental observations provide valuable guidance for the development of future Braille AI systems. All related resources in BrailleBench are publicly available for future research.

</details>


### [182] [LLMs Can Design Near-Optimal OR Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27296)
*Jackie Baek*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: We ask whether large language models (LLMs) can design effective algorithms for well-specified operations research (OR) problems. We study inventory control, queueing network control, and assortment optimization. We evaluate two levels of LLM use: at level 1, the model receives one problem instance and returns a solution for that instance; at level 2, it receives only the problem class description and broad parameter ranges, and returns an algorithm that maps instance parameters to solutions. Human input is minimal: we give one untuned prompt that describes the problem, and the model has access to a Python sandbox tool with a fixed compute budget.
  The strongest model we test, gpt-5.6-sol, matches or outperforms the best existing method on almost all evaluated instances. This holds even at level 2, where the returned algorithm is fixed before seeing the evaluation instances. Performance also improves sharply across models released less than eight months apart, suggesting that this capability is moving quickly. Thus, for the well-specified operations problems we study, a single untuned LLM query can already produce algorithms competitive with specialized methods. These results suggest that frontier LLMs can be a serious empirical baseline for algorithm design in well-specified OR problems.

</details>


### [183] [Verify Smarter, Evolve Further: Efficient Harness Evolution through Behavior-Aware Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27311)
*Jinghan Xu,Yikai Zhang,Aili Chen,Weiyuan Li,Jiaqing Liang,Deqing Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HarnessLens is a budget-aware framework for automated evolution of agent harnesses (instructions, tools, runtime components for language-model agents). It addresses costly verification problems in existing methods by selectively verifying candidates on behavior-relevant tasks using an attributable-evidence gate, achieving 7.6-13.6% performance improvements with less evaluation budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing propose-and-verify methods for harness adaptation waste rollouts on unrelated behaviors and allow aggregate scores to obscure specific regressions, making verification costly and inefficient.

Method: HarnessLens jointly explores task space and user-configurable components, derives candidate modifications from execution trajectories, and uses an attributable-evidence gate to selectively verify each candidate only on behavior-relevant tasks.

Result: Across three agent harnesses and four benchmarks, HarnessLens improves average held-out performance by 7.6-13.6% while consuming substantially less evaluation budget than competing baselines.

Conclusion: Behavior-aware verification with explicit attribution enables more reliable and sample-efficient harness evolution under constrained interaction budgets.

Abstract: Agent harnesses shape how language-model agents use instructions, tools, and runtime components, but adapting these harnesses requires costly verification. Existing propose-and-verify methods typically score every candidate on a fixed task set, wasting rollouts on unrelated behaviors and allowing aggregate scores to obscure specific regressions. We introduce HarnessLens, a budget-aware framework for automated harness evolution. HarnessLens jointly explores the task space and user-configurable components, derives candidate modifications from execution trajectories, and selectively verifies each candidate on behavior-relevant tasks using an attributable-evidence gate. Across three agent harnesses and four benchmarks, HarnessLens improves average held-out performance by 7.6-13.6% while consuming substantially less evaluation budget than competing baselines. These results demonstrate that behavior-aware verification with explicit attribution enables more reliable and sample-efficient harness evolution under constrained interaction budgets. Our code is available at https://github.com/jhxu5214/HarnessLens.

</details>


### [184] [Not All Eval-Awareness Is Equal: Capabilities Framing Predicts Compliance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27340)
*Allison Zhuang,Santiago Aranguri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper challenges the assumption that eval-awareness (a model's recognition of being tested) is a single, uniform quantity. It demonstrates that eval-awareness manifests in different "flavors" (capabilities-flavored vs safety-flavored) that have opposing implications for model compliance, with aggregate suppression metrics potentially masking qualitatively different behavioral outcomes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current safety evaluation pipelines treat eval-awareness as a single quantity to be suppressed. This paper questions that assumption by investigating whether eval-awareness is behaviorally uniform or whether different framings of eval-awareness lead to different outcomes.

Method: The authors analyzed verbalized eval-awareness in chain-of-thought reasoning on Qwen3-32B over the FORTRESS dataset, categorizing instances as capabilities-flavored ("testing my ability"), safety-flavored ("testing my boundaries"), both, or neither. They conducted CoT-prefill interventions on eval-awareness-negative rollouts to test causality, with 11 prefills tested.

Result: Capabilities-framing predicts compliance with a +24 to +46 percentage-point gap over safety-framing across all tested steering conditions. Of 11 CoT-prefill interventions, 10 shifted compliance in the predicted direction, suggesting a causal link.

Conclusion: Eval-awareness is not behaviorally uniform. Aggregate suppression rates can move while the safety-relevant component remains unchanged, and the same "X% suppression of eval-awareness" can correspond to qualitatively different behavioral outcomes. This finding has important implications for safety evaluation pipelines.

Abstract: Steering interventions targeting eval-awareness, a model's recognition that it is being tested, are increasingly used in safety evaluation pipelines, where evaluation-awareness is treated as a single quantity to be suppressed. We show that verbalized eval-awareness in chain-of-thought can be identified as capabilities-flavored ("the user is testing my ability to follow instructions"), safety-flavored ("the user is testing my boundaries"), both, or neither: framings that predict compliance very differently. On Qwen3-32B over the FORTRESS dataset, capabilities-framing predicts compliance with a +24 to +46 percentage-point gap over safety-framing across all tested steering conditions. A CoT-prefill intervention on eval-awareness-negative rollouts suggests the link is causal, with 10 of 11 prefills shifting compliance in the predicted direction. Then, eval-awareness is not behaviorally uniform: aggregate suppression rates can move while the safety-relevant component does not, and the same "X% suppression of eval-awareness" can correspond to qualitatively different behavioral outcomes.

</details>


### [185] [Sophistication in GenAI Use: Field Evidence from a Large Firm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27364)
*Nicholas J. Hallman,Zachary T. Kowaleski,Anu Puvvada,Jaime J. Schmidt*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study examines how sophistication in generative AI (genAI) usage varies among nearly 4,000 back-office employees across 15 functional areas in a large firm, using 713,564 employee prompts and LLM responses over eight months in 2025. The research finds that senior employees demonstrate more sophisticated genAI use, sophistication varies significantly across functions (highest in Strategy, Digital Innovation, and Project Management), and neither time nor formal AI training leads to lasting improvements in sophistication.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding how employees actually use generative AI in workplace settings is critical for managers seeking to improve AI adoption and outcomes. Despite the rapid adoption of genAI tools, there is limited empirical evidence on how sophistication of AI use varies across employee groups, functions, and time. This study addresses this gap by providing large-scale, proprietary data on actual genAI usage patterns in a corporate setting.

Method: The researchers analyzed proprietary data comprising 713,564 employee prompts and corresponding large language model responses from 3,987 back-office employees across 15 functional areas over an eight-month period in 2025. They measured "sophistication" of genAI use and examined variations by employee seniority, functional area, and time, including before and after formal AI training programs.

Result: Three main findings emerge: (1) Senior employees exhibit more sophisticated genAI use, supporting the hypothesis that domain expertise complements genAI capabilities; (2) Sophistication varies considerably across functions, with highest levels in Strategy, Digital Innovation, and Project Management—groups focused on firmwide strategic initiatives and organizational change; (3) Neither improvements in sophistication over time nor lasting improvements following formal AI training are observed, suggesting sophisticated use is difficult to develop or change.

Conclusion: The study provides actionable measures and insights into sophisticated genAI use for both managers and researchers. The findings suggest that sophisticated AI use is more likely to emerge from employees with strong domain expertise rather than through training interventions. Organizations should consider these patterns when designing AI adoption strategies and should not expect formal training to dramatically improve genAI sophistication levels.

Abstract: We study how sophistication in generative AI (genAI) use varies among the back-office workforce of a large firm. Using proprietary data, we observe 713,564 employee prompts and their corresponding large language model responses from nearly 4,000 back-office employees across 15 functional areas over eight months in 2025. We document three main findings. First, senior employees exhibit more sophisticated genAI use, consistent with domain expertise complementing genAI capabilities. Second, sophistication varies considerably across functions and is highest in Strategy, Digital Innovation, and Project Management, three groups that share a focus on firmwide strategic initiatives and organizational change. Third, we observe neither improvements in sophistication over time nor lasting improvements following formal AI training, suggesting that sophisticated use can be difficult to change. Together, our study provides measures of and insights into sophisticated genAI use that managers can use to improve outcomes and that researchers can use in future research.

</details>


### [186] [CorporateBench: Large-Scale Q&A Benchmarking with Temporal Knowledge Bases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27391)
*Sil Hamilton,Albert Yu Sun,Oscar J. Romero,Carl-Leander Henneking,David Mimno,Bishan Yang,Igor Labutov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CorporateBench (CB) is a large-scale multi-task Q&A benchmark for evaluating LLMs on corporate communication datasets, featuring over 230,000 documents across four synthetically generated firms. It addresses the evaluation gap for LLMs processing enterprise-scale document collections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating LLMs on enterprise-scale documents is challenging due to data privacy concerns with real corporate data and the simplicity of existing synthetic datasets. There was no benchmark that adequately simulates realistic corporate communication networks.

Method: CB creates synthetically generated firms (12 to 10,000 employees) with temporally evolving knowledge bases ensuring cross-document logical consistency. It evaluates LLMs across two dimensions: information extraction and knowledge base querying. Five LLMs were tested on this benchmark.

Result: The evaluation reveals increasingly poor performance as input size approaches realistic scales, demonstrating that current LLMs struggle with large corporate document collections.

Conclusion: CorporateBench provides LLM developers with a much-needed metric for corporate communication reasoning, filling a crucial gap in the benchmarking ecosystem for evaluating LLMs at scale.

Abstract: LLMs are increasingly able to answer complex questions about enterprise-scale document collections. But evaluation is hard: companies don't want to share internal communications, and synthetic datasets have been overly simple. We present CorporateBench (CB), a human-validated multi-task Q&A benchmark whose scale approaches the conditions LLMs encounter in corporate communication networks, with evaluation corpora surpassing 230,000 documents. CB evaluates LLMs across two dimensions (information extraction and knowledge base querying) through four synthetically generated firms ranging from 12 to 10,000 employees. Each corpus is sampled from a temporally evolving knowledge base describing a consistent world, guaranteeing cross-document logical consistency even across hundreds of thousands of documents. We evaluate five LLMs on CB, revealing increasingly poor performance as input size approaches realistic scales. CB provides LLM developers a metric for corporate communication reasoning, filling a crucial gap in the benchmarking ecosystem.

</details>


### [187] [Learning a Continuous Sepsis Severity Score Without Hour-by-Hour Supervision: A Two-Site Retrospective Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27421)
*Kevin Zhu,Ryan Zhang,Baraa Abed,Tilendra Choudhary,Malvern Madondo,Mehak Arora,Yixuan Yang,Alasdair Gent,Aditya Nagori,Omer T. Inan,Krista L. Haines,Patrick Georgoff,Suresh M. Agarwal,Vijay Krishnamoorthy,Tetsu Ohnuma,Mihai V. Podgoreanu,Michael R. Pinsky,Gilles Clermont,Craig M. Coopersmith,Craig S. Jabaley,Rishikesan Kamaleswaran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study develops a novel sepsis severity index using 43 routinely charted variables over 72 hours, trained using mortality as a treatment-level ranking signal rather than traditional per-state targets. The index was validated on 36,807 sepsis patients across two hospital systems and demonstrated meaningful prognostic separation between survivors and non-survivors, with cross-institutional validity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current sepsis severity indices rely on fixed variables and weights established decades ago, which are coarsely discretized and calibrated to outdated cohorts. No alternative learned directly from patient trajectories is in routine clinical use.

Method: Retrospective two-cohort study with 29,116 patients from Massachusetts and 7,691 from Georgia, all meeting Sepsis-3 criteria. Developed a sepsis index using 43 routinely charted variables over a 72-hour treatment window. Used mortality as a treatment-level ranking signal allowing credit redistribution non-uniformly across timesteps. Evaluation on 20% test holdout using clinical vignettes and Spearman correlation, with uncertainty intervals via bootstrap resampling.

Result: Non-survivors scored 1.19-1.64 points higher than survivors on 0-10 scale within all strata of baseline SOFA-2, with similar results for lactate, MAP, and creatinine strata. Within-patient change correlated with lactate change (Spearman rho = 0.39; n=1,854). Cross-institutional agreement was 70-77% of same-site correlation. External within-patient correlations were 0.54 and 0.59 against ceilings of 0.92 and 0.90. Correlated with established indices while null controls remained near zero.

Conclusion: The index demonstrates hourly prognostic information that meaningfully separates patient outcomes and is consistent with clinical expectation, indicating potential as a decision support tool complementing clinical judgment.

Abstract: Currently used sepsis severity indices rely on fixed variables and weights established decades ago, which are coarsely discretized and calibrated to a cohort that no longer reflects contemporary critical care. No alternative learned directly from patient trajectories is in routine use. We conducted a retrospective two-cohort study on a total of 29,116 and 7,691 adult patients meeting Sepsis-3 criteria from two hospital systems in Massachusetts and Georgie, respectively.
  We developed a sepsis index using 43 routinely charted variables over a 72-hour treatment window. Unlike previous studies, we use mortality as a treatment-level ranking signal rather than a per-state target, allowing credit to be redistributed non-uniformly across timesteps. Evaluation was done on a permanent 20% test holdout, using clinical vignettes and Spearman correlation. Uncertainty intervals were obtained by bootstrap resampling of whole patients. Under this ranking scheme, non-survivors scored 1.19-1.64 points higher than survivors on a 0-10 scale within all strata of baseline SOFA-2, with similar results stratifying within lactate, mean arterial pressure (MAP), and creatinine. Within-patient change in the index correlated with change in lactate (Spearman rho = 0.39; n = 1,854). Similar, weaker correlations were found for MAP and creatinine. On a cohort level, cross-institutional agreement measured by Spearman correlation between models trained on different sites, were 70-77% of same-site correlation. External within-patient correlations were 0.54 and 0.59 against ceilings of 0.92 and 0.90. Our index also correlated with established indices, while null controls stayed near zero.
  Our index demonstrated hourly prognostic information that meaningfully separates patient outcomes and is consistent with clinical expectation, indicating potential as a decision support tool complementing clinical judgement.

</details>


### [188] [Mechanistic Reaction Prediction via Discrete Flow Matching on Graph-Structured Electron Occupation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27429)
*Nguyen Xuan-Vu,Octavian Susanu,Daniel Armstrong,Philippe Schwaller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Chemical reactions are fundamentally transformations in electron space, yet most machine learning approaches model them either through \textit{de novo} generation of product molecules or through heuristic graph edits that operate directly on molecular topology.
  We introduce MAELLE (\textbf{M}ech\textbf{A}nistic \textbf{E}dit f\textbf{L}ow-matching on e\textbf{L}ectron r\textbf{E}arrangements), which instead models reactions as discrete flow matching over electron occupation vectors.
  Concretely, we formulate the reactant-to-product mapping as a Continuous-time Markov Chain (CTMC) over the graph-structured integer-valued electron occupation space defined on all bonding, non-bonding, and hydrogen sites.
  To construct the intermediate edit trajectories, we generalize the discrete flow matching mixture path to discrete electron rearrangements using Optimal Transport, yielding a sequence of mechanistically interpretable edit moves without requiring elementary step annotations.
  MAELLE achieves competitive performance on the USPTO-480K benchmark compared with leading reaction prediction models.
  Beyond in-distribution accuracy, we evaluate robustness across two out-of-distribution settings - structural complexity and reaction type - and find that MAELLE maintains strong performance where existing methods degrade.
  Finally, because the learned flow operates over the full electron redistribution, MAELLE naturally recovers mechanistic trajectories that align with known chemistry and can predict side products of a reaction.

</details>


### [189] [WikiSkill: Compiling Agent Experience into Persistent Knowledge for Skill Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27454)
*Liyan Tang,Cyrus Rashtchian,Chun-Sung Ferng,Andrew Tomkins,Da-Cheng Juan,Tu Vu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WikiSkill is a framework that co-evolves AI agent skills with a persistent knowledge base (wiki), separating execution experience, accumulated knowledge, and executable skills to enable systematic accumulation and reuse of agent experience across iterations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The insights guiding skill development in AI agents remain scattered across optimization histories, limiting systematic reuse across iterations. Current skill-evolution methods fail to systematically accumulate and refine agent experience for developing reusable and transferable skills.

Method: WikiSkill separates raw execution experience, accumulated knowledge, and executable skills, while continuously consolidating experience into a persistent wiki that subsequent skill updates can build on. The framework was evaluated across diverse benchmarks and models.

Result: WikiSkill consistently outperforms state-of-the-art skill-evolution methods and improves over no-skill baselines in most model-benchmark settings. Key findings: (1) Larger models benefit more from evolved skills; (2) Smaller models with skills can outperform substantially larger models without them; (3) Skills transfer effectively across models and model families; (4) Skills evolved by other models can outperform self-evolved skills; (5) Persistent knowledge accumulation in the wiki is critical for effective skill evolution.

Conclusion: Skill evolution and model scaling are complementary. Systematic accumulation and refining of agent experience through a persistent knowledge base enables developing reusable and transferable skills that can outperform even larger models without such skills.

Abstract: Agent skills package specialized knowledge and workflows into reusable resources that extend AI agent capabilities. Recent work automatically discovers such skills from agent experience, which enables agents to progressively adapt through interaction. However, the insights that guide skill development typically remain scattered across optimization histories, limiting their systematic reuse across iterations. We introduce WikiSkill, a framework that co-evolves agent skills with a persistent knowledge base (wiki). At a high level, WikiSkill separates raw execution experience, accumulated knowledge, and executable skills, while continuously consolidating experience into the wiki, which subsequent skill updates can build on. Across diverse benchmarks and models, WikiSkill consistently outperforms state-of-the-art skill-evolution methods and improves over no-skill baselines in most model-benchmark settings. We find that skill evolution complements model scaling: larger models generally benefit more from evolved skills, while smaller models with skills can outperform substantially larger models without them. We also find that evolved skills transfer effectively across models and model families, and skills evolved by other models can outperform self-evolved skills. Finally, our ablation studies confirm that persistent knowledge accumulation in the wiki is critical for effective skill evolution. These results demonstrate the benefits of systematically accumulating and refining agent experience for developing reusable and transferable skills.

</details>

### [190] [Attention-Guided Reliability Scaling for Contrastive Decoding in Robust Audio-Visual Speech Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26213)
*YoungChae Kim,Da-Hee Yang,Joon-Hyuk Chang*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper applies contrastive decoding (CD) to LLM-based audio-visual speech recognition (AVSR) systems, where audio-only conditioning is contrasted against full audio-visual conditioning within the same model. To address the trade-off introduced by fixed contrastive strength across different noise levels, the authors propose reliability-aware scaling that adaptively modulates contrastive influence based on attention dynamics and inter-model predictive divergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Contrastive decoding can stabilize LLM generation but using fixed contrastive strength creates a trade-off: stronger intervention helps under severe noise but may over-correct reliable predictions in clean conditions. Current methods lack adaptability across varying noise levels.

Method: The proposed method contrasts audio-only conditioning with full audio-visual conditioning within the same underlying model. Reliability-aware scaling is achieved by deriving reliability signals from attention dynamics and inter-model predictive divergence, which adaptively modulates the contrastive influence at each token rather than using a fixed strength.

Result: Experiments on LRS3 dataset show consistent improvements across both clean and low-SNR (signal-to-noise ratio) conditions.

Conclusion: The reliability-aware scaling approach successfully addresses the trade-off problem in contrastive decoding for AVSR, achieving robust performance across varying noise conditions without requiring additional training.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based audio-visual speech recognition (AVSR) systems are robust under noise. Contrastive decoding (CD), originally introduced to stabilize LLM generation by contrasting a weaker model against a stronger one at inference time, adjusts predictions without additional training. In this work, we apply CD to AVSR by contrasting audio-only conditioning with full audio-visual conditioning within the same underlying model. However, using a fixed contrastive strength introduces a trade-off across noise levels: stronger intervention helps under severe noise but may over-correct reliable predictions in clean conditions. We propose reliability-aware scaling of CD for AVSR. Instead of using a fixed strength, we adaptively modulate the contrastive influence at each token based on reliability signals derived from attention dynamics and inter-model predictive divergence. Experiments on LRS3 show consistent improvements across clean and low-SNR conditions.

</details>


### [191] [StreamAV-Bench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Streaming Audio-Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26336)
*Kaiqi Liu,Haoxuan Zeng,Jingqi Liu,Jiacong Fang,Ziqi Cai,Yunyao Mao,Henglin Liu,Yu Sheng,Shuchen Weng,Boxin Shi*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: StreamAV-Bench is the first comprehensive benchmark for streaming audio-video generation, addressing the gap that existing benchmarks only evaluate completed sequences. It includes two evaluation tracks (progressive and interactive) across 32 fine-grained dimensions, and reveals that current models suffer from temporal drift and responsiveness bottlenecks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent advancements in generative models enable unbounded streaming audio-video generation for real-time interactive worlds, but existing benchmarks primarily evaluate completed sequences and struggle to capture streaming properties, creating a need for a dedicated streaming evaluation benchmark.
