Method: Introduced StreamAV-Bench with two unified evaluation tracks: (1) Progressive track for instruction adherence and long-horizon stability, and (2) Interactive track for interactive response and state retention and reuse. Conducted extensive evaluation of 13 representative systems across 32 fine-grained dimensions with expert-verified evaluation cases, followed by comprehensive failure analysis.

Result: Current models suffer from two main issues: (1) temporal drift in progressive generation, and (2) responsiveness bottlenecks during interactive control. The benchmark reveals limitations in both progressive and interactive generation capabilities.

Conclusion: Based on failure analysis, insights are shared to guide the development of native joint audio-video streaming models, addressing temporal drift and responsiveness issues for advancing the field.

Abstract: Recent advancements in generative models are pushing video generation toward unbounded streaming audio-video generation for real-time interactive worlds. However, existing benchmarks primarily evaluate completed sequences and struggle to capture streaming properties. To bridge this gap, we introduce StreamAV-Bench, the first comprehensive benchmark tailored for streaming audio-video generation. StreamAV-Bench establishes a unified evaluation framework, including the progressive track for instruction adherence and long-horizon stability, and the interactive track for interactive response and state retention and reuse. With expert-verified evaluation cases across 32 fine-grained dimensions, we conduct an extensive evaluation of 13 representative systems. Our analysis reveals that current models suffer from temporal drift in progressive generation and responsiveness bottlenecks during interactive control. Based on a comprehensive failure analysis, we share insights to advance the development of native joint audio-video streaming models.

</details>


### [192] [Decay-Region Group Delay as a Forensic Cue for AI-Generated Impulsive Sounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26346)
*JaeHyeong Chang,Chengzhe Sun,Siwei Lyu*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether AI-generated impulsive sounds can be distinguished from real ones using group delay (GD) analysis. The key finding is that AI-generated sounds show nearly identical onset-region GD but significantly different decay-region GD behavior, with decay-region KL divergence of 0.322 vs near-zero onset divergence (0.022). A Random Forest classifier using nine decay-region features achieves AUC = 0.884, while a CNN using GD maps as 2D input achieves 90-94% accuracy. Under generator hold-out evaluation, the RF achieves the highest average hold-out accuracy (66.7%) with stable AUC across 27 STFT configurations (0.700-0.847).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to develop forensic methods for distinguishing AI-generated audio from real recordings, as AI-generated impulsive sounds (like gunshots, door slams, etc.) become more sophisticated. The authors aim to find physically interpretable cues that can complement magnitude-based classifiers.

Method: The method involves group delay (GD) analysis, which examines the phase characteristics of signals. The authors analyze onset-region vs decay-region GD distributions, compute KL divergence between real and AI-generated sounds, use Random Forest classification with 9 decay-region features, and CNN classifiers with GD maps as 2D inputs. They also evaluate cross-band GD variability and test parameter sensitivity across 27 STFT configurations.

Result: Key results: (1) Decay-region GD shows measurable differences (KL divergence = 0.322) while onset-region GD is nearly identical (0.022); (2) Single-feature cross-band GD variability achieves AUC = 0.720; (3) RF with 9 decay features achieves AUC = 0.884 under sample-disjoint evaluation; (4) CNN with GD maps achieves 90-94% accuracy; (5) Under generator hold-out, RF achieves 66.7% avg accuracy (highest), CNN avg AUC = 0.762, AST avg AUC = 0.772; (6) RF remains stable across 27 STFT configs (AUC 0.700-0.847, std = 0.035).

Conclusion: The conclusion suggests that decay-region group delay serves as a physically interpretable forensic cue that complements magnitude-based classifiers for detecting AI-generated impulsive sounds. However, broader validation remains necessary, particularly under generator hold-out conditions where performance varies significantly (AUC 0.457-0.918).

Abstract: We investigate whether AI-generated impulsive sounds can be distinguished from real ones through group delay analysis. Our central finding is that AI-generated impulsive sounds show near-identical onset-region group-delay distributions but exhibit measurably different group-delay behavior in the late decay region: decay-region KL divergence reaches $0.322$ compared to near-zero onset divergence ($0.022$). Cross-band GD variability achieves single-feature AUC~=~0.720, and a Random Forest (RF) over nine decay-region features reaches AUC~$=$~0.884 under sample-disjoint evaluation. A group delay map used as a standalone 2D input to CNN classifiers achieves 90--94\% accuracy, demonstrating that group delay carries substantial discriminative information. Under generator hold-out, CNN and transformer classifiers show highly variable AUC (0.457--0.918). The group delay RF achieves the highest average hold-out accuracy among the evaluated methods ($66.7\%$) and avoids extreme below-random collapse, although its average AUC (0.731) is lower than CNN avg (0.762) and AST (0.772). Parameter sensitivity analysis across 27 STFT configurations confirms that the RF AUC remains stable (0.700--0.847, std~=~0.035). These results suggest that decay-region group delay can serve as a physically interpretable forensic cue that complements magnitude-based classifiers, while broader validation remains necessary.

</details>


### [193] [AudioSpan: Spanning the Duration and Depth of Audio Comprehension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26431)
*Wen Huang,Yunfei Chu,Meng Gao,Haolin He,Jin Xu*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: AudioSpan is a new benchmark for evaluating large audio-language models (LALMs) on long-form audio (10 min to 2+ hours), featuring 3,240 questions across three cognitive levels (perception, understanding, and reasoning). It uses two QA approaches (Native QA and Anchor QA) with an automated pipeline, and finds that the primary challenge is extracting relevant facts from long, redundant audio signals, particularly for perception and temporal grounding tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for audio-language models rely on short clips (seconds) where scores saturate and models converge. Recent long-form efforts still evaluate long audio using the same approach as short clips. There was a need for a benchmark that spans both duration and depth to properly evaluate LALMs on realistic long-form audio tasks.

Method: The benchmark uses two question-answering paths: (1) Native QA - extracts questions from audio content with multiple-choice and open-ended formats graded by detailed rubrics; (2) Anchor QA - injects ground truth by planting acoustic anchors into audio and building a perception-to-reasoning chain scored to the first error. Construction uses fully automated pipeline with structured captioning, QA generation, and adversarial critic feedback. 12 LALMs were evaluated.

Result: Evaluating 12 LALMs on AudioSpan revealed that the hard part comes before reasoning: distilling relevant facts from long, redundant signals. This difficulty grows with audio length and falls hardest on perception, especially temporal grounding.

Conclusion: AudioSpan provides a more realistic and challenging benchmark for LALMs by testing on long-form audio with multi-level cognitive questions. The key bottleneck is not reasoning but perception and information extraction from lengthy audio, particularly temporal grounding tasks. The benchmark is available at HuggingFace.

Abstract: General audio comprehension now covers speech, sound, and music over durations from seconds to hours, driven by large audio-language models (LALMs) that are increasingly omni-modal. Yet the benchmarks that test them still rely on clips of seconds, where scores saturate and models converge; recent long-form efforts extend duration but evaluate long audio much as short clips are. We introduce AudioSpan, a benchmark that spans both duration and depth: it pairs audio from 10 minutes to over 2 hours with 3,240 questions across three cognitive levels, namely perception, understanding, and reasoning. Two paths supply the questions, differing in how question content is sourced and how ground truth is obtained. Native QA extracts questions from the audio's content, posing each as a multiple-choice item and an open-ended one graded by detailed rubrics. Anchor QA instead injects ground truth, planting acoustic anchors into the audio and building a perception-to-reasoning chain scored only to the first error. A fully automated pipeline constructs every item through structured captioning, QA generation, and adversarial critic feedback. Evaluating 12 LALMs on AudioSpan, we find the hard part comes before reasoning: distilling a few relevant facts from a long, redundant signal. This difficulty grows with audio length and falls hardest on perception, especially temporal grounding. AudioSpan is available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/holvan/AudioSpan.

</details>


### [194] [SpeechGym: An Audio-Native Gym for Training Voice Agents via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26432)
*Jiajun Fan,Jingyuan Li,Prashanth Gurunath Shivakumar,Jia-Hong Huang,Qi Luo,M. Maruf,Ivan Bulyko,Ge Liu,Roger Ren*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: SpeechGym is an audio-native agentic training environment that enables end-to-end training of voice agents through native audio conversation, without ASR/TTS or API boundaries. It addresses the gap between text-based training and voice deployment by creating a fully trainable audio loop where two omni-modal models converse directly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The dominant paradigm trains voice agents in text, but existing frameworks either use cascaded TTS/ASR with proprietary APIs (preventing gradient flow and making RL costly) or stay entirely in text (can't improve voice agents). The authors wanted to create a modality-matched training environment where the interaction modality is the only variable.

Method: SpeechGym creates an audio-native environment where two omni-modal models converse in native audio over the same tasks, tools, and success criteria as an established text agentic benchmark. The key innovation is using per-turn process rewards instead of outcome-only GRPO, because almost all rollouts fail identically in outcome-only training (gradient starvation). Each successful tool call receives credit, restoring variance to nearly every group.

Result: The trained agent transfers with no further tuning to an independently implemented voice benchmark, more than doubling task success rate and moving an open-weights model from last place to second on the leaderboard, while using fewer turns and tokens than before training.

Conclusion: Audio agentic capability does not simply emerge from audio understanding. The primary failures are perceptual (misheard tool arguments) rather than reasoning deficits, and behavioral failures (unauthorized writes under pressure). Both failure types are trainable because the environment provides free labels: failed calls vs successful calls. The key insight is that outcome-only RL fails due to sparsity; per-turn process rewards restore gradient variance and enable effective training.

Abstract: Voice agents must call tools and hold multi-turn dialogue entirely through speech, yet the dominant paradigm trains them in text. Existing frameworks either cascade TTS and ASR around a proprietary voice API, where gradients cannot flow and per-call cost makes on-policy reinforcement learning prohibitive, or stay in text: they measure voice agents but cannot improve them. We present SpeechGym, an audio-native agentic environment in which two omni-modal models converse in native audio, with no external ASR or TTS and no API boundary, over the unmodified tasks, tools and success check of an established text agentic benchmark, so that the interaction modality is the only variable and the loop stays local and trainable end to end. Audio agentic capability does not follow from audio understanding. The failures speech introduces are perceptual rather than reasoning deficits: the agent picks the right tool and the right argument slot but fills it with a value misheard from the waveform, and that single error cascades into a failed call, a retry of the same call, and a wasted step budget. A second failure is behavioural: under an insistent caller the agent performs an unauthorised write and ends the episode believing it helped. Both are trainable, because the environment labels them for free: a call with a misheard argument fails against the database while a correct one succeeds. The obstacle is sparsity, not signal. Outcome-only GRPO is gradient-starved here, since almost every rollout group fails identically, while a per-turn process reward crediting each successful tool call restores variance to nearly every group. Trained this way, the agent transfers with no further tuning to an independently implemented voice benchmark, more than doubling task success and carrying an open-weights model from last place to second on that leaderboard, while using fewer turns and tokens than before training.

</details>


### [195] [Direct or Mediated? Task-Dependent Audio Information Routing in Large Audio Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27026)
*Yizhou Zhang,Wangjin Zhou,Xin Gu,Yichi Wang,Wei Tan,Yi Zhao,Zhi Gong,Keisuke Imoto,Tatsuya Kawahara*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper investigates how Large Audio Language Models (LALMs) handle concatenated audio inputs, discovering a task-dependent robustness gap: ASR remains stable while audio question answering (AQA) degrades significantly. Through layer-wise attention knockout analysis, the authors identify distinct information routing mechanisms—ASR relies on direct audio token retrieval while AQA depends on a mediated route through prompt tokens. Interestingly, task-relevant audio attributes remain decodable in middle/later layers even during AQA failure, suggesting the bottleneck lies in information utilization rather than loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LALMs show strong performance on single audio segments but their behavior under less familiar input configurations (like concatenated audio) remains underexplored. Understanding failure modes is crucial for deployment reliability.

Method: Controlled setting concatenating two audio segments into single input; layer-wise attention knockout to analyze how audio information routes through LALM decoders; probe prompt-token representations under audio concatenation to assess decodability of audio attributes.

Result: Task-dependent robustness gap observed: ASR remains stable across multiple LALMs while AQA degrades substantially. ASR relies on direct retrieval from audio tokens by answer tokens; AQA depends on mediated route where audio information integrates into prompt tokens first. Task-relevant audio attributes remain decodable in middle/later decoder layers even when AQA performance drops sharply—dissociation between information presence and utilization.

Conclusion: LALMs exhibit task-dependent audio information routing with distinct pathways for ASR vs AQA. The AQA failure is not due to complete loss of audio information from decoder states, but rather a downstream bottleneck in retrieving or utilizing prompt-mediated information during answer generation. Information utilization is a key limitation on LALM generalization.

Abstract: Large Audio Language Models (LALMs) have demonstrated strong performance across a wide range of audio understanding tasks. However, they are typically evaluated on single, coherent audio segments, leaving their behavior under less familiar input configurations underexplored. We study this issue through a controlled setting in which two audio segments are concatenated into a single input. Across multiple LALMs, we observe a striking task-dependent robustness gap: automatic speech recognition (ASR) remains comparatively stable, whereas audio question answering (AQA) degrades substantially. To investigate the mechanisms underlying this disparity, we analyze how audio information is routed through LALM decoders using layer-wise attention knockout. The results reveal distinct task-dependent pathways. ASR relies primarily on direct retrieval from audio tokens by answer tokens, whereas AQA depends more strongly on a mediated route in which audio information is first integrated into prompt tokens and subsequently accessed during generation. We further probe prompt-token representations under audio concatenation and find that task-relevant audio attributes remain readily decodable, particularly in middle and later decoder layers, even when AQA performance deteriorates sharply. This dissociation indicates that the failure cannot be explained by complete loss of audio information from the decoder states and is instead consistent with a downstream bottleneck in retrieving or utilizing prompt-mediated information during answer generation. Together, our findings reveal task-dependent audio information routing in LALMs and highlight information utilization as a potential limitation on their generalization.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [196] [SLM-Conditioned Hierarchical Relation Routing for Labeled Property Graph Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26132)
*Michal Podstawski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes SLM-Conditioned Hierarchical Relation Routing, a novel architecture that integrates a small language model (SLM) into graph neural networks for processing labeled property graphs. The method uses a topology GNN as a structural anchor, while an SLM processes graph soft tokens to perform target-conditioned message routing within and across relationship types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional graph neural networks represent node and relationship properties as static feature vectors, which limits their ability to dynamically determine which semantic evidence should influence message propagation for specific prediction targets. There is a need for architectures that can adaptively select relevant contextual information based on the prediction target.

Method: The architecture combines a topology GNN (providing structural representation and prediction anchor) with an SLM. For each target node, incident messages incorporate neighbor's structural state, node-property encoding, relationship-property encoding, and relationship type. The SLM processes structured graph soft tokens to generate target-conditioned routing queries that: (1) select relevant messages within each relationship type, then (2) route information across relation-level summaries. The result is a bounded residual update to the topology anchor.

Result: The architecture provides interpretable analysis at both neighbor and relationship-type levels, preserving structural evidence while allowing contextual semantic information to modify predictions. It offers a general mechanism for integrating language-derived semantics into property-rich graph learning.

Conclusion: The proposed SLM-Conditioned Hierarchical Relation Routing successfully bridges language models with graph neural networks, enabling adaptive, target-specific message routing in property-rich graphs while maintaining interpretability and preserving structural information.

Abstract: Labeled property graphs combine relational structure with heterogeneous textual and categorical properties attached to both nodes and relationships. Conventional graph neural networks typically represent these properties as static feature vectors, limiting their ability to determine which semantic evidence should influence message propagation for a particular prediction target. We propose SLM-Conditioned Hierarchical Relation Routing, an architecture that integrates a small language model directly into graph message selection. A topology GNN provides a stable structural representation and prediction anchor. For each target node, incident messages combine the neighbor's structural state, node-property encoding, relationship-property encoding, and relationship type. A parameter-efficient SLM processes structured graph soft tokens and produces a target-conditioned routing query. This query first selects relevant messages within each relationship type and subsequently routes information across relation-level summaries. The resulting representation provides a bounded residual update to the topology anchor, preserving structural evidence while allowing contextual semantic information to modify the prediction. The architecture supports interpretable analysis at both the neighbor and relationship-type levels and provides a general mechanism for integrating language-derived semantics into property-rich graph learning.

</details>


### [197] [NeuronFuzz: Safety Neuron Guided Fuzzing for LLM Safety Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26222)
*Zhiyuan Xu,Muhammad Firhard Roslan,Joseph Gardiner,Sana Belguith,Lichao Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NeuronFuzz is a white-box fuzzing framework that exploits internal safety neurons as continuous execution feedback for LLM safety evaluation, eliminating the need for costly response generation in the fuzzing loop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automated testing methods for LLM safety rely on response-level feedback, which is expensive and provides sparse guidance on strongly aligned models where most candidate prompts are rejected with the same failure outcome. This limits the effectiveness of jailbreak attack discovery.

Method: NeuronFuzz uses a SafetyOracle that converts safety-neuron activations into a continuous safety alarm score obtainable during prefill. It identifies safety neurons using template-invariant harmful/benign inputs and stability-aware selection. The differentiable alarm score enables gradient-based identification of safety-sensitive template positions, while a masked language model generates fluent, context-compatible mutations preserving the original harmful payload.

Result: Across 21 text and multimodal models, NeuronFuzz achieves 76-100% jailbreak discovery rate on 5 white-box source models, outperforming baselines by up to 48 percentage points. Optimized templates transfer zero-shot to open-weight and 6 proprietary models with average ASR/EASR of 69.6%/92.6% and 44.1%/60.0%.

Conclusion: NeuronFuzz demonstrates that internal safety neurons provide effective continuous feedback for LLM safety evaluation, enabling highly effective jailbreak discovery and transferable attack templates across diverse model architectures.

Abstract: Safety evaluation is critical for assessing whether aligned Large Language Models (LLMs) remain robust against jailbreak attacks. Existing automated testing methods, however, largely rely on response-level feedback: each candidate prompt typically requires generating a target-model response to evaluate its attack effectiveness. This process is expensive and, more importantly, provides only sparse guidance on strongly aligned models, where most candidates are rejected with the same failure outcome.
  This paper presents NeuronFuzz, a white-box fuzzing framework that exploits internal safety neurons as continuous execution feedback for LLM safety evaluation. A SafetyOracle converts safety-neuron activations into a continuous safety alarm score that serves as feedback for fuzzing and can be obtained during prefill, eliminating response generation from the fuzzing loop. To construct the SafetyOracle, NeuronFuzz uses template-invariant harmful and benign inputs and stability-aware selection to identify a compact set of safety neurons whose activations capture harmful-intent recognition. Moreover, since the safety alarm score is differentiable, NeuronFuzz uses its gradients to identify safety-sensitive template positions and a masked language model to generate fluent, context-compatible mutations while preserving original harmful payload and avoiding additional optimization variables. We evaluate NeuronFuzz across 21 text and multimodal models. Across five white-box source models, it achieves a 76-100% jailbreak discovery rate, outperforming baselines by up to 48 percentage points. Its optimized templates further transfer zero-shot to open-weight and six proprietary target models, achieving average ASR and top-5 ensemble ASR (EASR) of 69.6%/92.6% and 44.1%/60.0%, respectively.

</details>


### [198] [Pruning Binarized Neural Networks: A Dedicated Framework and Globally Weighted Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26233)
*Roan Rubiales,Jean Pierre David*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a PyTorch framework for designing and optimizing binarized neural networks with pruning capabilities, and proposes a novel global weighting pruning method that achieves 70% pruning rate on VGG11 with maintained accuracy, significantly outperforming the previous 41% state-of-the-art in binarized settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extreme neural network compression via binarization reduces memory and computational costs for edge devices (FPGAs, microcontrollers). While combining binarization with pruning could yield additional gains, existing pruning methods are ineffective for binarized representations and fail to provide meaningful hardware savings.

Method: The authors developed a PyTorch-based research framework incorporating freezing and pruning mechanisms for binarized neural networks. They proposed a novel pruning method using a global weighting mechanism that evaluates the relative importance of learned parameters across different abstraction levels.

Result: The proposed global weighting pruning method consistently achieves superior accuracy-pruning rate trade-offs. On VGG11, it achieves a 70% pruning rate while maintaining constant accuracy, compared to the previous state-of-the-art limit of only 41% pruning rate in binarized settings.

Conclusion: The framework enables rapid evaluation of state-of-the-art approaches and fast prototyping of new methods. The global weighting pruning approach successfully addresses the limitations of existing pruning strategies for binarized neural networks, enabling significantly higher compression rates without accuracy loss.

Abstract: Extreme compression of deep neural networks, up to full binarization, dramatically reduces memory footprint and arithmetic complexity, facilitating deployment on constrained edge hardware with field-programmable gate arrays (FPGAs) and microcontrollers. Although combining binarization with pruning promises additional efficiency gains, existing pruning strategies are ill-suited to binarized representations and rarely translate into meaningful hardware savings. We introduce a PyTorch-based, research-oriented framework that incorporates freezing and pruning mechanisms for designing and optimizing binarized neural networks. The framework enables rapid and reproducible evaluation of state-of-the-art approaches and the fast prototyping of new ones. Leveraging this framework, we propose a novel pruning method that accounts for the relative importance of learned parameters across abstraction levels. Such a global weighting mechanism consistently achieves a superior trade-off between model accuracy and pruning rate, achieving a 70% pruning rate on VGG11 with constant accuracy, while state-of-the-art results reach only 41% in the binarized setting.

</details>


### [199] [Muon with Finite Newton-Schulz: The Smoothing Benefit in Nonsmooth Nonconvex Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26288)
*Mingyi Li,Taira Tsuchiya*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the Muon optimizer for training large language models, focusing on its use of finite Newton-Schulz iterations. The key finding is that contrary to existing theory suggesting finite iterations hurt convergence, finite Newton-Schulz can actually be beneficial for nonsmooth nonconvex optimization. The paper shows that a logarithmic number of iterations suffices for convergence, while the exact polar factor update may fail to converge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing theory on Muon optimizer either replaces the finite Newton-Schulz iteration with the exact polar factor or treats finite iterations as approximation errors. This means the actual update rule Muon uses has no positive theoretical guarantees. The authors aim to show that finite Newton-Schulz iterations are not just harmless but actually beneficial.

Method: The paper uses the online-to-nonconvex conversion framework, viewing Muon's update rule as an online learner and converting its regret bound into a stationarity guarantee. The key insight is that finite Newton-Schulz iterations smooth the discontinuous polar map into a Lipschitz map of singular values, acting as a smoothed spectral potential. The analysis extends to general spectral maps with this smoothing property.

Result: The paper proves that a Newton-Schulz depth growing only logarithmically in target accuracy suffices for convergence to stationary points in nonsmooth nonconvex optimization, whereas Muon with the exact-polar update may fail to converge. The sample complexity bounds match the best-known guarantees for nonsmooth nonconvex optimization and are optimal for smooth nonconvex optimization up to problem-dependent factors.

Conclusion: Finite Newton-Schulz iterations in Muon are not detrimental but actually beneficial for nonsmooth nonconvex optimization. The smoothing effect of finite iterations is essential for convergence guarantees. This insight extends beyond Newton-Schulz to general spectral maps with similar smoothing properties, providing a theoretical foundation for why Muon works well in practice.

Abstract: Muon has emerged as a strong optimizer for the matrix-valued parameters in large language model pretraining, approximately orthogonalizing its momentum with a few Newton-Schulz iterations. Existing theory either replaces this iteration with the exact polar factor it approximates, or treats its finite depth as an approximation error, and thus the iteration Muon actually runs can only hurt the guarantees. We show that finite Newton-Schulz can instead be beneficial for nonsmooth nonconvex optimization. To this end, we analyze Muon through the online-to-nonconvex conversion, which views the update rule as an online learner and converts its regret bound into a stationarity guarantee. The finite Newton-Schulz iteration smooths the discontinuous polar map into a Lipschitz map of the singular values, and Muon with finite Newton-Schulz can be regarded as an online learner with a smoothed spectral potential. This smoothing is exactly what the conversion needs: we prove that a Newton-Schulz depth growing only logarithmically in the target accuracy suffices for convergence to stationary points in nonsmooth nonconvex optimization, whereas Muon with the exact-polar update may fail to converge. The resulting sample complexity bounds match the best-known guarantees for nonsmooth nonconvex optimization and are optimal for smooth nonconvex optimization up to problem-dependent factors. The argument extends beyond Newton-Schulz to general spectral maps with the same smoothing property.

</details>


### [200] [Algebraic Multigrid Acceleration for Efficient Label Spreading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26309)
*Antonia van Betteray,Jonathan Klees,Miriam Schäfers,Matthias Rottmann*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes AMELS (Algebraic Multigrid Acceleration for Efficient Label Spreading), a framework that addresses the computational and memory limitations of traditional label spreading for large-scale, high-dimensional datasets. It combines fast neighborhood graph construction with algebraic multigrid solvers to enable efficient label propagation from few labeled examples to a larger pool of unlabeled data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual annotation of large-scale datasets is expensive and time-consuming. While label spreading is an effective semi-supervised learning technique that propagates labels from limited labeled examples to unlabeled data, its application to large-scale, high-dimensional datasets is constrained by computational costs and memory limitations.

Method: AMELS improves scalability through two key innovations: (1) fast construction of neighborhood graphs, and (2) incorporation of algebraic multigrid solvers that replace the ordinary random walk iteration typically used in label spreading. The multilevel nature of algebraic multigrid enables label information to spread across graphs of any size in a single multigrid cycle.

Result: AMELS achieves significant runtime reductions compared to existing implementations while demonstrating greater robustness to hyperparameter choices in terms of both runtime and classification accuracy. The framework enables efficient label spreading on large-scale image datasets and produces accurate labels even with only a few labeled samples available.

Conclusion: AMELS provides an effective solution for scalable label spreading on large datasets, addressing the computational and memory constraints of traditional approaches while maintaining accuracy with limited labeled data.

Abstract: Modern machine learning models rely on large amounts of labeled data. However, manual annotation of large-scale datasets is expensive and time-consuming. Label spreading is a semi-supervised learning technique that addresses this challenge by propagating information from a few labeled examples to a larger pool of unlabeled data. Despite its effectiveness, its application to large-scale, high-dimensional datasets is limited by computational costs and memory constraints. To address these limitations, we propose Algebraic Multigrid Acceleration for Efficient Label Spreading (AMELS), an efficient label spreading framework that improves scalability by fast construction of neighborhood graphs and the incorporation of algebraic multigrid solvers. The latter is an iterative solver that replaces the ordinary random walk iteration typically performed in label spreading. Due to the multilevel nature of algebraic multigrid solvers, AMELS spreads given label information across a graph of any size in a single multigrid cycle. We demonstrate that AMELS achieves significant runtime reductions compared to existing implementations while also being more robust to hyperparameter choices in terms of both runtime and classification accuracy. Our framework therefore enables efficient label spreading on large-scale image datasets and produces accurate labels even when only a few labeled samples are available.

</details>


### [201] [Privacy Without Regret: Differentially Private Inference-Time Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26324)
*Ishi Jain,Nandini Bhattad,Sayak Ray Chowdhury*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses two key problems with Best-of-N (BoN) sampling: reward hacking and lack of privacy protection for human preference data. The authors propose that adding calibrated noise to reward scores resolves both issues simultaneously. They introduce two methods: Private Best-of-N (PrivBoN) using Gumbel noise for differential privacy, and Private Inference-Time Pessimism (PrivITP) using χ²-regularized rejection sampling with Gaussian mechanism. Both methods achieve the information-theoretic optimal alignment while providing privacy guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to solve two critical problems in inference-time alignment: (1) reward hacking, where selected responses exploit errors in the proxy reward model, and (2) the absence of privacy protection for sensitive human preference data used to train reward models. Existing BoN sampling degrades beyond a critical number of responses, and no privacy-preserving solution exists.

Method: The paper proposes two methods: (1) PrivBoN adds Gumbel noise at an appropriate scale to reward scores before selection, providing ε-differential privacy while implementing KL-regularized alignment; (2) PrivITP combines χ²-regularized rejection sampling with a two-phase Gaussian mechanism, achieving ex-post (ε,δ)-DP with privacy cost independent of the number of responses n.

Result: PrivBoN provides ε-differential privacy and achieves regret-optimal regularization when privacy budget exceeds critical threshold ε*. PrivITP achieves ex-post (ε,δ)-DP with privacy cost independent of n, cleanly decouples regularization from privacy parameters, and attains the skyline up to a noise-inflation term. Experiments confirm both methods are scaling-monotonic (unlike BoN which degrades past critical n), with PrivITP matching or outperforming PrivBoN, especially in the strong-privacy regime.

Conclusion: A single intervention—adding calibrated noise to reward scores—simultaneously resolves both reward hacking and privacy issues in BoN sampling. When privacy budget exceeds ε*, privacy-mandated noise achieves the information-theoretic skyline with zero additional alignment cost. PrivITP is recommended for strong privacy regimes as it decouples regularization from privacy parameters and achieves better performance.

Abstract: Best-of-N (BoN) sampling is the simplest and most widely deployed inference-time alignment strategy, but it suffers from two distinct problems: reward hacking, in which the selected response exploits errors in the proxy reward model, and the absence of any privacy protection for the sensitive human preference data used to train that reward model. We show that a single intervention-adding calibrated noise to reward scores before selection-resolves both. Our first result, Private Best-of-N (PrivBoN), establishes that Gumbel noise at an appropriate scale simultaneously provides $ε$-differential privacy and implements KL-regularized alignment. Whenever the privacy budget exceeds a critical threshold $ε^*$, the privacy-mandated noise is the regret-optimal regularization, and privacy imposes zero additional alignment cost-matching the information-theoretic skyline of Huang et al. (2025). Because $ε^*$ depends on an unknown coverage coefficient, we introduce Private Inference-Time Pessimism (PrivITP), which combines $χ^2$-regularized rejection sampling with a two-phase Gaussian mechanism. PrivITP achieves ex-post $(ε,δ)$-DP with a privacy cost independent of the number of responses $n$, cleanly decouples the regularization parameter from the privacy parameter, and attains the skyline up to a noise-inflation term. Experiments across several language models, datasets, and reward models confirm our results: PrivBoN and PrivITP are scaling-monotonic (unlike BoN, which degrades past a critical $n$), and PrivITP matches or outperforms PrivBoN at equivalent privacy levels, with the largest gains in the strong-privacy regime.

</details>


### [202] [Soft Active Electromyography Interface for Machine Learning-Enabled Silent Speech Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27048)
*Yuta Kurotaki,Shusuke Yamakoshi,Reitaro Yoshida,Yutaka Isoda,Tamami Takano,Yuji Isano,Yusuke Miyake,Kentaro Kuribayashi,Hiroki Ota*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a soft, wearable electromyography (EMG) interface for silent speech recognition (SSR). The device, worn on the hand with a fingertip electrode, uses liquid metal interconnects and flexible printed circuit electrodes to acquire stable EMG signals near the lips. A deep neural network achieved 97.2% accuracy on a 30-word vocabulary, and real-time drone control demonstrated practical applicability in noisy or privacy-sensitive environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional silent speech recognition approaches are limited by three key issues: (1) need for constant facial attachment, (2) privacy concerns, and (3) unstable signal acquisition. This work aims to address these limitations by developing a wearable, non-intrusive EMG system that acquires signals only when needed.

Method: The proposed interface integrates: (1) a fingertip electrode positioned near the lips for EMG acquisition, (2) liquid metal (LM) interconnects for reliable electrical connections, (3) transparent flexible printed circuit (FPC) electrodes, and (4) elastomer encapsulation for mechanical stability during finger motion. A deep neural network was trained on the acquired EMG signals for word-level classification.

Result: The system achieved a mean accuracy of 97.2 ± 1.3% across three subjects when classifying a 30-word vocabulary, demonstrating robust linguistic discrimination. Real-time drone control validated the approach in noisy and privacy-sensitive environments where conventional voice recognition fails.

Conclusion: This study demonstrates the potential of soft, wearable EMG systems as secure and intuitive human-machine interfaces. The fingertip-worn device offers a non-intrusive alternative for silent speech recognition, with applications in privacy-sensitive and noisy environments where traditional voice recognition is impractical.

Abstract: Silent speech recognition (SSR) provides an alternative communication pathway in the absence of audible speech. However, conventional approaches are limited by the need for constant facial attachment, privacy concerns, and unstable signal acquisition. Here, we propose a soft, active electromyography (EMG) interface that enables word-level SSR using machine learning. Worn on the hand, the device uses a fingertip electrode that can be positioned near the lips to acquire EMG signals only when needed. The interface integrates liquid metal (LM) interconnects, transparent flexible printed circuit (FPC) electrodes, and elastomer encapsulation to ensure high mechanical stability during finger motion. A deep neural network trained on these stable signals achieved a mean accuracy of 97.2 $\pm$ 1.3% across three subjects in classifying a 30-word vocabulary, demonstrating robust linguistic discrimination. Furthermore, real-time drone control validates the practicality of this approach in noisy and privacy-sensitive environments where conventional voice recognition fails. This study highlights the potential of soft, wearable EMG systems as secure and intuitive human-machine interfaces.

</details>


### [203] [Mitigating Strong-Modality Collapse in Multimodal Learning via Inverted Asymmetric Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26879)
*Mary Ogbuka Kenneth,Foaad Khosmood,Abbas Edalat*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Fusing multiple modalities is expected to improve model performance. However, on the MultiHuSE dataset, early, late, and symmetric attention fusion often fail to outperform the best unimodal baseline (text). Pathway isolation of a symmetric attention fusion model reveals that the text-pathway accuracy drops from 74.9% to 56.4% after fusion in one such setting, indicating that the dominant modality can be degraded during integration. We term this strong-modality collapse and argue that it helps explain why some multimodal models fail to surpass unimodal baselines. We propose Inverted Asymmetric Fusion (IAF), which avoids forcing mutual attention across modalities. The dominant modality is preserved by passing through fusion unchanged, while weaker modalities attend to it as a contextual anchor. Before fusion, weaker modalities are strengthened using Modality-Aware Knowledge Distillation. We evaluate IAF on three benchmarks with different modality hierarchies: text-dominant datasets (MultiHuSE, UR-FUNNY) and an audio-visual-dominant dataset (MUStARD). Pathway isolation shows that IAF preserves the dominant modality's internal accuracy at its unimodal ceiling across all tested configurations, whereas symmetric fusion degrades it by up to 18.5% on MultiHuSE. IAF improves over the strongest unimodal baseline by up to 8.25%.

</details>


### [204] [Beyond Capability Benchmarks: Learning Operational Fingerprints of LLM Cloud Services from Production Incident Metadata](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26332)
*Meiwei Zhang,Eduardo Miranda,Bruce Baynes,Suvigya Jain,Wanlong Chen,Tao He,Sergey Borodavkin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OpEmbed is a framework that learns compact operational fingerprints of LLM cloud services from support-case metadata, using temporal contrastive learning and reconstruction techniques to improve operational forecasting and support planning without accessing case text.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model selection and service planning for LLM services rely on capability benchmarks that don't reveal operational behavior post-deployment, necessitating a method to understand real-world operational characteristics.

Method: Aggregates model-time windows into 8-channel operational signatures; learns low-dimensional representations via temporal contrastive learning, cross-view reconstruction, and generational-ordinality regularization; uses privacy-preserving support-case metadata only.

Result: Evaluated on 33,000+ production support cases across 7 LLM families over 26 months at Google Cloud: recovers interpretable family/version structure, improves leave-one-model-out operational forecasting over baselines, remains useful with limited early-window data, and supports cross-model fault-type transfer.

Conclusion: OpEmbed successfully enables practical applications including model onboarding, support readiness assessment, and operational monitoring, demonstrating value for real production LLM systems at scale.

Abstract: Managed LLM services are now part of real production systems, but model selection and service planning still rely heavily on capability benchmarks that reveal little about operational behavior after deployment. We present Operational Embedding (OpEmbed), a framework for learning compact operational fingerprints of LLM cloud services from structured, privacy-preserving support-case metadata, without using case text. OpEmbed aggregates model--time windows into an eight-channel operational signature and learns a low-dimensional representation via temporal contrastive learning, cross-view reconstruction, and generational-ordinality regularization. Evaluated on more than 33,000 production support cases spanning seven LLM families over 26 months at Google Cloud, OpEmbed recovers interpretable family- and version-level structure, improves leave-one-model-out operational forecasting over non-learned baselines, remains useful under limited early-window data, and supports cross-model fault-type transfer. We report the practical lessons learned from building and evaluating this tool for model onboarding, support readiness assessment, and operational monitoring.

</details>


### [205] [CG4AI: A Column Generation Framework for Training AI Models Under Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26375)
*Youcef Magnouche,Abderrahmane Driouch,Sébastien Martin,Pierre Bauguion*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CG4AI is a framework that creates convex combinations of AI models while enforcing linear constraints on outputs. It uses a master LP for optimal mixture weights, a pricing subproblem for new models guided by dual variables, and cutting-plane for extended feasibility. Demonstrated on MNIST classification and multi-commodity flow routing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard ML training minimizes loss but doesn't guarantee predefined rules or constraints on outputs. Many real-world applications (autonomous systems, network routing) require such guarantees to ensure safety and reliability.

Method: CG4AI framework: (1) Master linear program determines optimal mixture weights for model combination; (2) Pricing subproblem generates new models guided by LP dual variables, focusing on violated constraints; (3) Cutting-plane procedure extends feasibility guarantees beyond training set.

Result: Four constraint applications on MNIST: learning from constraints alone, improving adversarial robustness, correcting misclassifications, and enforcing output relabeling. On multi-commodity flow: link capacity constraints enforced on neural network routing predictors. CG4AI reliably produces feasible predictors with better accuracy than single-model baselines on MNIST and SNDLIB benchmark networks.

Conclusion: CG4AI successfully combines multiple AI models while guaranteeing linear constraints are satisfied, achieving superior accuracy over single-model baselines while maintaining feasibility on both image classification and network routing problems.

Abstract: Standard machine-learning training minimizes a loss function over a dataset, but does not guarantee that the resulting model will satisfy predefined rules or constraints on its outputs. In many real-world applications, ranging from autonomous systems to network routing, such guarantees are essential. We propose CG4AI, a framework that builds a convex combination of AI models while enforcing linear constraints on the combined output. A master linear program (LP) determines the optimal mixture weights, while a pricing subproblem generates new models guided by LP dual variables, focusing attention on the most violated constraints. A cutting-plane procedure extends feasibility guarantees beyond the training set. We apply CG4AI to two problems: (i) digit classification on MNIST, where we demonstrate four distinct uses of constraints, learning from constraints alone, improving adversarial robustness, correcting misclassified examples, and enforcing output relabeling; and (ii) the multi-commodity flow problem, where link capacity constraints are enforced on neural-network routing predictors. Experiments on MNIST and standard SNDLIB benchmark networks show that CG4AI reliably produces feasible predictors while achieving better accuracy than single-model baselines.

</details>


### [206] [The Latent Diagnostic Taxonomy: A Framework for Constructing Classifiers and Diagnosing Their Decisions, Applied to Prompt Injection Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26423)
*Jaturong Kongmanee,Smile Thanapattheerakul*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a Latent Diagnostic Taxonomy framework that combines a dimensionality-optimized classifier with a diagnostic system to identify which confident classifier decisions can be trusted. The framework identifies vulnerable prompts through latent support vectors and categorizes them into actionable zones (Safe, Heuristic Bias, Heuristic Override, Insufficient Context) with remediation strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors observed that a substantial fraction (~77%) of confident classifier decisions are not robust to removing a single token, revealing two failure patterns: confidence calibration failure and exploitable shortcuts. This highlights a critical need for diagnostic tools to assess classifier reliability beyond accuracy metrics.

Method: The framework has three components: (1) Constructing a dimensionality-optimized classifier using cross-validated performance to select embedding dimensionality empirically; (2) Identifying ~29% of training examples as latent support vectors representing influential prompts for detecting tokens that alter predicted labels; (3) Using these tokens and attack magnitudes to build a diagnostic taxonomy for categorizing prompts into treatment zones.

Result: Applying the framework to a prompt injection classifier revealed that ~77% of confident decisions are brittle when a single token is removed. This brittleness separates into two distinct failure patterns: confidence calibration failure and genuinely exploitable shortcuts. The taxonomy provides end-to-end guidelines for flagging prompts requiring different treatments.

Conclusion: The Latent Diagnostic Taxonomy framework offers a practical approach for identifying trustworthy classifier decisions and diagnosing failure patterns. It provides actionable remediation strategies for each taxonomy zone, enabling more reliable deployment of classifiers in safety-critical applications.

Abstract: This paper proposes a framework for constructing a classifier as a safeguard layer, and for developing a complementary diagnostic that identifies which of the classifier's confident decisions can be trusted. This framework, the Latent Diagnostic Taxonomy, consists of (i) constructing a dimensionality-optimized classifier, in which the embedding dimensionality is empirically selected via cross-validated performance rather than fixed a priori, (ii) locating a relatively small set of latent support vectors (~ 29% of total training examples) representing influential prompts for identifying tokens that alter the classifier's predicted labels, and (iii) utilizing such tokens and their associated attack magnitudes for constructing a diagnostic taxonomy. This diagnostic taxonomy provides an end-to-end guideline for flagging prompts that require different treatments: rely Safely on the classifier's decision; flag Heuristic Bias and Heuristic Override cases; route Insufficient Context cases for further human/safety review. Applying the framework to a classifier trained on a public prompt injection dataset, we find that a substantial fraction of its confident decisions (~ 77%) are not robust to removing a single token, and that this brittleness separates into two distinct failure patterns: a confidence calibration failure and a genuinely exploitable shortcut. For each zone of the taxonomy, we also recommend strategies for remediating diagnosed prompts. We illustrate the framework as a series of steps, demonstrating how each step operates.

</details>


### [207] [FedCMAPSS: A Benchmark for Federated Learning in Remaining Useful Life Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26433)
*Amelia Sorrenti,Matteo Pennisi,Concetto Spampinato,Simone Palazzo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces FedCMAPSS, a benchmark framework for federated remaining useful life (RUL) estimation based on the NASA C-MAPSS dataset, addressing the lack of standardized evaluation frameworks for federated learning in predictive maintenance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data-driven prognostics and health management is crucial for Industry 4.0, but developing robust RUL estimation models is hindered by limited run-to-failure data. While federated learning enables collaborative model training without sharing raw sensor data, existing research lacks a common evaluation framework for comparing federated approaches in this domain.

Method: The authors develop FedCMAPSS, a benchmark based on the widely-used NASA C-MAPSS dataset. They define five standardized tasks simulating real-world industrial challenges—from ideal IID settings to extreme statistical heterogeneity—and systematically evaluate state-of-the-art federated optimization algorithms across multiple neural network architectures. Source code and data splits are made publicly available.

Result: The paper establishes reproducible baselines for federated RUL estimation, providing a standardized foundation for developing and comparing federated predictive maintenance solutions. The benchmark enables systematic comparison of different federated optimization algorithms under varying levels of data heterogeneity.

Conclusion: FedCMAPSS addresses a critical gap in federated learning for predictive maintenance by providing a common evaluation framework. This work enables standardized benchmarking, facilitates reproducible research, and aims to accelerate the development of practical federated RUL estimation solutions for industrial applications.

Abstract: Data-driven prognostics and health management has emerged as a key enabler for Industry 4.0, yet the development of robust remaining useful life (RUL) estimation models is often limited by the scarcity of run-to-failure data. While federated learning offers a promising paradigm to collaboratively train predictive models without sharing sensor data, research efforts have operated so far in the absence of a common evaluation framework. To address this gap, this paper introduces FedCMAPSS, a benchmark for federated RUL estimation based on the commonly-used NASA C-MAPSS dataset. We define a set of five standardized tasks designed to simulate real-world industrial challenges, ranging from ideal IID settings to extreme statistical heterogeneity, and conduct a systematic evaluation of state-of-the-art federated optimization algorithms across multiple neural architectures. By establishing reproducible baselines and making the source code and data splits publicly available, this work aims to provide a standard foundation for developing and comparing federated predictive maintenance solutions.

</details>


### [208] [NeoTriFuse: Reliability-Aware Multimodal Fusion under Missingness Heterogeneity for Neonatal Mortality Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26436)
*Jiyuan Tian,Qincheng Shen,Ye Lin,Yu Gao,Haohui Lu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NeoTriFuse is a reliability-aware multimodal fusion framework for predicting neonatal mortality from bedside monitoring data, treating missingness as an explicit signal rather than a preprocessing issue. It achieves F1=0.6736 and AUROC=0.9454.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neonatal mortality prediction is challenging due to extreme class imbalance, heterogeneous clinical risk factors, multi-scale temporal dynamics, and substantial missingness in bedside monitoring data.

Method: NeoTriFuse integrates static perinatal variables, local-global temporal encoders, and patient-level statistical summaries through reliability-guided gating mechanisms. It models missingness as a dynamic reliability signal that modulates modality contributions during fusion, while jointly optimizing mortality prediction and length-of-stay objectives.

Result: NeoTriFuse achieves F1 score of 0.6736 ± 0.0216 and AUROC of 0.9454 ± 0.0056. Ablation studies show local-global temporal architecture and patient-level summary branch contribute most, while reliability-aware gating improves threshold-dependent metrics under heterogeneous observation completeness.

Conclusion: Reliability-aware multimodal fusion is a practical approach for neonatal mortality prediction under realistic clinical missingness conditions, with stable performance across hyperparameter settings.

Abstract: Neonatal mortality risk prediction from bedside monitoring data remains challenging due to extreme class imbalance, heterogeneous clinical risk factors, multi-scale temporal dynamics, and substantial missingness. We propose NeoTriFuse, a reliability-aware multimodal fusion framework for missingness-heterogeneous neonatal monitoring data. Unlike conventional multimodal approaches that treat missingness primarily as a preprocessing issue, NeoTriFuse models missingness as an explicit reliability signal that dynamically modulates modality contributions during fusion. The framework integrates static perinatal variables, local-global temporal encoders, and patient-level statistical summaries through reliability-guided gating mechanisms, while jointly optimizing mortality prediction and an auxiliary length-of-stay objective. NeoTriFuse achieves competitive performance, with an F1 score of 0.6736 +/- 0.0216 and an AUROC of 0.9454 +/- 0.0056. Ablation studies indicate that the local-global temporal architecture and patient-level summary branch contribute most substantially to predictive performance, while reliability-aware gating provides additional improvements on threshold-dependent metrics under heterogeneous observation completeness. Sensitivity analyses further suggest stable performance across nearby hyperparameter settings. Overall, the findings support reliability-aware multimodal fusion as a practical approach for neonatal mortality prediction under realistic clinical missingness conditions.

</details>


### [209] [Subgraph Filtering for Fair Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26437)
*Haohui Lu,jiyuan Tian,Fangyu Zhou,Shahadat Uddin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SF-GNN is a lightweight, architecture-agnostic framework that mitigates structural bias in GNNs by identifying bias-prone edges (combining sensitive homophily with structural propagation amplifiers like hub participation and triadic closure) and applying stochastic edge filtering during message passing, combined with a statistical-parity regularizer with warm-up schedule.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graph neural networks can exhibit unfair behavior even when sensitive attributes are excluded from node features, because graph topology and message passing propagate group-correlated signals under sensitive homophily. Existing fairness-aware GNN methods mainly constrain representations or prediction distributions at a global level, without explicitly controlling the local structural pathways through which biased information propagates during aggregation.

Method: 1) Identify bias-prone edges by combining sensitive homophily with structural propagation amplifiers (hub participation and triadic closure); 2) Incorporate stochastic edge filtering into each message-passing step to selectively downweight or remove these edges while preserving remaining graph structure; 3) Training incorporates a statistical-parity regularizer with a warm-up schedule to stabilize optimization.

Result: Experiments on five benchmark datasets show that SF-GNN achieves consistent fairness improvements while maintaining competitive predictive performance, leading to a better fairness--accuracy trade-off than recent fairness-aware GNN baselines.

Conclusion: SF-GNN effectively mitigates structural bias at its source by controlling local structural pathways during message passing, offering a lightweight and architecture-agnostic solution that outperforms existing fairness-aware GNN methods in balancing fairness and accuracy.

Abstract: Graph neural networks (GNNs) can exhibit unfair behavior even when sensitive attributes are excluded from node features, because graph topology and message passing propagate group-correlated signals under sensitive homophily. Existing fairness-aware GNN methods mainly constrain representations or prediction distributions at a global level, without explicitly controlling the local structural pathways through which biased information propagates during aggregation. We propose Subgraph Filtering for Fair Graph Neural Networks (SF-GNN), a lightweight and architecture-agnostic framework that mitigates structural bias at its source. SF-GNN identifies bias-prone edges by combining sensitive homophily with structural propagation amplifiers, including hub participation and triadic closure. It then incorporates stochastic edge filtering into each message-passing step to selectively downweight or remove these edges while preserving the remaining graph structure. Training further incorporates a statistical-parity regularizer with a warm-up schedule to stabilize optimization. Experiments on five benchmark datasets show that SF-GNN achieves consistent fairness improvements while maintaining competitive predictive performance, leading to a better fairness--accuracy trade-off than recent fairness-aware GNN baselines.

</details>


### [210] [Toward Equitable Low-Carbon Mobility: Fairness-Aware Demand Prediction for Expanding Bike-Sharing Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26451)
*Man Luo,Yixuan Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FairGIN is a fairness-aware graph neural network for demand prediction in expanding bike-sharing systems that addresses cold-start prediction challenges and reduces income-based disparities through three components: Expansion-Simulated Increment Training, Attention-Based Knowledge Transfer, and Fairness-Aware Optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bike-sharing systems are expanding but face two key challenges: 1) Cold-start prediction - newly deployed stations lack historical ridership records, causing distribution gaps between training and inference for graph-based models; 2) Equity concerns - historical demand data may encode structural inequalities, where lower ridership in low-income neighborhoods reflects limited infrastructure access rather than actual demand, potentially reinforcing existing mobility disparities when models are trained directly on such data.

Method: FairGIN integrates three components: (1) Expansion-Simulated Increment Training - stochastically simulates network expansion during training to reduce cold-start distribution gap; (2) Attention-Based Knowledge Transfer - combines station-adaptive temperature scaling with orthogonal embedding alignment to transfer representations from data-rich existing stations to data-sparse new stations; (3) Fairness-Aware Optimization - introduces income-stratified regularization and an equity-calibrated deployment score to support more inclusive station placement.

Result: Experiments on NYC and Seattle datasets demonstrate that FairGIN achieves state-of-the-art predictive accuracy across diverse expansion scenarios while substantially reducing income-based disparities without compromising overall system efficiency.

Conclusion: FairGIN successfully addresses both the technical challenge of cold-start prediction in expanding bike-sharing networks and the societal challenge of equity in station placement, offering a principled approach that balances predictive accuracy with fairness considerations.

Abstract: Bike-sharing systems are an important component of low-carbon urban mobility, but continued expansion creates challenges in both cold-start prediction and equitable resource allocation. Newly deployed stations lack historical ridership records, causing a mismatch between training and inference for graph-based models on evolving networks. Historical demand may also encode structural inequalities, as lower ridership in low-income neighborhoods can reflect limited infrastructure access rather than weak latent demand. Models trained directly on such data may therefore reinforce existing mobility disparities. We propose FairGIN, a fairness-aware graph neural network for demand prediction in expanding bike-sharing systems. FairGIN integrates three components. Expansion-Simulated Increment Training stochastically simulates network expansion during training to reduce the cold-start distribution gap. Attention-Based Knowledge Transfer combines station-adaptive temperature scaling with orthogonal embedding alignment to transfer representations from data-rich existing stations to data-sparse new stations. Fairness-Aware Optimization introduces income-stratified regularization and an equity-calibrated deployment score to support more inclusive station placement. Experiments on NYC and Seattle demonstrate that FairGIN achieves state-of-the-art predictive accuracy across diverse expansion scenarios while substantially reducing income-based disparities without compromising overall system efficiency.

</details>


### [211] [Distributed Training using an Intelligent Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26453)
*Nihar Shah,Ben Blier*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes making wide area networks (WAN) active participants in distributed machine learning training by leveraging multicast technology for outbound traffic and in-line FPGAs for inbound traffic aggregation, combined with a topology-aware optimization framework that generates synchronized rotating cliques to maximize information exchange across network nodes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Distributed training across WAN faces significant challenges due to limited bandwidth, high latency, and uneven network topology. Current approaches treat the network as a passive carrier, missing opportunities to optimize parameter exchange between geographically distributed compute islands.

Method: The paper proposes a co-design approach combining systems and algorithmic solutions: (1) Systems层面 - leveraging multicast technology for efficient outbound traffic replication and in-line FPGAs for inbound traffic aggregation to address egress/ingress bottlenecks; (2) Algorithm层面 - developing an optimization framework that produces synchronization schedules (rotating cliques of islands) tailored to the underlying network topology and available hardware capabilities.

Result: The approach was evaluated on a nine-city topology modeled on the DoubleZero network (a live programmable WAN). The results demonstrate how optimal synchronization schedules shift dynamically based on network capabilities, effectively narrowing the performance gap compared to colocated training.

Conclusion: By treating the network as an active participant in distributed training and co-designing both the network infrastructure (multicast, FPGAs) with optimization algorithms (rotating cliques), the paper shows WAN distributed training can approach the performance of data center training, opening new possibilities for geographically distributed machine learning.

Abstract: Distributed training across a wide area network (WAN) is challenging, as continuous parameter exchange by islands of compute is constrained by limited bandwidth, high latency, and uneven topology. We propose making the network an active participant in training. On the systems side, such networks should leverage (i) multicast technology to replicate outbound traffic and (ii) in-line FPGAs to aggregate inbound traffic, to ease egress and ingress bottlenecks. These technologies are used for training across workers within a data center, but this paper extends them to the WAN. On the algorithms side, we develop an optimization framework that produces rich synchronization schedules (namely, rotating cliques of islands) around the underlying network topology and these technologies, to maximize information exchange. Finally, we illustrate this on a nine-city topology modeled on the DoubleZero network, a live programmable WAN equipped with both technologies, and show how the optimal schedules shift with the network's capabilities. Together, these can narrow the gap to the gold standard of colocated training.

</details>


### [212] [Diff Mining: Logit Differences Reveal Finetuning Objectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26462)
*Greg Kocher,Robert West,Clément Dumas,Julian Minder*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Diff Mining is a framework that identifies what a finetuned language model has learned by comparing its output logits to those of its base model,surfacing amplified tokens that serve as a fingerprint of finetuning objectives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Finetuning is widely used to refine model behaviors, but it remains unclear what specific behaviors emerge during this process. As models become more capable,understanding finetuning becomes critical, especially since unwanted behaviors may arise. Existing model diffing methods often require model internals and don't scale well to large models.

Method: Diff Mining consists of two modular stages: (1) extracting per-context logit differences between finetuned and base models on a reference corpus, and (2) aggregating signals using either a simple Top-K frequency method or NMF-based approach to disentangle multiple finetuning objectives into distinct token clusters. The method only requires access to output logits, not model internals.

Result: Diff Mining significantly outperforms state-of-the-art model diffing methods on finetune domain detection in both token relevance identification and downstream interpretability agent performance. On models with injected biases, it identifies more than one-third of biases without targeted probing.

Conclusion: Diff Mining shows promise for developing auditing tools to detect finetuning objectives, providing a scalable and interpretable approach for understanding what language models learn during finetuning.

Abstract: Finetuning has become the gold standard for refining existing behaviors and inducing new ones in language models, yet it often remains unclear exactly which behaviors emerge during this process. As models grow ever more capable, understanding finetuning better becomes increasingly important, particularly since unwanted behaviors may arise during finetuning. In this paper, we introduce Diff Mining, a simple yet effective framework for identifying what a finetuned model has learned by comparing its logits to those of its base model. Diff Mining effectively surfaces salient tokens that are amplified in the finetuned model, serving as a fingerprint of its training -- even on text unrelated to the finetuning domain. Unlike many existing model diffing methods which require model internals, Diff Mining only needs access to output logits and scales to large models. The framework consists of two modular stages: (i) extracting per-context logit differences between the finetuned and base models on a reference corpus, and (ii) aggregating the resulting signals to construct an interpretable token set representing the finetune. For aggregation, we explore both a simple Top-K frequency method and a Non-negative Matrix Factorization (NMF)-based approach for disentangling multiple finetuning objectives into distinct token clusters. Empirically, Diff Mining succeeds across diverse settings: on finetune domain detection, it significantly outperforms state-of-the-art model diffing methods both in identifying relevant tokens and in downstream performance when an interpretability agent is given access to the extracted token set; on models with injected biases, it identifies more than one third of the biases without targeted probing. Overall, our framework shows promise in developing auditing tools to detect finetuning objectives.

</details>


### [213] [Active Curriculum Refinement for Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26469)
*Zhenya Liu,Yuxin Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: In many reinforcement learning (RL) domains, environments are connected by prerequisite relations, such as difficulty-increasing edits or parameter increments, which induce a directed acyclic curriculum graph (DAG). Although this structure is often exploited only implicitly, explicitly modeling it can improve training. We introduce PATH, a curriculum-learning framework that performs active learning over the curriculum graph. PATH first expands coverage by sampling diverse curriculum paths and then reallocates training toward regions that remain unmastered. Experiments across diverse environments show that PATH explicitly leverages the graph structure to achieve strong robustness and generalization.

</details>


### [214] [Shared Actors Need Not Share Critics: Effects of Value Mismatch in Parallel Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26481)
*Zhenya Liu,Yang Meng,Zhuokai Zhao,Xuefeng Liu,Yuxin Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper identifies a "value mismatch" problem in parallel reinforcement learning where a single critic across multiple environments must reconcile different value targets for the same input, leading to shifted advantages and degraded learning. The proposed solution is conditioning the critic on an environment index (multihead conditional critic), which improves performance significantly, including 40.8% improvement across 16 Procgen games.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When training a single policy in parallel across multiple environments (procedurally generated levels, randomized dynamics, or curricula), implementations commonly use one critic across all sampled environments. However, different environments can assign different expected returns to the same input, causing a critic without environment information to reconcile distinct value targets, systematically shifting sampled advantages within individual environments.

Method: The authors use illustrative bandit models with multiple environments and a common optimal arm to characterize how value mismatch redistributes sampled policy updates. They propose a minimal intervention: giving only a logged environment index to the critic so it can separate value targets (multihead conditional critic). Experiments are conducted on CartPole, MuJoCo, BipedalWalker, and Procgen settings.

Result: The oracle processes using no baseline, shared value, or environment-specific value have the same mean logit update at a fixed policy and converge to the same optimal policy, but their realized learning paths differ sharply. The multihead conditional critic exposes predicted shifted values, advantages, and performance gaps. In BipedalWalker and Procgen settings, it yields more stable learning and higher returns. Across all 16 Procgen games, the multihead conditional critic improves aggregate normalized return on 600 unseen levels per game by 40.8%.

Conclusion: The theory identifies value mismatch as a direct mechanism through which critic sharing can degrade stochastic learning dynamics, not captured by scalar estimator variance alone. The experiments show that conditioning on an environment index is broadly effective in parallel reinforcement learning settings.

Abstract: When a single policy is trained in parallel across multiple environments of the same task, such as procedurally generated levels, randomized dynamics, or curricula, implementations commonly use one critic across all sampled environments. Yet different environments can assign different expected returns to the same input visible to the critic. A critic without environment information must then reconcile distinct value targets, systematically shifting the sampled advantages within individual environments. Using illustrative bandit models with multiple environments and a common optimal arm, we characterize how this value mismatch redistributes sampled policy updates, reinforcing unhelpful actions while attenuating or even reversing useful ones. The oracle processes using no baseline, the shared value, or the value specific to the sampled environment have the same mean logit update at a fixed policy and converge to the same optimal policy, yet their realized learning paths can differ sharply. The analysis motivates a minimal intervention: give only a logged environment index to the critic so that it can separate the value targets. Controlled CartPole and MuJoCo experiments expose the predicted shifted values, advantages, and performance gaps. In the more complex BipedalWalker and Procgen settings, the same intervention yields more stable learning and higher returns. Across all $16$ Procgen games, the multihead conditional critic improves aggregate normalized return on $600$ unseen levels per game by $40.8\%$.
