We further complemented the runtime evaluation with static analysis of the generated source code and an exploratory comparison with human-written implementations of related backend scenarios. The results show that memory usage is the most consistent indicator of potential software aging, with statistically significant upward trends in most application-language combinations, while response time and throughput exhibit more heterogeneous behavior. Static analysis identified plausible code-level aging mechanisms, and the comparison with human-written systems showed that aging trends can also emerge in manually developed implementations. These findings indicate that functional correctness alone is insufficient to assess the operational reliability of LLM-generated software before deployment in continuously running environments.

</details>


### [288] [Spec2Vision: Contract-Guided Delivery of AI-Generated Computer Vision Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26400)
*Ghfran Jabour,Sergey Ivanov*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Spec2Vision is an experimental framework for generating and evaluating specification-grounded computer vision pipeline bundles through a staged runtime that maintains explicit task contracts across synthesis, screening, testing, and bounded repair. The benchmark evaluates 850 runs across 17 CV tasks and 10 executable conditions, demonstrating that keeping the task contract explicit across staged generation, checking, and repair yields the strongest results (81/85 evaluator-test passes).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem addressed is that generated computer-vision code can be runnable but fail to satisfy the task contract enforced by a downstream evaluator. This gap between runnability and successful task delivery is the key motivation for the research.

Method: Spec2Vision uses a staged runtime approach with four main phases: synthesis, screening, testing, and bounded repair. The benchmark evaluates 17 CV tasks, 10 executable conditions, and 5 repeats per task-condition cell (850 primary runs). The framework tests different configurations by removing components: structural repair, compatibility scaffolding, and generator preflight. Single-agent baselines with progressively richer task specifications are also evaluated.

Result: Primary results: Spec2Vision achieves 81/85 evaluator-test passes. When removing components: structural repair drops to 55/85, compatibility scaffolding to 58/85, and generator preflight to 39/85. Baselines range from 17/85 (lightweight task grounding) to 35/85 (direct source-spec exposure). The lightweight baseline is core-runnable in 85/85 runs but only achieves 17/85 evaluator-test passes and 6/85 strict-delivery successes.

Conclusion: The strongest evidence comes from keeping the task contract explicit across staged generation, checking, and repair. The key finding is that runnability is not equivalent to delivery - code can execute without satisfying the task requirements. The framework's success relies on the combination of all components working together.

Abstract: Generated computer-vision code can be runnable without satisfying the task contract enforced by a downstream evaluator. We study that gap with Spec2Vision, an experimental framework for producing and evaluating specification-grounded CV pipeline bundles through a staged runtime that keeps the task contract explicit across synthesis, screening, testing, and bounded repair. The benchmark evaluates 17 CV tasks, 10 executable conditions, and 5 repeats per task-condition cell, for 850 primary runs. In the primary 850-run evaluation, Spec2Vision reaches 81/85 evaluator-test passes; removing structural repair drops to 55/85, compatibility scaffolding to 58/85, and generator preflight to 39/85. The executable single-agent baselines expose progressively richer task specifications to the model, culminating in direct source-spec exposure, yet remain much weaker overall, from 17/85 for lightweight task grounding to 35/85 evaluator-test passes. The lightweight baseline nevertheless remains core-runnable in 85/85 runs but reaches only 17/85 evaluator-test passes and 6/85 strict-delivery successes, showing that runnability is not equivalent to delivery. Across this benchmark, the strongest evidence comes from keeping the task contract explicit across staged generation, checking, and repair. Artifacts are provided to support audit of run bundles, model-visible inputs, and derived tables.

</details>


### [289] [STILL: Recovering Lowered STL Semantics for LLM-assisted C++ Decompilation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26408)
*Xiaohan Wang,Kevin Leach*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: STILL is a system that predicts STL container semantics from stripped C++ control-flow graphs and renders them as hints for LLM-based decompilation refinement, improving executability from 8.9% (raw Ghidra) to 28.4%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-assisted decompilation struggles with stripped C++ functions using STL because compilation, optimization, and symbol stripping remove container types and library-call structure information that traditional decompilers fail to recover.

Method: STILL predicts function-level STL container semantics from stripped control-flow graphs and renders them as compact hints for LLM refinement. It uses a structured semantic interface approach.

Result: On StlBench, STILL achieves the strongest cross-dataset results for stable string and vector slices. On stripped HumanEval decompilation, DeepSeek-chat with STILL hints achieves 28.4% executability vs 17.4% without hints and 8.9% with raw Ghidra. The utility of hints is downstream-backbone-dependent.

Conclusion: STILL's structured semantic interface successfully recovers STL container semantics from stripped binaries, enabling significant improvements in LLM-assisted decompilation executability. However, decompilation-specialized models require lightweight adaptation to fully benefit from this interface.

Abstract: LLM-assisted decompilation improves readability and re-executability, but still underperforms on stripped C++ functions that use the Standard Template Library (STL). Compilation, optimization, and symbol stripping remove or obscure source-level semantics such as container types and library-call structure, while traditional decompiler output often fails to recover them. We present STILL, a structured semantic interface that predicts function-level STL container semantics from stripped control-flow graphs and renders them as compact hints for LLM refinement. On StlBench, STILL predicts common container-level STL semantics, with the strongest cross-dataset results for stable string and vector slices. On stripped HumanEval decompilation, these hints enable DeepSeek-chat refinement to reach 28.4% executability, compared with 17.4% for no-hint refinement and 8.9% for raw Ghidra decompilation; hint utility is downstream-backbone-dependent, with decompilation-specialized models requiring lightweight adaptation to benefit from the same interface.

</details>


### [290] [DeepRepro: State-Aware Subplanning for Paper-to-Code Reproduction in Evolving Repositories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26557)
*Hongru Song,Ruqing Zhang,Jiafeng Guo,Xueqi Cheng,Maarten de Rijke*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: DeepRepro is a state-aware framework for automatic ML paper-to-code reproduction using execution-state-aware subplanning. It dynamically transforms evolving repository states and runtime feedback into fine-grained implementation subplans, outperforming existing code-agent baselines on PaperBench Code-Dev.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automatic ML paper-to-code reproduction remains a challenging long-horizon problem requiring constructing and maintaining fully functional repositories with continuously evolving states. Existing systems rely on static upfront planning followed by sequential file-level generation, leading to inconsistencies as dependencies, interfaces, and execution feedback change over time.

Method: DeepRepro uses execution-state-aware subplanning that dynamically transforms evolving repository states and runtime feedback into fine-grained implementation subplans, keeping planning aligned with execution throughout repository construction. The framework incorporates repository-aware orchestration and a lightweight process-aware interface for transparent monitoring of long-horizon reproduction.

Result: Experiments on PaperBench Code-Dev show that DeepRepro consistently outperforms strong scientific and commercial code-agent baselines.

Conclusion: DeepRepro's state-aware approach with dynamic subplanning effectively addresses the challenges of automatic ML paper-to-code reproduction by maintaining alignment between planning and execution throughout the repository construction process.

Abstract: Recent advances in agentic large language models (LLMs) have enabled increasingly autonomous software engineering workflows, yet automatic machine learning (ML) paper-to-code reproduction remains a challenging long-horizon problem. Unlike conventional code generation, this task requires constructing and maintaining a fully functional repository whose state continuously evolves during execution. Existing systems typically rely on static upfront planning followed by sequential file-level generation, which often leads to inconsistencies as dependencies, interfaces, and execution feedback change over time. We propose DeepRepro, a state-aware framework for paper-to-code reproduction based on execution-state-aware subplanning. DeepRepro dynamically transforms evolving repository states and runtime feedback into fine-grained implementation subplans, keeping planning aligned with execution throughout repository construction. The framework further incorporates repository-aware orchestration and a lightweight process-aware interface for transparent monitoring of long-horizon reproduction. Experiments on PaperBench Code-Dev show that DeepRepro consistently outperforms strong scientific and commercial code-agent baselines.

</details>


### [291] [The Thousand-Graph Hypothesis: A Testable Hypothesis of Task-Conditioned Relation Materialization in Repository-Level Code Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26602)
*Fei Ding*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper proposes an entity-only external interface with task-conditioned relation materialization during inference to handle large software repositories that exceed model context limits. A two-layer index architecture separates global routing from local entity focus, achieving 95.6% success rate on SWE-bench Verified without pre-built entity-relation edges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large software repositories often exceed model context limits. Training repository knowledge into models is costly and quickly becomes stale. Local retrieval methods can miss scattered requirements, and explicit relation graphs require ongoing maintenance burden.

Method: The proposed method uses an entity-only external interface with task-conditioned relation materialization during inference. It employs a two-layer index architecture: (1) global routing layer and (2) local entity focus layer. The system evaluates under zero pre-built entity-relation edges condition.

Result: On SWE-bench Verified with DeepSeek-V4-Flash, the success rates are: base condition (without the proposed method): 92.1%, one-layer condition: 94.2%, and two-layer condition: 95.6%.

Conclusion: The two-layer index with task-conditioned relation materialization effectively improves code task performance, achieving a 3.5% improvement over the base model. The approach eliminates the need for pre-built entity-relation edges, reducing maintenance burden while maintaining high accuracy.

Abstract: Large software repositories are often beyond model context limits. Training repository knowledge into models is costly and quickly stale, while local retrieval can miss scattered requirements, and explicit relation graphs add ongoing maintenance burden. We propose an entity-only external interface with task-conditioned relation materialization during inference. A two-layer index separates global routing from local entity focus and is evaluated on DeepSeek-V4-Flash and SWE-bench Verified. The base, one-layer, and two-layer conditions achieve 92.1%, 94.2%, and 95.6% success, respectively, under zero pre-built entity-relation edges.

</details>


### [292] [Processing/p5 Defined through Practice and Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26614)
*Kit Kuksenok,Lee Tusman*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper examines how Processing/p5 libraries maintain consistent creative coding priorities across different programming languages (JavaScript and Lua), proposing a framework of software decision-making aspects that define Processing/p5 regardless of host language.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to understand what constitutes the essence of Processing/p5 across different language ecosystems, addressing the tension between host language affordances and the shared characteristics of creative coding tools.

Method: Case study methodology examining two implementations of Processing/p5 in different host languages (JavaScript and Lua), with comparative analysis against other exploratory creative tools.

Result: A proposed list of software decision-making guiding aspects that constitute Processing/p5's identity, with examples of how these aspects can be operationalized differently in various tools.

Conclusion: The framework highlights opportunities for learning, research, and artistic practice through creating new Processing/p5 libraries for creative coding and algorithmic art.

Abstract: Processing/p5 libraries across different programming languages enact consistent priorities for creative coding as a designed experience. While different programming language ecosystems, like Java and JavaScript, are each associated with their own affordances, community norms, and patterns of use, Processing/p5 sketches across these languages share similarities. Based on case studies of building an implementation of Processing/p5 in two host languages, JavaScript and Lua, we propose a list of software decision-making guiding aspects that constitute Processing/p5, regardless of host language. We discuss this framework in the context of decisions in other exploratory and creative tools that demonstrate how each of the guiding aspects can be operationalized differently than in the case studies. The proposed list highlights opportunities for learning, research, and artistic practice through creation of new Processing/p5 libraries for creative coding and algorithmic art.

</details>


### [293] [FaultLens: Learning Compact Behavioral Test Suites for Generated Operational Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26746)
*Zeming Liu,Hang Lyu,Jingtao Zhang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: FaultLens is a method for learning compact behavioral test suites to validate generated operational programs. It uses a sparse outcome cache storing fault-probe kill relations and combines a fault-driven greedy component with a mutation-independent diversity component to achieve high fault coverage (99%) while using only 1.2-2.0% of the exhaustive test domain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generated operational programs are typically validated using either a few hand-written examples (which miss sparse boundary and interaction faults) or exhaustive regression suites (which are unnecessarily expensive). There is a need for a method that achieves high fault coverage efficiently while maintaining an auditable connection to executed evidence.

Method: FaultLens executes a rich probe domain once and stores the fault-probe kill relation as a sparse outcome cache. It learns probe orderings from earlier program generations using two complementary components: (1) a fault-driven greedy component that exploits known kill structure, and (2) a mutation-independent diversity component that covers probe families, cases, templates, and temporal bins. These components alternate in a hybrid approach that remains useful when new programs contain fault mechanisms absent from ordering construction.

Result: A 32-probe hybrid learned on generations 1-3 covers 576/582 (99.0%) dynamically killable faults in generations 4-5 using only 1.2-2.0% of the exhaustive domain. When an entire fault family is withheld from training, diversity raises scenario-family macro coverage from 84.6% to 94.9%. In downstream deployment, a conservative admission rule reduces severe tail regressions from 15/20 to 0/20 program-environment groups.

Conclusion: FaultLens provides a prioritized evidence mechanism for testing generated operational programs, not a proof of correctness. It makes its budget, evidence source, generalization split, and misses explicit. The method achieves near-complete fault coverage (99%) with minimal test suite size (1.2-2.0% of exhaustive testing), and generalizes well to unseen fault families through its diversity component.

Abstract: Generated operational programs are often validated with either a few hand-written examples or exhaustive regression suites. The former can miss sparse boundary and interaction faults, while the latter can be unnecessarily expensive. We introduce FaultLens, a method for learning compact behavioral test suites while preserving an auditable connection to executed evidence. It executes a rich probe domain once, stores the fault-probe kill relation as a sparse outcome cache, and learns probe orderings only from earlier program generations. A fault-driven greedy component exploits known kill structure, while a mutation-independent diversity component covers probe families, cases, templates, and temporal bins. Their alternating hybrid remains useful when a new program contains a fault mechanism absent from ordering construction.
  We evaluate twenty generated operational policies across four environments, ten execution seeds, 1,200 measured run summaries, 2,160 controlled program transformations, and 4,120,200 executed program-probe pairs. Of 1,960 intended faulty transformations, 1,779 alter a contract or output somewhere in the finite audit domain; 200 additional controls preserve behavior. A 32-probe hybrid learned on generations 1-3 covers 576/582 (99.0%) dynamically killable faults in generations 4-5 using 1.2-2.0% of the exhaustive domain. With an entire fault family withheld from training, diversity raises scenario-family macro coverage from 84.6% to 94.9%. In a downstream deployment study, a conservative admission rule reduces severe tail regressions from 15/20 program-environment groups to 0/20. FaultLens provides a prioritized evidence mechanism, not a proof of correctness, and makes its budget, evidence source, generalization split, and misses explicit.

</details>


### [294] [Beyond Execution: Auditing Experimental Fidelity in LLM-Driven Scientific Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26753)
*Lezhi Yu,Xiaogang Xu,Yuhua Zhou,Shuibing He,Aimin Pan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper introduces ABE-Ralph, an auditing framework to detect "methodological hallucinations" in LLM agents used for scientific experimentation - where agents silently reduce resources, replace failed components, or draw conclusions from underpowered settings. The framework achieves 93% execution rate across 30 reproduction runs and matches/exceeds SOTA on 5/23 NatureBench tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents for scientific experimentation often produce code that appears to work but fails to faithfully implement the reference method or test the paper's claims. Current evaluation focuses on code execution or plausible metrics rather than whether experiments actually support the claimed findings.

Method: ABE-Ralph framework with three components: (1) Structured representation of claims, protocols, required components, baselines, and metrics as experimental constraints; (2) 8-step workflow guiding implementation; (3) Quantitative, qualitative, and code-level verification.

Result: 93% robust execution rate across 30 long-horizon reproduction runs covering 12 ML domains; identifies five scientific failure modes. On 23 NatureBench discovery tasks, matches/exceeds SOTA on 5 tasks.

Conclusion: Reliable evaluation of AI scientists must assess whether experimental design faithfully tests intended claims and whether evidence supports them, rather than treating code execution or plausible metrics as scientific success.

Abstract: LLM agents used for scientific experimentation must do more than generate executable code: they must implement the reference method faithfully, design experiments that test the paper's claims, and provide evidence supporting those claims. We show that agents often produce methodological hallucinations: silently reducing datasets or training budgets, replacing failed learning or generative components with lookup or oracle functions, or drawing conclusions from resource-limited settings where a method's claimed advantage disappears. To detect these failures, we introduce ABE-Ralph, a reference-anchored auditing framework that represents claims, protocols, required components, baselines, and metrics as structured experimental constraints, guides implementation through an 8-step workflow, and performs quantitative, qualitative, and code-level verification. Across 30 long-horizon reproduction runs covering 12 machine learning domains, ABE-Ralph achieves a 93% robust execution rate and identifies five scientific failure modes. In 23 NatureBench discovery tasks, ABE-Ralph matches or exceeds state-of-the-art performance on 5 tasks. These results show that reliable evaluation of AI scientists must assess whether the experimental design faithfully tests the intended claim and whether the resulting evidence supports it, rather than treating code execution or plausible metrics as evidence of scientific success.

</details>


### [295] [An Empirical Evaluation of Using Large Language Models for Automated Model-Based Test Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27094)
*Hafize Sanli,Onur Kilincceker,Cihat Cetinkaya*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper evaluates five state-of-the-art LLMs (GPT-5.1, GPT-5.2, Claude Opus 4.5, Claude Sonnet 4.5, Gemini 2.5 Pro) for automated model-based test generation, comparing them against GraphWalker tool with random and quick random algorithms across four models of varying complexity (Parabank, Testinium, TLC, RISC-V). The study aims to address scalability challenges in industrial MBT adoption.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model-based testing (MBT) faces broad scalability challenges for industrial adoption. This paper seeks to explore whether Large Language Models can address this limitation by providing automated test generation capabilities.

Method: Empirical evaluation comparing five LLMs against GraphWalker (a state-of-the-art MBT tool) using its built-in algorithms (random and quick random) for edge and vertex coverage settings. Tests were conducted on four GraphWalker models of escalating complexity: two web applications (Parabank, Testinium) and two hardware applications (TLC, RISC-V).

Result: The evaluation indicates that LLMs show strong potential to optimize and shorten test paths and step sizes compared to GraphWalker's algorithms across the four test models.

Conclusion: LLMs demonstrate strong potential for optimizing MBT by producing shorter, more efficient test paths, suggesting they could be a viable solution for addressing scalability challenges in industrial MBT adoption.

Abstract: Large language models have shown strong potential for software engineering tasks, particularly software testing. Model-based testing (MBT) is a software testing technique. To address the broad scalability challenge for industrial adoption of MBTs, our paper presents an empirical evaluation of Large Language Models (LLMs) for automated model-based test generation, compared with a state-of-the-art model-based testing tool (GraphWalker) and its built-in algorithms (random and quick random for edge and vertex coverage settings). Our evaluation indicates strong potential to optimize and shorten test paths and step sizes using the recent five state-of-the-art LLMs (GPT-5.1, GPT-5.2, Claude Opus 4.5, Claude Sonnet 4.5, and Gemini 2.5 Pro) against four GraphWalker models (two web applications (Parabank and Testinium) and two hardware applications (TLC and RISC-V) ) of escalating complexity.

</details>


### [296] [Mutation Testing for Reproducibility Safeguards in Machine Learning Research Software: An Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27100)
*Ilya Shulepov*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents an empirical study using MLReproMutate to test whether existing validation workflows in ML research repositories can detect controlled mutations to reproducibility-relevant factors (random seeds, dependency pins, data splits, cross-validation folds). Of 23 non-equivalent mutations tested, only 2 were detected (8.7%), suggesting existing validation workflows often fail to catch changes to important experimental choices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reproducibility in ML research depends on experimental choices like random seeds, dependency versions, data partitioning, and evaluation configuration. Existing repository validation workflows may execute successfully without detecting changes to such choices, potentially compromising research reproducibility.

Method: The researchers conducted an outcome-blind empirical study of 39 frozen repository-operator cases using four mutation classes: random seed, dependency pin, data split, and cross-validation fold count. They applied MLReproMutate to inject controlled reproducibility-relevant mutations and evaluated them against validation workflows already present in the repositories. Primary execution yielded outcomes for 13 cases; a bounded restoration procedure increased this to 24. After excluding one confirmed-equivalent mutation, 23 confirmed non-equivalent mutations remained for analysis.

Result: The selected validation workflows detected only 2 of 23 non-equivalent mutations, corresponding to an observed detection proportion of 8.7%. This indicates that existing validation workflows in ML research repositories largely fail to detect changes to reproducibility-relevant experimental choices.

Conclusion: The findings do not imply that the studied repositories are irreproducible. Rather, they demonstrate that existing validation workflows often do not detect the particular controlled reproducibility-relevant changes introduced by the study. This motivates reproducibility-oriented mutation testing as a complementary approach to assess whether research-software safeguards adequately constrain experimentally important choices.

Abstract: Reproducibility in machine-learning research depends on experimental choices such as random seeds, dependency versions, data partitioning, and evaluation configuration. Existing repository validation workflows may execute successfully without detecting changes to such choices. We study this problem using MLReproMutate, research software that applies controlled, reproducibility-relevant mutations to ML research repositories and evaluates them against validation workflows already present in those repositories.
  We conducted an outcome-blind empirical study of 39 frozen repository-operator cases using four mutation classes: random seed, dependency pin, data split, and cross-validation fold count. Repository revisions, mutation candidates, and validation workflows were fixed before mutation outcomes were observed. Primary execution yielded outcomes for 13 of 39 cases; a bounded restoration procedure increased the combined evaluable set to 24. After excluding one confirmed-equivalent mutation, 23 confirmed non-equivalent mutations remained. The selected validation workflows detected 2 of these 23 mutations, corresponding to an observed detection proportion of 8.7%.
  These results do not imply that the corresponding repositories are irreproducible. Rather, they show that, in this sample, existing validation workflows often did not detect the particular controlled reproducibility-relevant changes introduced by the study. The findings motivate reproducibility-oriented mutation testing as a complementary way to assess whether research-software safeguards constrain experimentally important choices.

</details>


### [297] [AROMA+: A Study of Factors Affecting Reproducible Builds in the Maven Ecosystem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27125)
*Mehdi Keshani,Amirhossein Rahmati,Mohammad Hossein Aref,Abbas Heydarnoori*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents AROMA+, a tool to automatically find source code and recover release environment information from Maven artifacts to enable reproducible builds in the Java ecosystem. The approach achieves up to 99.8% accuracy compared to manual methods, finding that 32% of Maven Central packages can be automatically checked for reproducibility, with 12% being fully reproducible.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Maven ecosystem is the backbone of the Java software supply chain, but its reproducibility remains understudied compared to initiatives like Debian's reproducibility community. Existing manual efforts (Reproducible Central) are limited and challenging to maintain. The research aims to automate the process of finding source code and recovering release environment information to support reproducibility verification.

Method: The researchers developed AROMA+, which uses several heuristics to obtain source code and original release environment information from Maven artifacts and source repositories. They then attempted to reproduce packages on Maven Central using this information and compared results field-by-field to the existing manual approach.

Result: AROMA+ achieves up to 99.8% accuracy compared to manual approaches. The tool revealed flaws in manually maintained lists (e.g., broken repository links). 32% of Maven Central packages are amenable to automatic reproducibility checking, and 12% of these packages are fully reproducible. The researchers successfully reproduced new packages and contributed some to the Reproducible Central repository.

Conclusion: Automatic reproducibility is feasible for a significant portion of the Maven ecosystem. The study provides actionable insights, outlines future work, and makes datasets and tools publicly available. This work supports and advances the Reproducible Central initiative through automation.

Abstract: Modern software engineering establishes software supply chains and relies on tools and libraries to improve productivity. However, reusing external software in a project presents a security risk when the source of the component is unknown or the consistency of a component cannot be verified. Reproducible builds present a mitigation strategy, as they can confirm the origin and consistency of reused components. A large reproducibility community has formed for Debian, but the reproducibility of the Maven ecosystem, the backbone of the Java supply chain, remains understudied in comparison. Reproducible Central is an initiative that curates a list of reproducible Maven libraries, but the list is limited and challenging to maintain due to manual efforts. Our research aims to support these efforts in the Maven ecosystem through automation. We investigate the feasibility of automatically finding the source code of a library from its Maven release and recovering information about the original release environment. Our tool, AROMA+, can obtain this critical information from the artifact and the source repository through several heuristics and we use the results for reproduction attempts of packages on Maven Central. Overall, our approach achieves an accuracy of up to 99.8% when compared field-by-field to the existing manual approach. In some instances, we even detected flaws in the manually maintained list, such as broken repository links. We reveal that automatic reproducibility is feasible for 32% of the packages on Maven Central using AROMA+, and 12% of these packages are fully reproducible. We demonstrate our ability to successfully reproduce new packages and have contributed some of them to the Reproducible Central repository. Additionally, we highlight actionable insights, outline future work in this area, and make our dataset and tools publicly available.

</details>


### [298] [AgentDV: Closed-Loop Agentic AI for Hardware Design Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27148)
*Navya Goli,Junzhe Liu,Zhenge Jia,Umamaheswara Rao Tida*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: AgentDV is a closed-loop agentic AI framework that automates RTL verification environment generation by transforming single-shot LLM testbench generation into a tool-grounded verification pipeline. It addresses the critical problem that LLM-generated testbenches often fail to be runnable, design-consistent, and coverage-producing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RTL verification consumes a major portion of modern SoC development effort, yet recent LLM-based verification-code generation often fails to produce runnable, design-consistent, and coverage-producing testbenches. This creates a need for an automated framework that can reliably generate valid verification environments.

Method: AgentDV combines LLM-guided analysis, testbench construction, simulation, coverage measurement, and iterative refinement. The framework introduces three key innovations: 1) Runnability filtering to reject invalid generated environments, 2) CSR-grounded checking to reduce hallucinated signals and incorrect expected behavior, and 3) Coverage-guided iteration to regenerate tests based on measured verification gaps.

Result: Direct single-shot prompting fails to produce valid coverage-producing environments. AgentDV achieves 100% pass rate on four DUTs and 80.9% average pass rate using Claude Sonnet 4.6. Llama and Qwen achieve 58.7% and 60.6% average pass rates respectively. Coverage results: Claude achieves 74.5% line coverage and 88.4% branch coverage on average; Llama achieves 69.1% line and 82.3% branch; Qwen achieves 64.9% line and 76.7% branch.

Conclusion: AgentDV successfully transforms LLM-based testbench generation into a practical verification tool. The closed-loop approach with runnability filtering, CSR-grounded checking, and coverage-guided iteration significantly improves pass rates compared to single-shot prompting. Claude Sonnet 4.6 outperforms Llama and Qwen models in both pass rate and coverage metrics.

Abstract: Register-transfer level (RTL) verification consumes a major part of modern system-on-chip (SoC) development effort. Yet, recent LLM-based verification-code generation often fails to produce runnable, design-consistent, and coverage-producing testbenches. We present AgentDV, a closed-loop agentic AI framework for automated RTL verification environment generation. AgentDV transforms single-shot LLM testbench generation into a tool-grounded verification pipeline by combining LLM-guided analysis, testbench construction, simulation, coverage measurement, and iterative refinement. The framework introduces three key ideas: 1) runnability filtering to reject invalid generated environments, 2) CSR-grounded checking to reduce hallucinated signals and incorrect expected behavior, and 3) coverage-guided iteration to regenerate tests based on measured verification gaps. We evaluate AgentDV using three LLMs on challenge DUTs and public OpenTitan peripheral and security IP blocks. From our analysis, we observed that direct single-shot prompting fails to produce a valid coverage-producing environment on benchmarks. AgentDV achieves 100% pass rate on four DUTs and an average of 80.9% pass rate on all DUTs using Claude Sonnet 4.6. Similarly, an average of 58.7% and 60.6% pass rate is achieved for Llama and Qwen models, respectively. In addition, an average of 74.5%, 69.1%, and 64.9% of line coverage and 88.4%, 82.3%, and 76.7% of branch coverage for the benchmarks under consideration for Claude Sonnet 4.6, Llama, and Qwen models, respectively.

</details>


### [299] [A Trans-Domain Digital Twin for Bio-Aware Control of Climate and Energy in Cattle Fattening Barns Using Single-Episode Optimizer Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27185)
*Mansoorali Amiri*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: In closed cattle-fattening barns, the indoor climate and herd growth are mutually interdependent. Temperature, relative humidity, airflow, and ventilation affect thermal comfort, feed intake, metabolic heat production, daily growth, feed efficiency, and energy consumption, while body-weight gain alters the future heat and moisture loads of the barn and, consequently, its ventilation, heating, and energy requirements. This article proposes a trans-domain digital twin framework with single-episode learning capability, customized for bio-aware climate and energy control in a closed cattle-fattening barn. The framework integrates a mechanistic climate simulator, a livestock growth simulator, model predictive control, lightweight reinforcement learning, and structured knowledge memory within a multi-rate temporal-loop architecture. The fast temporal loop operates every five minutes to evaluate actuator decisions and maintain short-term thermal comfort, safety, and energy efficiency, whereas the slow temporal loop provides biological guidance based on daily climatic conditions, feed efficiency, heat production, and growth-limiting factors. The results show that climate, growth, energy, feed, biological guidance, and memory can be linked within a single executable control cycle. Remaining limitations include the need for field validation, improved management of feed pressure, and reduction of abrupt actuator-command variations.

</details>


### [300] [Twelve Quick Tips for Managing IT Disasters in Small Research Software Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27196)
*Greg Wilson*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A practical guide for small research software teams on disaster planning and recovery, motivated by recent events (2025 US government attacks on research, GitHub outages, wildfires) that exposed vulnerabilities in research computing. Designed for non-experts while encouraging collaboration with institutional support staff.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent unprecedented events in 2025—including US government attacks on scientific research groups, GitHub's first availability drop below 90%, and wildfires displacing researchers—highlighted the fragility of research computing systems and the urgent need for disaster preparedness.

Method: Provides practical tips and guidance for disaster planning and recovery, assuming readers handle these tasks alongside regular duties without system administration expertise. Encourages leveraging institutional resources (research computing groups, data librarians, environmental health-and-safety offices).

Result: The paper establishes what "done" looks like for disaster planning, enabling research teams to better prepare for and recover from disruptions to their computing infrastructure.

Conclusion: Planning for disasters is one of the most effective ways to prevent them. Small research teams should focus on understanding the end goal while collaborating with institutional experts who can provide implementation support.

Abstract: In 2025, the US government launched an unprecedented series of attacks on its own scientific research groups. A year later GitHub dropped below 90% availability for the first time, while wildfires in Canada, France, Spain, and elsewhere forced researchers from the homes and labs. These events and others have reminded us just how fragile research computing systems can be, and that planning for disasters is one of the most effective ways to prevent them.
  This paper is a short guide to disaster planning and recovery for a small research software team. The tips assume you are doing everything yourself on top of your regular job, and that you aren't an experienced system administrator. Some of the tips do require that kind of expertise, but most research institutions have research computing groups, data librarians, and environmental health-and-safety offices whose entire job is to help with exactly these problems. This paper tells you what "done" looks like; they can often provide it.

</details>


### [301] [Persona-Execution Separation: An Architecture Pattern for Evolving LLM Agents under Execution Audit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27427)
*Yisen Xi*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper introduces Persona-Execution Separation (PES), an architectural pattern for LLM agents in governed organizations that separates persona (free-form, drifting instructions) from execution (audited, traceable work) into different trust domains connected by a governed contract bridge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Organizations need LLM agents to allow persona evolution (instructions, tone, self-presentation) while maintaining execution traceability and auditability. A single trust domain cannot satisfy both requirements cost-effectively.

Method: PES architecture separates persona and execution into different trust domains with a governed contract bridge. Key mechanisms include: graded data-loss-prevention (DLP), approval matrix, continuous identity, and audit enforcement. The approach was validated through a development/pilot case in a regulated digital-employee platform over one month, testing five model configurations and comparing against a pre-separation build.

Result: The mechanism check found no execution-side re-validation under persona perturbation across five model configurations, and no persona fingerprint on hard-asserted fields. The pre-separation build showed execution was decoupled from persona by omission rather than by construction, making PES an audited architectural rule rather than accidental isolation.

Conclusion: PES provides a principled architecture for LLM agents when multi-user deployment, execution audit, and persona churn requirements jointly apply. The pattern ensures free persona drift while maintaining execution traceability through architectural separation rather than accidental coupling.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents in governed organizations must let the persona (instructions, tone, self-presentation) evolve freely, while keeping execution (stateful, audited work) traceable. A single trust domain does not satisfy both cheaply. We present Persona-Execution Separation (PES): persona and execution reside in different trust domains, connected by a governed contract bridge. The persona is singly-homed and may drift; execution is faceless and audited. Status summaries may return; data bodies remain in the restrictive domain except a graded data-loss-prevention (DLP) exception; identity stays continuous. An approval matrix, DLP, and audit enforce the crossing. PES follows from three goals---free drift, execution traceability, and decoupling. Under LLM representational indistinguishability, any single-domain mechanism that meets all three must re-introduce typed change objects, an external gate, and a stable audit anchor: PES rebuilt at higher coupling cost. A development/pilot case in a regulated digital-employee platform records five decisions over one month, each with a rejected alternative. A mechanism check on the shipped implementation found no execution-side re-validation under persona perturbation (five model configurations) and no persona fingerprint on hard-asserted fields. A probe of a recovered pre-separation build found the governed execution path decoupled from the persona by omission, not by construction; a later wiring change could reverse that isolation, which PES makes an audited architectural rule. The pattern applies when multi-user deployment, execution audit, and expected persona churn hold jointly.

</details>


### [302] [From Static to Dynamic: Benchmarking Real-World Code Review with MCR-Bench](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27442)
*Dewu Zheng,Yanlin Wang,Xiwen Wang,Kefeng Duan,Hongyu Zhang,Xilin Liu,Yuchi Ma,Zibin Zheng*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: MCR-Bench is the first defect state-aware benchmark for realistic multi-round code review, containing 2,269 real-world code review tasks across 5 programming languages with fine-grained defect metadata and dynamic state annotations. Experiments reveal mainstream LLMs have limited overall capability in defect detection and tracking, with performance degrading significantly across rounds, and struggle with semantically complex or low-salience defects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based code review approaches oversimplify the process into single-round, static decision tasks, failing to capture the multi-round interactive nature and complex problem-solving processes of real-world code review. This gap necessitates a realistic benchmark for evaluating LLMs on multi-round code review.

Method: MCR-Bench was constructed with 2,269 real-world multi-round code review tasks across 5 programming languages, annotated with fine-grained defect information (description, type, severity) and cross-round state labels. Extensive experiments were conducted on mainstream LLMs to evaluate their performance.

Result: Three key findings: (1) Limited overall capability - LLMs show limited performance in defect detection and lifecycle state tracking, degrading significantly with more interaction rounds; (2) Defect-sensitive performance - LLMs vary across defect types/severity, missing semantically complex or low-salience defects more often; (3) Underlying failure mechanisms - false positives and false negatives stem from cross-round temporal misalignment and inadequate long-range memory.

Conclusion: MCR-Bench reveals critical weaknesses in current LLMs for multi-round code review. Future work should focus on improving LLMs' temporal reasoning across review rounds and enhancing their memory capabilities for complex defect tracking.

Abstract: In real-world software development, code review typically involves iterative interactions between developers and reviewers to improve software quality, making the process costly and time-consuming. Although recent work explores large language models (LLMs) for automated code review, most approaches oversimplify code review into a single-round, static decision task, which fails to capture the multi-round interactive nature and the complex problem-solving processes inherent in realistic review scenarios. To bridge this gap, we introduce MCR-Bench, the first defect state-aware benchmark designed for realistic multi-round code review. MCR-Bench covers five commonly-used programming languages and consists of 2,269 real-world multi-round code review tasks, each of which is annotated with fine-grained defect information and cross-round state labels. Each task in MCR-Bench is equipped with fine-grained defect metadata (e.g., description, type, severity) alongside dynamic state annotations, capturing the complete evolutionary trajectory of a defect throughout the multi-round process. We obtain several findings through extensive experiments on MCR-Bench with mainstream LLMs. (1) Limited overall capability: experiments reveal that mainstream LLMs exhibit limited overall performance in defect detection and defect lifecycle state tracking, with performance degrading significantly as the number of interaction rounds increases; (2) Defect-sensitive performance: LLMs' performance varies substantially across different defect types and severity levels, with semantically complex or low-salience defects being significantly more likely to be missed; (3) Underlying Failure Mechanisms: our in-depth error analysis dissects the distinct drivers of false positives and false negatives, revealing critical weaknesses such as cross-round temporal misalignment and inadequate long-range memory.

</details>


### [303] [SWE-Prime: Fewer Trajectories, Better Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27449)
*Dewu Zheng,Ruizhe Ye,Yanlin Wang,Yang Ye,Hongyu Zhang,Ensheng Shi,Xilin Liu,Yuchi Ma,Jianxing Yu,Zibin Zheng*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: To improve large language models' ability to resolve real-world software issues, prior work has focused on constructing large-scale agent trajectory datasets and performing supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on successful trajectories. However, task success does not guarantee high-quality supervision: successful trajectories may still contain ineffective, redundant, or risky steps. Directly using such trajectories for SFT can introduce noisy supervision and encourage models to imitate undesirable problem-solving behaviors. Therefore, we propose SWE-Prime, a multi-granularity, two-stage SFT data selection method that progressively filters training data at the trajectory and segment levels. Specifically, the first stage performs trajectory-level screening based on process quality, result quality, and data representativeness, selecting a high-quality and representative subset of successful trajectories. The second stage performs segment-level selection by grouping consecutive steps into semantic segments and assessing each segment based on its contribution to the final solution, learnability, and potential risks. During SFT, all segments remain in the sequence to preserve context, while only selected segments contribute to the loss computation. Experiments on SWE-Bench Pro and SWE-Bench Verified show that training on the 10% trajectory subset selected by SWE-Prime outperforms training on the full resolved dataset, yielding relative performance gains of up to 12.2% and 24.2%, respectively.

</details>
